The second man's argument is loud because it is empty, and that is the picture the verse draws of a dispute with no knowledge behind it. The correction is not a louder answer but a plain one, and the plain answer is stated in the next verse by the One who knows.


---


## Verse 3:67

> Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist

**THE CLAIM THE VERSE REFUSES**

The answer to the argument comes in a single sentence that begins with a denial: ***“Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian”***. This sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as God's rejection of the claim the two communities had put forward, and al-Qurṭubī notes that he is cleared of the claims that were false. The order of the denial is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, first the religion of the Jews and then the religion of the Christians, since each group had made him one of its own.

The refusal is not an insult to the two communities but a statement about the man and his date. Since the Torah and the Gospel came long after him, the names the arguers used could not have belonged to his lifetime, and the verse states what their own chronology requires. Al-Jalālayn draws the line directly and notes that he was dissociated from their claims, and that the title of Jew and the title of Christian came into use after the two books were revealed.

**THE TWO WORDS THAT REPLACE THE TITLE**

Instead of the names the two sides wanted, the verse gives two words of its own: ***“he submitted in all uprightness”***. The first of the two is explained by al-Baghawī as the one who turns away from all the religions toward the upright way, and al-Qurṭubī adds that such a man professes God's oneness, performs the pilgrimage, offers the sacrifice, is circumcised and faces the prayer direction. Two senses are kept by al-Ālūsī for the second word, since it may mean one who submits to the truth or one who declares God's oneness.

The two are put together by al-Jalālayn, which shows a man who inclined away from every other religion toward the upright one. The pair of words is the exact opposite of the pair the arguers used, since a title says where a man was born into a community and these words say what he did with his life. What follows for the reader is a test of the labels that were at issue, because the verse measures the man by his surrender rather than by the company that claimed him.

**THE LINE THAT EXCLUDES THE THIRD PARTY**

The sentence closes with ***“and was not a polytheist”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads it as the statement that Ibrāhīm did not follow the religions of those who associated partners with God, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the description of his way is complete on both sides, since the sentence gives what he was and what he was not. The third clause matters because the argument in the sūrah is older than either of the two books, and the verse guards the subject of the argument from every side.

Think of a house that two families claim as an ancestor's home, and a deed that shows the ancestor lived two centuries earlier and owned land elsewhere. The deed does not insult the families. It simply removes the house from the case, and the verse removes the two titles from Ibrāhīm in the same way. What remains is the way itself, which the next verse describes in terms of those who walked it.


---


## Verse 3:68

> Indeed, those who have the best claim to Abraham are his followers, this Prophet, and the believers. And Allah is the Guardian of those who believe

**THE CLAIM PUT IN ORDER**

The dispute over who owned Ibrāhīm is then answered by ranking the claimants: ***“Indeed, those who have the best claim to Abraham”*** and ***“are his followers, this Prophet”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that the nearest of people to him are those who followed him, then this Prophet, then the believers, and Ibn ʿAbbās reports the words of the Jewish leaders who had told the Prophet that they were nearer to the religion of Ibrāhīm than he was.

The verse gives the answer to that claim in a form the claimants themselves could weigh, since the nearest of people to a man is the one who follows his way and not the one who invents a title for him. Al-Saʿdī expands the point and notes that the followers of the friend of God before Muḥammad are included with the believers, so the sentence gathers the whole line of those who walked the way. Since the way is what makes the claim, the ranking is a definition rather than a preference.

**THE CLAUSE THAT INCLUDES THE COMMUNITY**

The Prophet is named in the middle of the list, and the believers are named with him: ***“and the believers.”***. The plain sense is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, namely that the followers of Ibrāhīm are those who held his way, and al-Jalālayn notes that those who followed him in his lifetime are nearest to him. The same reading is kept by al-Ālūsī, who adds that those who follow his religion in every later age are included as well.

The order shows the reader how the community is joined to the man, since the mention of the Prophet between the early followers and the believers ties the two ends of the line together. The declaration of this verse is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the closest of people to Ibrāhīm were those who followed him during his life and those who follow his way after him. The verse therefore answers a dispute about ancestry with a sentence about practice, and the claim to a father is settled by whether his way is walked.

**THE GUARDIANSHIP THAT CLOSES THE VERSE**

The verse closes with ***“And Allah is the Guardian of those who believe”***. The sense that God is the helper of the believers and the one who takes charge of their affair is kept by al-Baghawī, and al-Saʿdī notes that the promise belongs to the people who were just named. The sentence is placed after the ranking of the claimants, and it tells the reader where the protection of the community rests, since the guardian named here is not a tribe or a book but God.

Think of a guild whose members argue about which of them stands closest to the founder, until an old ledger is opened that lists the founder's apprentices and their students. The ledger settles the argument by naming the line of work rather than the line of blood, and the verse settles the same kind of argument in the same way. What follows is a turn to the people who tried to break that line and to the response their effort received.


---


## Verse 3:69

> Some of the People of the Book wish to mislead you ˹believers˺. They mislead none but themselves, yet they fail to perceive it

**THE WISH THAT TARGETED THE BELIEVERS**

The sūrah turns from the argument about Ibrāhīm to the intention behind it: ***“Some of the People of the Book wish to mislead you ˹believers˺.”***. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Baghawī both connect the verse with the report that the Jews of Banū al-Naḍīr, Qurayẓah and Qaynuqāʿ invited Muʿādh b. Jabal, Ḥudhayfa b. al-Yamān and ʿAmmār b. Yāsir to their religion, and Ibn ʿAbbās records the invitation of Kaʿb al-Ashraf to the same companions after the Battle of Uhud.

The well-known report is kept by al-Ālūsī, who notes that the People of the Book here are the Jews and adds the second reading, which takes the phrase to cover both communities. The wording concerns the wish rather than the act, and the difference matters, since the harm is aimed at the faith of the believers and not at their persons. The same sense for the opening is kept by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the desire belongs to a group within the community and not to all of it.

**THE TURN THE SENTENCE MAKES**

The same sentence completes itself with a turn: ***“They mislead none”*** and ***“but themselves,”*** and ***“yet they fail to perceive it”***. Al-Jalālayn explains that the sin of leading others astray falls upon the leaders themselves, while the believers do not follow them in the matter, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain reading that they turn no one away from the religion of God except themselves.

The point is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the victim of the effort to misguide is no one other than the one who makes it, and the sentence calls the people blind to that outcome. Since the aim was to weaken a community, the result is a loss measured in the ledger of the plotters alone, and the verse states the exchange before the reader can miss it. Al-Saʿdī treats the whole passage as a favour to the community, because it is told in advance what its enemies are planning.

**WHAT THE VERSE TELLS THE COMMUNITY**

The sentence is a warning with a floor under it, and the floor is the part that the plotters cannot see. The community is told both the intention and the outcome in a single breath, so that no believer reads the effort as a sign of strength, and the failure is named before the response is required. The verse gives the reader a settled confidence that does not depend on the size of the group that is plotting.

Think of a man who digs a pit beside a road for a traveler he dislikes, and who then walks that road himself in the dark. The pit is his own, and the verse makes the same point about a plan built on misleading others. The next verse turns the address to the people who made the plan, and it asks them what their own books were saying while they did it.


---


## Verse 3:70

> O People of the Book! Why do you reject the signs of Allah while you bear witness ˹to their truth˺

**THE QUESTION PUT TO THE READERS OF THE BOOKS**

The address returns to the community whose books had prepared them for the message: ***“O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Why do you reject the signs of Allah”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the question as the rebuke of those who reject what is in God's book, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading of Qatādah and al-Suddī, which takes the signs to be the proofs in their own scriptures.

The two sides are drawn together by al-Ālūsī, who shows the signs as what is recited to them from the Qurʾān together with what the Torah and the Gospel indicate about its truth. The question presses the point that the rejection is happening in the presence of evidence, since a person who denies a case he has not examined is not in the same position as one who has seen the record. The verse therefore charges them with a refusal that their own learning had made impossible.

**THE WITNESS NAMED IN THE SAME LINE**

The question is closed with the clause that takes away any excuse: ***“while you bear witness ˹to their truth˺”***. The witness is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as their knowledge from their own scripture that Muḥammad is a prophet sent by God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense, since the descriptions are found in the books they carried. The sentence puts the knowledge and the denial in one line, and the contradiction is left for the reader to see.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the reason the reproach is sharp here, which is that the mention of rejection after knowledge increases its force, and the same work adds that unbelief is forbidden in every condition. Since the question is addressed to people who knew, the answer cannot be ignorance, and the refusal is left standing on its own. The question also keeps the door open, because the fault is named rather than the person, and a fault that is named can still be abandoned. What follows is a second question about the method by which the knowledge was being handled.

**THE SHAPE OF THE REPROACH**

The verse makes its point with a question rather than a statement, and the shape is deliberate, because a question invites the person addressed to answer for himself. Al-Baghawī keeps the address to the two communities, and al-Saʿdī places the passage among the signs that show the community what its opponents were doing with the truth they had. The reader of the sūrah is being taught how to read a refusal, and the lesson is that the refusal is answered by evidence the refuser already holds. A community that is asked why it is doing something is being given a chance to stop, and the chance comes before any sentence of judgment is passed. The person who turns away from a proof he has read carries the proof with him, and the position is described with two questions rather than with an order.

Think of a clerk who keeps the original of a contract in his own safe and then tells the court that no contract exists. The safe, not the argument, decides the case, and the verse puts the same kind of document before the people it addresses. The next verse goes further and describes what is done with the truth once it is in hand.


---


## Verse 3:71

> O People of the Book! Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly

**THE MIXING THAT BLURS THE RECORD**

The address continues with a question about method: ***“O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Why do you mix the truth with falsehood”***. The first verb is explained plainly by al-Ṭabarī as the act of mixing, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same sense, since the word for the act is the common word for blending one thing into another. The two ways of reading the sentence are given by al-Ālūsī, either as covering the truth with what is false or as mixing the two together.

The reports name the subject that was being mixed. The passage is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the mixing of the traits of the false claimant with the traits of Muḥammad, since a reader who finds the two sets of marks side by side cannot tell which belongs to whom. Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the mixing was done by distorting and falsifying scripture, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the same picture of the true statement blended with statements that were invented. The act is therefore not a denial of the record but a blurring of it.

**THE SILENCE THAT KEEPS THE TRUTH BACK**

The second half of the question names the quieter half of the same work: ***“and hide the truth knowingly”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the hidden truth as the description and traits of Muḥammad which are found in their scripture, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the descriptions of the Prophet. The same understanding is listed by al-Ālūsī among the views of the scholars, and he notes that the concealment is the second crime after the mixing, since the two together leave a reader with no way to the truth.

The adverb at the end is the one that closes the door: the concealment is done with knowledge, so ignorance cannot be offered as the reason. The reproach is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the question is put to people who were handling a text they understood. Since the hiding is described as deliberate, the verse is not asking about a mistake but about a method, and the method is what the community is being warned against in its own teaching.

**WHY THE QUESTION IS STILL ASKED**

The verse asks why rather than simply condemning, and the shape leaves the person addressed a place to turn. The connection between this verse and the one before it is kept by al-Qurṭubī, since the two questions belong to a single reproach, and al-Baghawī keeps the address to the two communities whose books stood before them. The question is therefore for the reader of the Book in every age, since the mixing of a true word with a false one is a temptation that does not need a library to attempt.

Think of a medicine seller who adds chalk to a remedy that works, and then keeps the price of the remedy. The buyer is harmed twice, once by the chalk and once by the name on the label, and the verse reports the same double injury to the truth. What follows is the plan that the mixing was meant to serve, and the plan is described in the words the plotters used among themselves.


---


## Verse 3:72

> A group among the People of the Book said ˹to one another˺, “Believe in what has been revealed to the believers in the morning and reject it in the evening, so they may abandon their faith

**THE PLAN SPOKEN IN PRIVATE**

The sūrah now reports a conversation that was never meant to be heard: ***“A group among the People of the Book said ˹to one another˺,”*** and the report continues into the words they used. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as God's account of what the group said to their brothers, and al-Qurṭubī names Kaʿb b. al-Ashraf and Mālik b. al-Ṣayf among those who spoke, addressing the humbler people of their own community.

The word for the group is explained by al-Ālūsī, who notes that the speech was addressed to some of their own number rather than to the believers, and the shape of the scheme is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since a plan spoken in private is already an admission that it cannot be defended in the open. Ibn ʿAbbās records Kaʿb and the leaders of his party as the speakers. The verse therefore exposes the method by which the community of the Book intended to reach the believers.

**THE DAY AND THE DOUBT IT WAS MEANT TO PLANT**

The words of the plan follow: ***“Believe in what has been revealed to the believers in the morning”*** and ***“and reject it in the evening”***. The instruction, al-Ālūsī notes, was to show the faith outwardly and not to hold it, since the aim was the appearance of belief and not belief itself. The reasoning behind the plan is set out by al-Jalālayn, which was that the believers would say the group was learned and would not have turned back unless it had found the message false.

The two halves of the day are the instrument of the scheme, and the plan relies on the respect the community had for learning. The calculation is set out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān in the same terms, and the rule behind it was sincerity with their own people and expediency with the believers. Since a quick reversal after a public acceptance puts a doubt where there had been none, the sentence shows a mind that had studied the faith of its neighbours rather than its own books.

**THE PURPOSE STATED AT THE END**

The plan names its own goal: ***“so they may abandon their faith”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the aim was for the followers of the Prophet to return to the religion and the prayer direction of the speakers, and he records a further report that the words concerned the change of the prayer direction itself. The verse is placed by al-Saʿdī among the warnings that tell the community what its opponents were arranging, and the sūrah reports the plan in advance so that the community can weigh it for what it is.

Think of a group of clerks who agree to praise a firm's accounts all morning and to condemn them in the afternoon, hoping the merchants will assume that the books must be crooked. The clerks never examine the ledger, and the scheme works only on the vanity of the onlookers. The verse answers the scheme in the same place it was made, and the answer begins with a sentence addressed to the Prophet himself.


---


## Verse 3:73

> And only believe those who follow your religion.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Surely, ˹the only˺ true guidance is Allah’s guidance.” ˹They also said,˺ “Do not believe that someone will receive ˹revealed˺ knowledge similar to yours or argue against you before your Lord.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Indeed, all bounty is in the Hands of Allah—He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.”

**THE SECOND HALF OF THE INSTRUCTION**

The reported speech continues with a rule for the group's own conduct: ***“And only believe those who follow your religion.”***. The instruction is read by al-Ṭabarī as the command not to trust anyone except a person of their own religion, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the sentence is a prohibition spoken by the leaders to the humbler people of their community. The same connection with the words that came before is kept by al-Baghawī, so the verse carries one piece of advice after another.

The rule completes the plan that the previous verse began, since the community was to be shown a public turnabout and the group's own people were told to keep their counsel among themselves. The two halves of the scheme are spoken by the same party and aimed at the same community, and the sūrah reports both before answering either. What the reader is shown is a method that depends on secrecy toward one side and confidence toward the other.

**THE ANSWER PLACED IN THE MIDDLE**

Between the two speeches of the group, the verse inserts the answer: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Surely, ˹the only˺ true guidance is Allah’s guidance.””***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that the guidance which is worth the name comes from God, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the point out, since the group had been treating guidance as a possession of their community. The sentence is placed in the middle of their own words, and the placement is the answer, because the guidance they were rationing is not theirs to ration.

The words also cut the claim that learning belongs to one group. Since the guidance is God's, it can be given to a people who were not expected to receive it, and the sentence anticipates the objection that the next speech of the group raises. The passage is kept by al-Saʿdī as part of the account of the favour shown to the community, and the favour includes knowing in advance what was being said behind its back.

**THE OBJECTION AND THE BOUNTY THAT ANSWERS IT**

The group then returns to its own number: ***“Do not believe that someone will receive ˹revealed˺ knowledge similar to yours”*** and ***“or argue against you before your Lord.”***. The words are explained by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān as the fear that another people would receive the like of what the group had received, and that the gift would then be used in argument against them. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense of a community that wanted the door of revelation closed behind itself.

The answer comes in the last sentence: ***“Indeed, all bounty is in the Hands of Allah”*** and ***“He grants it to whoever He wills.”*** and ***“And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.””***. Al-Qurṭubī notes the readings of the early scholars on the mercy named in the passage, and al-Ālūsī gathers the same views, with prophethood in one reading and the Qurʾān and Islam in another. Think of a household that locks its well when strangers come near, forgetting that the spring feeds every well in the valley. The verse leaves the key where it was and names the owner of the water.


---


## Verse 3:74

> He chooses whoever He wills to receive His mercy. And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty

**THE CHOICE NAMED IN ONE SENTENCE**

The answer to the group's fear is put in a single sentence: ***“He chooses whoever He wills to receive His mercy.”***. The verb is explained by al-Ṭabarī as the act of singling out a person for a gift, and the object of the choosing is the mercy named in the same line. Al-Qurṭubī records the readings of the early scholars, with al-Ḥasan and Mujāhid taking the mercy as prophethood and guidance, and Ibn Jurayj taking it as Islam and the Qurʾān.

The same three readings are gathered by al-Ālūsī, with another added that takes the mercy as a heart occupied with the remembrance of God, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the choice as the selection of Muḥammad and his companions for the religion. The variety among the readings belongs to the breadth of the mercy itself, and not to any disagreement among the scholars, since every reading names a gift that God gives where He wills. The sentence therefore answers a fear about a closed door with a statement about an open hand.

**WHY THE SENTENCE WAS NEEDED**

The group had worried that revelation might be given to another people, and the verse answers the worry by naming the giver. Since no community controls the mercy, no community can shut it away, and the sentence removes the assumption on which the scheme of the previous verses was built. The reader is shown that the whole plan rested on a mistake about who owned the thing they were protecting.

The sūrah's larger argument is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, where the people who held the books are answered not by a denial of their books but by a statement about the One who sent them. The same reading of the mercy as prophethood is kept by al-Baghawī, and the verse leaves the community with a settled confidence about the gift it had received. What follows for the reader is a change of subject, since the next verse turns from the gift to the dealings of the people who had been discussing it.

**THE BOUNTY THAT CLOSES THE PASSAGE**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty”***. The title is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that the generosity named here has no measure, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the words that God is of bounty abounding. The title is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the generosity shown to Muḥammad by the gift of prophethood and the religion, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage among the favours that the community is told about.

Think of a gardener who is told that the owner of the orchard may plant a new tree in a neighbour's field, and who then spends a season guarding the gate of his own plot. The gate was never his to keep, and the close of the passage leaves the orchard with its owner. The next verse turns to the way the same people dealt with property entrusted to them, and the change from doctrine to dealing is deliberate.


---


## Verse 3:75

> There are some among the People of the Book who, if entrusted with a stack of gold, will readily return it. Yet there are others who, if entrusted with a single coin, will not repay it unless you constantly demand it. This is because they say, “We are not accountable for ˹exploiting˺ the Gentiles.” And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**THE TWO KINDS OF DEPOSIT**

The sūrah turns from belief to dealings: ***“There are some among the People of the Book”*** and ***“who, if entrusted with a stack of gold, will readily return it.”***. Al-Qurṭubī names ʿAbdallāh b. Salām among the trustworthy, and al-Jalālayn records the report of a man who left a great weight of gold with him and received it back in full. The pair of verses is treated by al-Ālūsī as another account of the faults and the merits of the community, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the sequence, since the passage on belief is now followed by a passage on property.

The second kind is drawn beside the first: ***“Yet there are others”*** and ***“who, if entrusted with a single coin, will not repay it”*** and ***“unless you constantly demand it.”***. Ibn ʿAbbās names Kaʿb b. al-Ashraf and his followers for this side, and al-Jalālayn records the report of the dinar that was denied by the same man. The contrast is exact, since the measure of the deposit shrinks to the smallest coin and the conduct still fails the test.

**THE EXCUSE THAT MADE THE FAILURE POSSIBLE**

The verse then reports the reasoning behind the conduct: ***“This is because they say, “We are not accountable”*** and ***“for ˹exploiting˺ the Gentiles.””***. The excuse turns on the identity of the creditor rather than on the value of the deposit, and it treats a debt owed to one people as no debt at all. The treacherous group, al-Saʿdī notes, dressed its conduct in a false reading, and the verse exposes the reading for what it was, since a rule that exists only in the interest of the ruler is not a rule.

The lesson is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, since the passage praises trustworthiness wherever it is found and refuses to treat it as a possession of one community. The sentence also warns the believers against the same temptation, because a claim about the other party's standing can be used to excuse any dealing. The verse therefore reports the excuse and leaves it standing with no support under it.

**THE LIE TOLD AGAINST GOD**

The sentence closes with ***“And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the claim was a fabrication against God, and Ibn Kathīr notes the account of these dishonest people and the warning given to the believers about them. The adverb returns at the end of this verse as it did in the earlier question, so the excuse was never a misunderstanding, and the last word of the verse is about the knowledge of the speaker.

Think of a servant who tells the household that the rules of honesty apply only to neighbours and not to guests. The servant invents a rule to cover a theft, and the same invention is reported there, since the invention itself is a lie against God. The correction is then stated in the verse that follows, and it answers the excuse with a promise rather than with a fine.


---


## Verse 3:76

> Absolutely! Those who honour their trusts and shun evil—surely Allah loves those who are mindful ˹of Him˺

**THE ANSWER THAT CORRECTS THE EXCUSE**

The verse opens with a single word that stops the excuse of the previous passage: ***“Absolutely! Those who honour their trusts”***. Al-Ālūsī reads the opening as the answer to the claim that no way lay against them in the matter of the outsiders, and al-Baghawī keeps the same sense, since the opening affirms what they had denied. The sentence then turns from the claim to the person who does the opposite, and the conduct that decides the case is placed at the front.

The two clauses of the opening are paired: ***“and shun evil”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the pledge is what a person owes between himself and his Lord or between himself and other people, and the guarding is what keeps him from treachery and from turning on what he promised. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the difference between a pledge reached between two parties and a promise made by one side alone, and notes that the first kind binds both of them. Since the excuse had turned a debt into a question about the debtor's community, the verse turns the matter back into a question about the debtor's conduct.

**THE MEASURE OF THE MINDFUL**

The sentence then names the two things it promises: ***“surely Allah loves those”*** and ***“who are mindful ˹of Him˺”***. Al-Qurṭubī joins the clauses and explains the second as a person who keeps himself from lying and from taking what was forbidden to him, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and adds that the love named here means the reward that follows. The promise is attached to the conduct rather than to the community, and the difference is the whole point of the verse.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the sūrah has returned to the same refutation twice, because the claim was repeated and the answer is repeated with it. Since the first answer was a statement about a deposit and the second is a statement about a pledge, the reader is shown the same rule applied to property and to speech. The verse therefore closes the passage on the trusts by naming the quality that holds them, and that quality is not a possession of any one group.

**WHAT THE VERSE SETTLES**

Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as a warning against being deceived by people of bad faith among the people of the Book, and al-Saʿdī notes that the passage describes two groups among them, one that returns what is left with it and one that does not. The verse answers the second group by naming the first as the one that is loved, so the contrast carries the judgment without a sentence of condemnation. The reader is given a measure of conduct rather than a verdict on a people.

Think of a creditor who is told that his debtor's family has a custom of not paying outsiders, and who then asks whether that custom appears anywhere in the debtor's own law. The verse puts the same question to the reader, and the custom comes out of the account. What follows is a verse that describes the price such a person takes for the pledge he breaks, and the price is named in the next line as a small one.


---


## Verse 3:77

> Indeed, those who trade Allah’s covenant and their oaths for a fleeting gain will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them, nor look at them, nor purify them on the Day of Judgment. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**THE PRICE AND THE THING SOLD**

The sentence describes an exchange: ***“Indeed, those who trade Allah’s covenant”*** and ***“and their oaths for a fleeting gain”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the description of people who sold the covenant of God and their oaths for a small price, and al-Baghawī records that the passage came down about the leaders of the Jews who concealed the covenant that God had taken from them in the Torah. The reading kept by Ibn Kathīr is that the buyers preferred the small things of a short life to the promise they had given.

The two things sold are joined in one phrase, since a covenant with God and an oath sworn in His name belong to the same order. The exchange is explained by al-Saʿdī as the buying of this world with religion, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense of the words. What follows for the reader is a warning about the price as much as about the act, because the sentence measures the loss from the side of the buyer. Since the gain is called fleeting, the trade is already failing at the moment it is made.

**THE PORTION AND THE SILENCE**

The sentence then names what the trade costs: ***“will have no share in the Hereafter.”*** and ***“Allah will neither speak to them”***. The first clause is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the loss of any portion in Paradise, and the second as the loss of kind speech on the Day of Judgment. The same sense is kept by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the silence comes from wrath, since the One who speaks to His servants on that day has no word for these.

The denials then continue: ***“nor look at them”*** and ***“nor purify them on the Day of Judgment.”***. The first is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the withholding of mercy and the second as the withholding of any clearing of their name, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reports that join the passage to a dispute over land and a false oath. The three refusals are arranged in an order of nearness, first speech, then sight, then cleansing, and the order matches the offence, since the tongue swore and the hand withheld.

**THE PAINFUL END**

The verse closes with ***“And they will suffer a painful punishment”***. The report of a quarrel between a Muslim and a Jew over a piece of land is kept by al-Qurṭubī, and the claim there was pressed with an oath that the verse answered. The lesson is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the breach of a covenant made with God is treated as a sale, since the seller parts with the thing he had promised to keep.

Think of a guard who is paid a month's wage to hold a gate shut, and who opens it for a coin because the coin is in his hand now. The verse measures the same folly in the currency of the next life, and the punishment is named rather than the profit. The next passage turns to the people who not only hide the record but read out what it never contained.


---


## Verse 3:78

> There are some among them who distort the Book with their tongues to make you think this ˹distortion˺ is from the Book—but it is not what the Book says. They say, “It is from Allah”—but it is not from Allah. And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**THE TONGUES AND THE BOOK**

The sūrah names a second practice: ***“There are some among them”*** and ***“who distort the Book with their tongues to make you think this ˹distortion˺ is from the Book”***. The twisting of the tongue is explained by al-Qurṭubī as the changing of words from their places, and al-Baghawī names Kaʿb b. al-Ashraf, Mālik b. al-Ṣayf, Ḥuyayy b. Akhṭab and Abū Yāsir as the party he has in view. The alteration, Ibn ʿAbbās records, touched the description of the prophet whom the books had announced.

The method works on the ear of the listener rather than on the page, and the sentence keeps the two apart. The clause is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that the twisting was done so that the hearer would reckon the words as part of the Book, and the aim is named in the same line. What follows for the reader is a warning about the sound of a text as much as about its content, since a false line read in a familiar voice is the hardest kind to catch.

**WHAT THE BOOK DOES NOT SAY**

The verse then corrects that impression: ***“but it is not what the Book says. They say, “It is from Allah””*** and ***“but it is not from Allah.”***. The denial is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās in both halves, first against the book and then against its author, and al-Saʿdī notes that the distortion covers both the wording of a text and the sense carried away from it. The two denials stand next to each other because the first lie needs the second to stand.

The two clauses are drawn together by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the denial is made while the speakers knew the truth of the matter. The sentence is placed by Ibn ʿUthaymīn in the same context as the verses before it, since the people addressed are the People of the Book throughout the passage. The verse therefore does not charge them with an error of reading, and the charge is the invention of a line and the naming of a source for it.

**THE LIE TOLD WITH KNOWLEDGE**

The sentence ends with ***“And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly”***. Ibn Kathīr keeps the reading that the lie is spoken against God while the speaker knows it, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the occasion of the passage, which was a question from a deputation about honouring a man as the Christians honour Jesus. The question was refused as soon as it was put, and the refusal is placed beside the practice of inventing lines and giving them a source.

Think of a clerk who writes a line into the office ledger in another man's hand and then reads the ledger aloud to a visitor. The forgery and the reading are two acts, and the visitor is harmed by both, but the clerk's own knowledge is what makes the pair a deliberate crime. The verse reports the same pair with the knowledge added, and the reader is left to weigh the intention that the sentence has laid bare. What follows is the denial that any of the prophets could have asked for such honour.


---


## Verse 3:79

> It is not appropriate for someone who Allah has blessed with the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to say to people, “Worship me instead of Allah.” Rather, he would say, “Be devoted to the worship of your Lord ˹alone˺”—in accordance with what these prophets read in the Scripture and what they taught

**WHAT NO PROPHET EVER SAID**

The sentence begins with a denial of fitness: ***“It is not appropriate”*** and ***“for someone who Allah has blessed with the Scripture, wisdom”*** and ***“and prophethood to say to people, “Worship me instead of Allah.””***. The construction is explained by al-Qurṭubī as the statement that no such thing is fitting, and he compares it with other sentences of the same shape in the Book, while al-Ālūsī reads it as the declaration that the prophets are free of any such claim.

The section's lesson is drawn out by Ibn Kathīr, which notes that no prophet ever called his people to worship him or to worship anything other than God. The impossibility is kept absolute by al-Saʿdī, since such an instruction would be unbelief, and the man who gave it would not be a prophet at all. Since the gifts named in the sentence are the Book, the wisdom and the prophethood, the verse removes the claim by the very honours that were supposed to support it.

**THE WORDS THAT WERE ACTUALLY SAID**

The sentence then supplies what a prophet does say: ***“Rather, he would say, “Be devoted to the worship of your Lord ˹alone˺””***. The title is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as men of knowledge and understanding who put what they learn into practice, and al-Jalālayn explains the form of the word and shows it to be built from the name for lordship. The two speeches are placed side by side so that the reader can see the distance between them without a comment.

The instruction is then tied to its source: ***“in accordance with what these prophets read in the Scripture”*** and ***“and what they taught”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the teaching here is the constant study of the Book, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the point out, since the devotion asked for is measured by what the Book says and not by what a teacher invents. The verse therefore gives the community a test of every claim made in the name of a prophet, and the test is the record the prophet himself carried.

**THE OCCASION BEHIND THE SENTENCE**

The reports behind the passage are kept by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the Christians of Najrān claimed that Jesus had commanded them to take him as a divinity, and that some of the Muslims asked for permission to bow to the man who had been sent to them. The verse came as the refusal of both requests, and it settled the practice before it could take root. The two verses are kept together by al-Saʿdī as one argument against the same error from both sides.

Think of a physician who is handed his diploma and then asked by a town to accept the honours of a god. The diploma and the request cannot stand together, because the paper states the work he was trained for and the request states another. The verse makes the same exclusion in a single sentence, and it does so with the gifts that the claimant had been boasting of. What follows is the second half of the denial, which extends it from the messenger to the honoured servants of God.


---


## Verse 3:80

> And he would never ask you to take angels and prophets as lords. Would he ask you to disbelieve after you have submitted

**THE SECOND THING NEVER ASKED**

The denial widens from the messenger to the honoured servants: ***“And he would never ask you to take angels”*** and ***“and prophets as lords.”***. The variant readings of the clause are kept by al-Baghawī and al-Ālūsī, who note that the sentence carries on the denial of the verse before it. The groups that took each of these as lords are named by al-Jalālayn, with the Sabaeans taking the angels, the Jews taking ʿUzayr and the Christians taking Jesus.

The list of the never-asked extends in both directions, since the man who brought the message and the angels who carried it stand on the same side of the line. The two verses are kept by al-Saʿdī as a single argument, because the impossibility of the first request is the reason the second is impossible too. Since the sentence is stated as a denial of fact and not only of duty, the reader is told what the messengers were and what they were not.

**THE QUESTION THAT EXPECTS NO**

The verse closes with a question: ***“Would he ask you to disbelieve after you have submitted”***. The report of the question put by the Jews about taking a man as a lord is kept by al-Qurṭubī together with the refusal that followed it, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the address to the Quraysh, the Jews and the Christians. The clause is read as the statement that no command to disbelieve can come from a messenger, least of all after a people has surrendered.

The question is its own answer, and the shape is used deliberately, since a question puts the conclusion in the mind of the hearer. The lesson of the passage is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the honour given to a prophet is measured by the message he carries and not by the person who carries it. What follows for the reader is a closed door on the practice that the deputation had proposed, and the door is closed by the impossibility of the thing rather than by a prohibition alone.

**THE LINE THE VERSE DRAWS**

The two verses together draw a line that runs through every community, since the error they describe has appeared among the worshippers of angels, among those who raised a teacher to a divine rank, and among those who were willing to do the same with a prophet. The three examples are kept side by side by al-Jalālayn, and the verse refuses all three with one denial. The reader is shown that the fault is one fault under three names.

Think of a household that keeps a portrait of the founder in the hall, and a visitor who begins to leave food before it. The household stops the practice because the portrait belongs to a servant of the house and not to its owner. The verse draws the same line, and the next passage goes further back than the community and reports a covenant that God took from the prophets themselves, long before the dispute began.


---


## Verse 3:81

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah made a covenant with the prophets, ˹saying,˺ “Now that I have given you the Book and wisdom, if there comes to you a messenger confirming what you have, you must believe in him and support him.” He added, “Do you affirm this covenant and accept this commitment?” They said, “Yes, we do.” Allah said, “Then bear witness, and I too am a Witness.”

**THE COVENANT TAKEN FROM THE PROPHETS**

The sūrah goes back further than the dispute and reports an agreement: ***“˹Remember˺ when Allah made a covenant with the prophets”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the account of the pledge taken from the prophets, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the opening word is the object of an implied verb of remembering, since the passage is addressed to the one who is told to recall it. The report kept by Ibn Kathīr is that the pledge was taken from every prophet from Adam down to ʿĪsā.

The terms of the covenant are then stated: ***“Now that I have given you the Book”*** and ***“and wisdom,”*** and ***“if there comes to you a messenger”*** and ***“confirming what you have, you must believe in him”*** and ***“and support him.”***. The reading of the early scholars is recorded by al-Qurṭubī, namely that the prophets were bound to believe in one another and to call their communities to believe, so that the support named here is the support of a common message.

**WHAT THE PLEDGE BOUND THEM TO**

The clause about support is the part that turns the passage toward the community of the last messenger, since a prophet who has died cannot support a messenger who comes after him, while his community can. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the prophets were to make the description and the merits of the coming messenger known to one another and to their nations. The covenant, al-Saʿdī notes, was taken because of the gifts the prophets had been given, since a gift from God calls for a duty in return.

The words of the pledge are then put as a question: ***“He added, “Do you affirm this covenant”*** and ***“and accept this commitment?””***. The gravity of the passage is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the agreement is taken with a question and answered in plain words, so that no one can say the terms were slipped past him. Since the covenant binds the teachers of every community, the dispute of the People of the Book is answered from the record of their own prophets.

**THE ANSWER AND THE WITNESSES**

The answer is reported in the words of those who gave it: ***“They said, “Yes, we do.””***. The plain sense of the exchange is kept by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the pledge was affirmed before the witness was called. The sentence gives the reader the shape of a covenant done openly, with the terms stated, the question asked and the answer given.

The verse then closes with ***“Allah said, “Then bear witness”*** and ***“and I too am a Witness.””***. Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the first call is addressed to the prophets and the second to God as the witness of the whole exchange, and al-Baghawī keeps the passage among the pledges that the earlier books recorded. A covenant made in the presence of the parties and of God leaves the later generations standing inside an agreement they did not make.

Think of a guild that admits its members under an oath taken at the founding, where the terms include a duty toward every master who will come afterward. A member who denies the later master denies the oath itself, and the verse puts the dispute of the two communities on that footing. What follows is a single line about the end of a person who turns back from the pledge, and the line closes the passage with the plainest of words.


---


## Verse 3:82

> Whoever turns back after this, they will be the rebellious

**THE TURNING AND ITS NAME**

The covenant is followed by one short sentence: ***“Whoever turns back after this, they will be the rebellious”***. The turn is read by al-Ṭabarī as the abandonment of belief in the messengers whom God sends to confirm what the prophets already had, and al-Qurṭubī reads the last word as the one who goes out of the faith. The sentence is placed directly after the exchange of the covenant, so the refusal is measured against the words that were affirmed.

The closing word is the key to the verse, since a rebel is not a person who never made a promise but one who broke the one he made. The same reading of the last word is kept by al-Baghawī, which gives the disobedient and the one outside belief, and al-Ālūsī records the early reading that the turning meant turning away from belief in Muḥammad and from supporting him. The sense kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the nations broke the covenant their prophets had brought them.

**WHY ONE SENTENCE IS ENOUGH**

The sentence carries no list of penalties, and the omission is part of its force, since the passage has already reported the terms and the answer. The closing word is kept by al-Jalālayn as the wicked, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the loss is the covenant itself. The sūrah's method is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, where the great passages of argument are closed with sentences short enough to remember.

Since the terms were stated by God and accepted by the prophets, a later turning back is not an open question but a departure from a settled agreement. The verse therefore places the reader inside the covenant as well, because the community that hears the record is asked to keep the terms its prophets had accepted. What follows is a question that turns the whole argument around and asks what the turners are actually looking for.

**THE VERDICT LEFT IN ONE WORD**

The two verses are kept together by al-Saʿdī, which notes that the account of the covenant and the account of the breach belong to one lesson, delivered so that the community can see where a refusal leads. The sentence is short and the verdict is heavy, and the shortness is the point, because nothing more needs to be said once the agreement has been described. A reader who has followed the passage from the prayer of Ḥannah has heard the terms, the question and the answer, and the record is complete before the refusal is mentioned.

Think of a village that agrees to keep a bridge in repair, where each household owes a plank, and a household that later walks past the gap without a word. The village has no long sentence for such a neighbour, and the verse keeps none for the one who turns back. The next verse then asks what such a person could be seeking, when everything in the heavens and the earth has already submitted.


---


## Verse 3:83

> Do they desire a way other than Allah’s—knowing that all those in the heavens and the earth submit to His Will, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned

**THE WAY THEY WERE SEEKING**

The question that closes the passage is put to the arguers: ***“Do they desire a way other than Allah’s”***. The report kept by al-Qurṭubī is that Kaʿb b. al-Ashraf and his companions brought their dispute with the Christians before the messenger and asked which of the two parties was more entitled to the way of Ibrāhīm. The same account is kept by al-Baghawī, who notes that each side claimed to stand on the religion of Ibrāhīm, and the report of the dispute is kept by al-Ālūsī with its chain to Ibn ʿAbbās.

The question is shaped as a rebuke rather than as a request for information, since a desire is named and not a doubt. The argument had been presented as a search for the better way, and the sentence exposes it as the desire for a way that is not God's. Since the two parties had been claiming the same ancestor, the verse asks what their dispute was really seeking, and the answer is placed before the reader as a question.

**THE SUBMISSION THEY OVERLOOKED**

The question is completed with ***“knowing that all those in the heavens”*** and ***“and the earth submit to His Will, willingly”*** and ***“or unwillingly”***. The first part of the submission is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the willing service of the angels and the second as the submission of those on earth who yield when they must, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the two clauses. The rebuke is drawn out by Ibn Kathīr, which notes that the people who prefer another way are answered by the record of the creation itself.

The verse therefore sets the dispute inside a larger fact, because a community that argues about the best way is arguing inside a world that has already submitted. The passage is kept by al-Saʿdī as one of the fundamentals on which the books and the messengers agree, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the name Islam carries the sense of submission and obedience before it carries any narrower sense. Since the whole creation is described as submitted, the question of the verse is not which way is best but whether any other way exists.

**THE RETURN THAT CLOSES THE LINE**

The verse ends with ***“and to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the return is the gathering for the account, and al-Baghawī keeps the same sense of the clause, with the whole creation going back to the One who made it. The line is placed after the mention of submission so that the two facts hold together, since the same authority that is obeyed by compulsion will be the authority that judges.

Think of two men arguing about the fastest road out of a valley while the river at the end of every road runs to the same sea. The argument may be sincere, but the destination is not in question, and the verse puts the dispute beside that fact. What follows for the reader is the creed that the sūrah then recites, and the recitation is given to the community as its own answer to every such argument.


---


## Verse 3:84

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants; and what was given to Moses, Jesus, and other prophets from their Lord—we make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we ˹fully˺ submit.”

**THE CREED PUT IN THE MOUTH OF THE COMMUNITY**

The answer to the dispute is given as a declaration: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “We believe in Allah”*** and ***“and what has been revealed to us”***. The instruction is read by al-Ālūsī as the command to the messenger to report the faith of himself and of the believers, and the plural form in the sentence joins the two together in one statement. The reading kept by al-Ṭabarī is that the sentence is the confession of the community, and the form fixes it as words to be said rather than an argument to be made.

The declaration then reaches back through the line of the prophets: ***“and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob”*** and ***“and his descendants”***. The list is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as belief in each prophet together with the scripture given to him, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the names and of the descendants who followed Jacob. The order of the names is the order of the family from which the two communities had drawn their claims.

**THE LINE THAT IS NOT DIVIDED**

The sentence then adds the founders of the two later communities: ***“and what was given to Moses, Jesus”*** and ***“and other prophets from their Lord”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the belief covers every prophet and every book, and al-Saʿdī notes that this creed is the one on which the books and the messengers had already agreed. The sūrah's purpose is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, since the community is given a confession that includes the prophets of both disputing parties.

The next clause states the principle that the list was building toward: ***“we make no distinction between any of them”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that no prophet is accepted while another is refused, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a faith that takes the whole line. Since the two communities had each built a case on one of the names, the refusal to divide them removes the ground of the dispute. The sentence therefore answers the disputants twice, first by naming their own prophets inside the creed and then by refusing the selection that had set one against another.

**THE SUBMISSION THAT CLOSES THE CREED**

The declaration ends with ***“and to Him we ˹fully˺ submit.””***. The clause is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the surrender of worship to God alone, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of a submission that belongs to the One who sent the prophets. Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the instruction to say these words is addressed to the messenger and, through him, to everyone who is addressed by the Book. The creed therefore ends where the previous verse ended, with the whole creation returning to God and the community placing itself in that return.

Think of a museum where two families argue over which wing holds their ancestor's work, and the curator posts one notice naming every room in the building. The notice does not take sides, and it ends the argument by including the whole collection. The verse posts the same kind of notice for the prophets, and the next verse states what is lost by the person who will not accept it.


---


## Verse 3:85

> Whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them, and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**THE WAY THAT WILL NOT BE TAKEN**

The creed is followed by a rule about its refusal: ***“Whoever seeks a way other than Islam,”*** and ***“it will never be accepted from them”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that a religion sought outside the surrender to God will not be received, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the construction of the sentence. The sentence concerns the seeking rather than the finding, since a person who seeks another way has already left the one that was given.

The sentence is stated in the form of a general rule, and the generality is deliberate, because the dispute that produced the passage was a dispute about the way of one ancestor. The report that the verse came down about a group who turned back from the faith and left Madīnah, twelve men among them, is kept by al-Ālūsī, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of those who abandoned the religion. The rule is therefore given for the community in whose presence the turning happened.

**WHAT THE NAME CARRIES**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the sense of the name and notes that surrender and obedience are its first meaning, while the later usage fixes it on the path revealed through the last messenger, and both senses are found in the Book. The reading kept by Ibn Kathīr is that no religion other than this one is accepted with God, and the clause is placed in the same sūrah that had just recited the faith of Abraham. Since the creed of the previous verse includes every prophet, the way named here is the one those prophets walked.

The passage is kept by al-Saʿdī as the close of the argument that began with the claims about Ibrāhīm, and the closing rule answers the claims with a definition. The reader is not asked to weigh two communities against each other but to see the way itself, and the sentence settles the case on the side of what was sent rather than on the side of who claims it.

**THE LOSS NAMED AT THE END**

The verse closes with ***“and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers”***. The last word is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the description of a person who misses Paradise and everything in it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a loss that is total and lasting. Al-Baghawī keeps the agreement of the reports on the seriousness of the sentence, and al-Ālūsī keeps the report of those who turned back as the first people addressed by it.

Think of a traveler who is told that the pass over the mountain is the only route that reaches the city, and who spends the season looking for another. The loss is measured in the season and in the destination at once, and the verse measures the same loss for the one who will not walk the way that was given. What follows is a passage that describes the people who had every proof and turned back anyway, and the description begins with a question about how they could be guided at all.


---


## Verse 3:86

> How will Allah guide a people who chose to disbelieve after they had believed, acknowledged the Messenger to be true, and received clear proofs? For Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**THE QUESTION THAT STATES THE CASE**

The passage opens with a question: ***“How will Allah guide a people”***. The question is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that guidance is far from such a people, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense, since the question is the strongest way of denying the thing asked about. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the people in view had disbelieved in God after belief had entered them.

The charge is then set out in stages: ***“who chose to disbelieve after they had believed, acknowledged the Messenger to be true”*** and ***“and received clear proofs?”***. Three steps are placed in order, belief, the acknowledgement that the messenger was true, and the proofs that came with him, so the reader is shown how much had been given before the turning. The same sequence is kept by Ibn Kathīr, which notes that the proofs named here were manifest ones.

**THE REPORT BEHIND THE SENTENCE**

The occasion of the passage is kept by al-Qurṭubī, which records the report of Ibn ʿAbbās about a man of the Anṣār who accepted the faith and then turned back and joined the idolaters, and who afterwards regretted it and sent word to his people to ask whether a return was still open to him. The answer came in the verse that follows the sentence of the curse, and the passage is therefore read as the account of a real case as well as a rule. The reader is shown a question that was asked by a man standing outside the door.

The impossibility is kept strong by al-Saʿdī, which notes that people who knew the faith and entered it and then turned on their heels have placed themselves far from guidance by their own act. Since the door was closed by the turning and not by the asking, the verse leaves its reader with the weight of the step rather than with a verdict on the person. What follows is the answer to the man of the Anṣār, and the answer is placed after the sentence of the curse.

**THE RULE AND THE READING OF IT**

The verse ends with ***“For Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the wrongdoers here are the idolaters and those who are not fit for the religion, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān takes up the doubt that the sentence seems to raise, since many people have turned back and then been guided afterwards. The answer given there is that the withholding is stated in the way people speak of a punishment following a crime, and what the sentence describes is the danger of the turning rather than a law that shuts the door on repentance.

Think of a traveler who is shown the road, walks a mile along it, and then turns back to the town he left, and who is warned that the road is hard to find a second time. The warning describes the danger of the turning, and the verse that follows shows the man of the Anṣār finding the road again. The passage therefore holds the severity and the door in one account, and the reader is left with both.


---


## Verse 3:87

> Their reward is that they will be condemned by Allah, the angels, and all of humanity

**THE CURSE AND WHO PRONOUNCES IT**

The sentence names the reward: ***“Their reward is that they will be condemned by Allah, the angels”*** and ***“and all of humanity”***. The curse is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the punishment of God together with the curse of the angels and of the believers, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same three parties in the same order. The word for humanity is the widest of its kind in the language, and the sentence leaves no witness outside it.

The three are arranged from the highest to the widest, and the arrangement matches the offence. The turning had been public, since the man had lived among a community that knew his story, and the condemnation is public in the same measure. The reading kept by Ibn Kathīr is that the three parties join in the sentence, and the joining is the point of the line.

**WHY THE WHOLE OF HUMANITY IS NAMED**

The inclusion of all humanity is the part that asks for a reading, because a person may accept the judgment of God and still expect the community around him to stay neutral. The reading kept by al-Saʿdī is that the sentence concerns people whose knowledge of the faith was complete, and it stands at the far end of the scale that the passage has been drawing. The curse of the angels and of humanity belongs to the same account, since a refusal made in the open is answered in the open.

The elements of the sentence are kept by al-Qurṭubī, which notes that no exception is written into it, and the verse that follows keeps the same people in view while it states how long the state lasts. Since the two verses stand together, the reader is shown a verdict that is stated once and then described from two sides. What follows for the community is a warning about the publicity of a refusal, because the witnesses named here are not a private court.

**THE SHAPE OF THE VERDICT**

The sentence is short and complete, with the parties, the curse and the people all named in one line. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the connection with the question of the previous verse, so the reward is understood as the answer to that question, and the answer is given without a single word of argument. The brevity is the strength of the line, and the verse that follows adds the duration that the sentence implies.

Think of a town that publishes a notice signed by the magistrate, the guild and the whole market. The notice is heavier than a private word, and the verse reports the same kind of weight. A refusal that was made in the open is answered by a witness that is public, and the sentence carries the whole of that answer in one line. What follows is a verse that names the duration of the state, and then a verse that opens the door of return for anyone who turns back while there is still time.


---


## Verse 3:88

> They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺

**THE DURATION AND THE WEIGHT**

The sentence continues the account of the same people: ***“They will be in Hell forever.”***. The abiding is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the state of the curse, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the abiding is in the Fire which the curse implies, so the two readings join rather than differ. The sentence then states the second fact: ***“Their punishment will not be lightened”***. The reading kept by Ibn Kathīr is that no relief comes to them at any point.

The two clauses hold together because a duration without a weight would be a lesser sentence and a weight without a duration would be a passing one. The words of the early scholars on the passage are kept by al-Qurṭubī, and the sentence is placed after the naming of the curse so that the reader measures the state from both sides. Since the punishment is never lightened, the passage cannot be read as a description of a temporary correction.

**THE RESPITE THAT IS NEVER GIVEN**

The verse closes with ***“nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no postponement is granted, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a respite that is never given. The clause closes the second door, since a person who cannot shorten a sentence may still hope for a delay, and the verse denies both in one line. The three parts of the sentence answer the three hopes of a person under sentence, and each hope is closed by a clause of its own, so nothing is left to the imagination.

The order of the clauses follows the way a hearer would ask his questions, first how long, then how heavy, then whether there is time. The seriousness of the passage is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, since the verses that follow it turn at once to the door of repentance, and the turn would have no force if the sentence had been stated lightly. The reader is shown a closed case and an open door in the same paragraph of the sūrah, and the order of the two is the sūrah's own instruction.

**THE DOOR THAT THE NEXT VERSE OPENS**

The severity of the line is not the last word of the passage, since the verse that follows begins with an exception. The report of the man of the Anṣār who regretted his turning and asked about the way back, is kept by al-Qurṭubī, and the exception was revealed in answer to his question. Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as one account in which the warning and the way out stand side by side.

Think of a sentence that names the prison, the weight of the chain and the absence of any hearing for release, and a clerk who then adds a line about the pardon that is still available. The pardon does not deny the sentence, and the verse that follows does not deny this one. What follows is the exception itself, with its two conditions and the two names of God that close it.


---


## Verse 3:89

> As for those who repent afterwards and mend their ways, then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**THE EXCEPTION AND WHO IT COVERS**

The passage turns with an exception: ***“As for those who repent afterwards”***. The sentence is kept by al-Ṭabarī as the exception to the account that came before it, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the man of the Anṣār whose question brought the verse down. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the repentance is the return from disbelief and from the hypocrisy that had gone with it.

The second clause adds the condition: ***“and mend their ways”***. The mending is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the sincere declaration of God's oneness, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of amends made in action. The two clauses together show that the return described here is not a word spoken in fear, since a sentence that named repentance alone could be read as a formula. What follows for the reader is a standard to measure a return by, and the standard is the change that comes after it.

**THE TWO NAMES AT THE CLOSE**

The sentence ends with ***“then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”***. The reading kept by al-Jalālayn is of forgiveness for the one who repents and mercy for the one who dies in that state, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same promise for the person who returns while there is time. The pairing is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the two names answer the two clauses of the condition, since the first covers the past and the second covers the fear that remains after forgiveness.

The promise is placed after the report of the curse and the fire, and the placement is deliberate, because the reader has just been shown what the turning costs. Since the door is opened by the two names at the end, the passage cannot be read as a sentence on a class of people, and each person is left with the conditions in his own hands. The verse therefore completes the account that the man of the Anṣār had asked about, and it does so in the plainest words. The two clauses of the condition are no formality, and the promise that follows them is a return accepted rather than merely announced.

**WHY THE PASSAGE NEEDED THE TURN**

The severity of the two verses before it would be crushing without this sentence, and the sūrah supplies the sentence before moving on. The reading kept by al-Ṭabarī is that the exception belongs to the same people, so that no one reading the passage can treat the curse as a fate fixed before birth. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same understanding and notes the promise attached to the return, since the order of the two verses is a mercy in itself.

Think of a man who has burned a bridge behind him and then finds a ford at the side of the same river. The ford does not deny the burning, and it refuses the claim that the far bank is gone. The verse works in the same way, and the passage then turns to a second kind of turning, where the person does not come back at all but walks further away.


---


## Verse 3:90

> Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed then increase in disbelief, their repentance will never be accepted. It is they who are astray

**THE TURNING THAT GROWS**

The next sentence describes a second case: ***“Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed”*** and ***“then increase in disbelief,”***. The reading of Qatādah, ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī and al-Ḥasan is recorded by al-Qurṭubī, which takes the verse to concern the Jews who disbelieved in Jesus and the Gospel and then grew in disbelief toward Muḥammad. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is of a growth that does not waver, since the increase is the mark of a settled choice.

The turning here is not the single step that the previous verse forgave but a road walked away from the faith. The distinction explains why the two sentences end so differently, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading of the two cases. Since the first verse opened its door to the person who turned and returned, this verse addresses the person who turned and kept going, and the difference is stated in the words of the text itself.

**THE REPENTANCE THAT IS NOT TAKEN**

The sentence then closes the door: ***“their repentance will never be accepted.”***. The sentence then closes the door, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the refusal concerns the repentance offered at the last breath, when the door of life is closing, while Ibn Kathīr keeps the same sense of a return made too late. The reading keeps the verse consistent with the one before it, since the return of a living person is not refused, and the sentence is aimed at the one who dies in his refusal.

The clause is placed after the increase for a reason, because the growth in disbelief is what makes a late return impossible rather than the first step alone. The connection between the two verses is kept by al-Ṭabarī, which notes that the increase is the thing that separates the two cases. Since the sentence is a threat and not a decree about a nation, the reader is asked to weigh the direction he is walking rather than the community he was born into.

**THE NAME AT THE END**

The sentence ends with ***“It is they”*** and ***“who are astray”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is of people far from guidance and from surrender, and the plural form places the verdict on the group as a group. The sūrah's habit is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān at this point, since the great warnings are closed with a phrase short enough to carry away, and this one names the loss as a wandering rather than as a fine.

Think of a man who leaves a road, walks deliberately farther from it, and then asks to be met where the road used to be. The distance is his own work, and the sentence names the same distance in the language of wandering. The two cases are placed next to each other so that the reader can measure the difference for himself. What follows is a verse about the ransom such a person might offer at the end, and the ransom is named in a measure that the whole world could not hold.


---


## Verse 3:91

> Indeed, if each of those who disbelieve then die as disbelievers were to offer a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world, it would never be accepted from them. It is they who will suffer a painful punishment, and they will have no helpers

**THE RANSOM AND ITS MEASURE**

The sentence opens a case and names a price: ***“Indeed, if each of those”*** and ***“who disbelieve then die”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the account of people who end their lives outside the faith, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the measure used for the price. The reading kept by al-Baghawī is that the measure is the amount that would fill the earth, and al-Ālūsī adds the extent from its east to its west. The reader is given a price so large that the imagination cannot hold it.

The price is then named in the terms of the most desired of metals: ***“as disbelievers were to offer a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world,”*** and ***“it would never be accepted from them.”***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is of a payment offered to ransom oneself and refused, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the conditional sentence. Since the offer is refused, the sentence has taken away the last instrument a person might expect to use, and it does so while naming the most valuable one.

**THE DEATH THAT FIXES THE MATTER**

The clause about death is the hinge of the verse, and Ibn Kathīr draws the point out by joining the two cases, since neither a repentance offered at the end of life nor a ransom offered on the Day of Resurrection is accepted. The reason is stated in the sentence itself, because the offer is made by people who die as they are, and a payment made after the judgment changes nothing about the person who makes it.

The sequence of the passage is kept in view by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since the previous verse had closed the door on the repentance of those who grew in disbelief, and this verse states what remains to such people. The passage is kept by al-Saʿdī as part of the same warning, and the reader is shown a person who has brought the dearest thing he owns to a settlement that will not take it. What follows for the reader is a lesson about the timing of a return rather than about the size of a payment.

**THE PAIN AND THE ABSENCE OF HELPERS**

The verse closes with two statements: ***“It is they”*** and ***“who will suffer a painful punishment”*** and ***“and they will have no helpers”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the pain named here reaches the heart, and he reads the helpers as those who could save a person from the punishment of God. The two clauses answer the two hopes of a person in that position, the hope that the pain is small and the hope that someone will intervene.

Think of a man who brings a wagon of gold to a gate that was closed at sunset, and who finds the road behind him already dark. The gold is not refused because of its weight, and the verse says as much about the ransom. What follows is a turn in the sūrah toward the believers and toward the charity that is accepted, and the contrast between the two passages is the sūrah's way of teaching the difference.


---


## Verse 3:92

> You will never achieve righteousness until you donate some of what you cherish. And whatever you give is certainly well known to Allah

**THE NAME AND WHAT IT COVERS**

The address turns to the believers with a condition: ***“You will never achieve righteousness”***. The reading kept by al-Baghawī, from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and Mujāhid, takes righteousness to refer to Paradise, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of the reward that follows a life of piety. The further reading of the reward and the honour that stand with God is added by Ibn ʿAbbās. The sentence is therefore a statement about attainment, and it places a condition in the way of it.

The breadth of what the verse promises is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that righteousness first refers to the perfect fulfilment of a person's rights and is also used for giving, so the verse is understood from both sides at once. The reading is kept by al-Qurṭubī as well, with the reports that name Paradise. Since righteousness takes in the whole of a good life, the condition attached to it here is not a small one, and the reader is prepared for the demand that follows.
