**THE THING THAT IS CHERISHED**

The condition is then stated: ***“until you donate some of what you cherish.”***. The phrase does not ask for a quantity but for a quality, since what is given must come from the part of the property that the owner loves. The account kept by Ibn Kathīr is of the man of the Anṣār whose garden was the dearest of his holdings and who gave it away, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same report at length. The story turns the sentence into a picture, and the picture is of a man giving the field he would rather keep.

The reading kept by al-Saʿdī is that the giving named here is from the best of what a person owns and the purest of it. Since the demand is measured by love and not by weight, the verse reaches the will rather than the purse, and the reader is shown that the gift which costs nothing is not the gift described. The sentence is a test of attachment as much as a rule about charity.

**THE KNOWLEDGE THAT CLOSES THE LINE**

The verse ends with ***“And whatever you give is certainly well known to Allah”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God is aware of what is spent and of the intention behind it, whether the giver sought the face of God or the praise of people. Al-Ālūsī keeps the sentence as a fresh statement about what benefits the believers, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the same sense of a knowledge that leaves nothing out.

Think of a merchant who keeps his best cloth at the back of the shop and sells only the flawed rolls, and who then wonders why his name is not trusted. The verse asks the same merchant for the roll he keeps at the back, and the last sentence tells him who is watching the counter. What follows is a passage about the food laws that the People of the Book had argued over, and the sūrah answers the argument from the history of their own ancestor.


---


## Verse 3:93

> All food was lawful for the children of Israel, except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Bring the Torah and read it, if your claims are true.”

**THE CLAIM THAT MET THE ANSWER**

The verse opens with a statement about a whole category: ***“All food was lawful”*** and ***“for the children of Israel,”*** and ***“except what Israel”*** and ***“made unlawful for himself”***. The occasion is recorded by al-Jalālayn, which was the objection of the Jews that the messenger claimed the creed of Ibrāhīm while eating the meat of the camel, since Abraham had not eaten it. The answer is given in the form of a history, and the history is about their own ancestor.

The reading kept by al-Qurṭubī is that the lawful here covers what is permitted, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same plain sense. Since the question was about a food law, the verse answers it by showing when the law came to be, and the answer removes the claim that the rule had been Abraham's. The sentence therefore does two things at once, since it grants the restriction and denies its antiquity.

**THE MAN AND THE VOW BEHIND THE EXCEPTION**

The exception is then explained: ***“for himself before the Torah was revealed.”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the account that Israel, who is Jacob, forbade himself the meat and the milk of camels, and al-Jalālayn keeps the report that he made the vow when he was afflicted with a painful condition in his back and gave the food up if he were cured. The report of al-Kalbī on the same objection is kept by al-Ālūsī, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the incident among the arguments of the sūrah with the People of the Book.

The order of the clause matters, since the vow came before the Torah and the restriction was therefore the act of one man rather than a law of Abraham. The reading kept by al-Baghawī is that the children of Israel followed their father in the matter, and the source of the practice is named without blaming the man for it. The reader is shown a private vow that later became a rule in the hands of a community.

**THE CHALLENGE PUT TO THE CLAIMANTS**

The verse then turns to the people who were arguing: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Bring the Torah”*** and ***“and read it, if your claims are true.””***. Ibn Kathīr keeps the reading that the challenge is met by the book they carried, since the record would show whether the restriction was Abraham's or Jacob's. The report of the question put to the Jews about what their ancestor had forbidden himself is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, and the answer given at the time did not match their claim.

The passage is kept by al-Saʿdī among the arguments the Jews used against the two prophets, since the claim that the law could not change was one of their instruments. The verse therefore answers the argument with the book that the arguers held, and the challenge is not an assertion but an invitation to read. What follows is a verse about the people who would still invent a claim after that reading.


---


## Verse 3:94

> Then whoever still fabricates lies about Allah, they will be the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

**THE LINE DRAWN AFTER THE PROOF**

The sentence follows the challenge with a warning: ***“Then whoever still fabricates lies about Allah,”***. The fabrication is read by al-Ṭabarī as the invention of a claim about God, and the expression carries the sense of something made up rather than mistaken. The reading kept by al-Ālūsī is that the invention here is the claim that the prohibition belonged to the prophets and their communities before the Torah. The sentence therefore names the next step after the evidence has been placed in front of a person.

The verse adds the word that fixes the time: the fabrication comes after the proof. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the claim is made after the exposition in the Torah had shown the claimants to be wrong, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a lie told after the matter has become clear. Since the proof had been brought from their own book, the person who continued had no room left for an honest error.

**THE NAME THAT CLOSES THE SENTENCE**

The sentence ends with ***“they will be the ˹true˺ wrongdoers”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the wrongdoers here are those who lie about God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of people who pass from truth into falsehood. The name is heavier than a mistake and lighter than the curses of the earlier passage, and the difference is the proof that the claimants had been given.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the same verdict on the people who invent a claim against God, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the close of the argument about the food law. The sūrah's method is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān here, since the argument is answered from the record, and the closing sentence is about the honesty of the person who handles the record. The reader is left with a question about his own use of what he has been given, and the question is not aimed at one community alone. The lesson is about the handling of a text already in a person's hands, and about the honesty of the one who reads it aloud to others.

**WHAT THE VERSE LEAVES WITH THE READER**

The warning is placed after the invitation to read, and the placement gives the sentence its force. A person who has been asked to open a book and who then repeats an invented claim is not in the position of the man who never saw the book. The connection between the two verses is kept by al-Ṭabarī, and the connection is the whole meaning of the clause that begins this one. A person who repeats an invented line after the record has been opened is not describing a doubt, and the verse treats the act as a fabrication rather than as an error.

Think of a clerk who is shown the ledger and who then repeats the invented figure to the owner of the shop. The ledger is his own, and the verse reports the same situation with the same word for the invention. What follows is a sentence in which the truthfulness of God is stated and the way of the upright ancestor is set before the reader as the way to walk.


---


## Verse 3:95

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah has declared the truth. So follow the Way of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist.”

**THE TRUTH STATED AND THE WAY NAMED**

The passage closes with a command: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah has declared the truth.”***. The sentence is read by al-Ṭabarī as the statement that God has spoken truly in the matter that was disputed, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the truthfulness of God is established by what He has said. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the point that the restriction at issue was not written in the Torah, so the sentence is the end of the argument about the food law.

The command then names the way: ***“So follow the Way of Abraham, the upright”***. The reading kept by al-Baghawī is that the call is addressed to the people who had been arguing, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading of a way described by the word for the one who turns from every false religion. Since the sūrah had already answered the claim about the ancestor, the command takes the ancestor's way as the standard and sets it before the disputants.

**THE ANCESTOR AND THE CLAIM ABOUT HIM**

The verse ends with ***“who was not a polytheist.””***. The reading kept by Ibn ʿAbbās is that the ancestor was free of any partnership given to God, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sūrah's argument to its head at this point, since the way named here is the one described in the earlier passage (3:67 — **“Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian”**). The two sentences belong together, because the denial of the two titles and the naming of the way are one answer.

The command is therefore not a change of subject but the conclusion of the dispute, and the reader is shown a claim about an ancestor replaced by an invitation to walk his way. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of the upright way, and the sentence leaves the argument behind for a practice. What follows for the reader is the answer to a question about belonging, and the answer is about following rather than about descent.

**THE CLOSE OF THE ARGUMENT**

The verse is the last of the passage that began with the invitation to a common term, and it closes the dispute about the ancestor with a command. The reading of the sentence as the final word on the matter is kept by al-Ṭabarī, and the sūrah then turns to a new subject with the passage that follows. The change of subject is itself a signal, since the argument has been answered and the community is given its own affairs to attend to.

Think of a signature at the foot of a document, placed there after the terms have been read and the objections answered, and after which the parties move to other business. The verse signs the argument in the same way, and it does so with the two words truth and way. What follows is a passage about the community that carries this way, and about the duty that its members owe to one another. A reader who has followed the argument from the dispute about Ibrāhīm is left with a way to walk rather than a title to carry, and the change of subject marks the end of the case.


---


## Verse 3:96

> Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people

**THE HOUSE THAT CAME FIRST**

The sūrah turns from the arguments of the People of the Book to the House itself: ***“Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established”*** and ***“for humanity is the one at Bakkah”***. The occasion is recorded by al-Jalālayn, which notes that the sentence came down when the People of the Book claimed that their prayer direction had come before the direction of the Muslims. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the House named here is the one at Mecca, and the answer is given in the order of history.

The first of the three honours is stated by the opening expression, since the sentence places this House at the head of every house of worship on the earth. On this point, Al-Ālūsī keeps the report of the Jewish claim that brought the verse down, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the clause about humanity as a reference to the believers and to every community that would face this House in prayer. The claim of priority is therefore answered with a priority of another kind, and the difference is named in the opening word.

**THE BLESSING AND THE GUIDANCE**

The sentence then names two things that the House carries: ***“a blessed sanctuary and a guide”*** and ***“for ˹all˺ people”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the spot holds forgiveness and mercy, and the guidance named here is the direction toward which the prophets and the believers have turned. Here, Al-Jalālayn adds the report that the House was built by the angels before Adam and that the sanctuary in Jerusalem followed it forty years later, so that the order of the two houses is settled by a report and not by a claim.

Since the guidance is for all people and not for one community, the sentence takes the House out of the argument between two claims and places it in the history of the whole creation. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the plain sense of a blessed place, and al-Saʿdī draws the greatness of the sanctuary out and notes that the blessings named here belong to the place and to those who come to it. The reader is shown a house that was not built to answer an argument, and the argument is answered by it all the same.

**WHAT THE ORDER OF HISTORY SETTLES**

The sūrah had been answering claims about Abraham and about the books that came after him, and the same method is used again. On the same passage, Ibn Kathīr keeps the reading that this House was appointed for humanity, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets out the distinctions named in the verse one by one, beginning with the priority of the House among the places of worship. Since the claim of the People of the Book rested on a date, the answer rests on a date as well, and the two are weighed on the same scale.

Think of two families who argue over which of them opened the market in a town, until the town's oldest ledger is opened and the first entry is found in the hand of neither. The ledger settles the argument by naming the market itself, and the verse settles the same kind of claim with a house. What follows is a verse that names the signs inside it and the obligation that the House lays upon those who are able to reach it.


---


## Verse 3:97

> In it are clear signs and the standing-place of Abraham. Whoever enters it should be safe. Pilgrimage to this House is an obligation by Allah upon whoever is able among the people. And whoever disbelieves, then surely Allah is not in need of ˹any of His˺ creation

**THE SIGNS INSIDE THE HOUSE**

The sentence opens with what the House holds: ***“In it are clear signs”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the signs named by the early scholars, among them the standing-place of Ibrāhīm, the stone on which he stood to build the House with the marks of his feet upon it, and the length of time the building has stood with hands passing over it. With this in view, Al-Ālūsī adds the sign of the tyrants who marched against the House and were destroyed, with the Companions of the Elephant named among them.

The first sign is then named directly: ***“and the standing-place of Abraham. Whoever enters it should be safe. Pilgrimage to this House is an obligation by Allah upon whoever is able among the people.”***. Following the same line, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the safety is a fact of the place, since no one is killed or wronged inside it, and it notes that the reward of a good deed is multiplied there and that birds do not fly over it. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the two elements of the wall of Ishmael and the black stone among the signs.

**THE SAFETY AND THE OBLIGATION**

The safety and the pilgrimage are placed next to each other, and the pairing carries the meaning of the whole passage, because a house where a person is safe is a house to which a person is bound to come. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the obligation falls on the person who can walk to the House and has what he needs on the way, and al-Saʿdī draws the same conditions out of the sentence. On the reading followed here, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets the distinctions of the House out in order, with its blessings, its guidance and the duty that follows from both.

The duty is then stated as a matter of ability rather than of every single person, and the clause about the one who is able is where the scholars of the early community placed the conditions of the journey. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the readings of the sentence and notes that the journey is owed by the person who has the means for it, and Ibn Kathīr records the same conditions in the discussion of this verse. So the passage names a house, a safety and a duty, and each of the three is stated plainly enough for a reader to act on.

**THE REFUSAL THAT HURTS NO ONE**

The sentence closes with ***“And whoever disbelieves, then surely Allah is not in need of ˹any of His˺ creation”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the refusal of the duty is a form of disbelief, and the closing clause answers the refusal before it is put, since a person who declines a duty is answering for himself rather than depriving the One who laid it down. In the same reading, Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a sufficiency that belongs to God alone.

Think of a town that keeps a gate open for every traveler, where the travelers who pass through it gain the shade and the water on the other side. The duty and the safety stand together for a reason, since a house that protects the visitor is a house the visitor is bound to reach. A man who refuses the gate deprives himself of the road and leaves the gate exactly as it was, and the same is true of the House and the pilgrimage. The passage then turns from the House to two questions addressed to the People of the Book, and the questions take up the practice described a few verses earlier.


---


## Verse 3:98

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Why do you deny the revelations of Allah, when Allah is a Witness to what you do?”

**THE QUESTION AND THE ADDRESS**

The passage returns to the community that had been arguing: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Why do you deny the revelations of Allah”***. Here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the command is addressed to the messenger and that the question is a reproach, and al-Ālūsī notes that the address names the people by the very quality that should have made the denial impossible, which is their possession of a book.

Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the revelations named here are the message and the Book, and that the denial of them was the denial of what their own scriptures had announced. On the same passage, Al-Saʿdī draws the sūrah's argument to this point out, since the proofs had been set out before them in the preceding passage and the denial is now answered by a question rather than by a further proof. The shape of the sentence leaves the person addressed to answer for himself.

**THE WITNESS NAMED IN THE SAME BREATH**

The question is closed with ***“when Allah is a Witness to what you do?”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the witness covers the concealment of the truth and the acts that went with it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the added note that the witness will requite them for it. The sentence places the knowledge of God beside the denial, and the two cannot be held apart.

With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the passage as the rebuke of a people who knew, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence of the sūrah out at this point, since the questions of this passage stand near the end of a long argument and their shape is that of a final call. Since the witness is named in the present tense, the sentence does not speak of a future reckoning alone and the denial is placed under observation as it happens. What follows for the reader is a lesson about the difference between a hidden doubt and an open refusal.

**WHY THE QUESTION IS PUT TO THEM**

The verse is one of a pair of questions, and the second follows with a sharper description. Following the same line, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as a reproach for the denial and for the blocking of the way, and the two faults are dealt with in the order in which they damage the community. The first is a matter between a person and his own knowledge, and the second is a matter between a person and the people around him.

Think of a clerk who is told that the owner of the office has been standing behind him all afternoon, and who continues to mark the false entries in the ledger. The observation is the whole of the answer, because nothing else needs to be said to a man already seen. The same instrument is used here, and the next question turns from a private denial to a public obstruction.


---


## Verse 3:99

> Say, “O People of the Book! Why do you turn the believers away from the Way of Allah—striving to make it ˹appear˺ crooked, while you are witnesses ˹to its truth˺? And Allah is never unaware of what you do.”

**THE SECOND QUESTION AND ITS SUBJECT**

The address continues with a public fault: ***“Say, “O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Why do you turn the believers away from the Way of Allah”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the turning away was done by diverting the believers from the religion of God, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a way that leads to God. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the verse's occasion, which included the invitation of a group to their own religion.

On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that this second question is another reproach put to the same community, and Ibn ʿAbbās records that the passage came down about those who invited ʿAmmār and his companions. For his part, Al-Saʿdī keeps the two verses as one rebuke delivered twice, once for the denial and once for the obstruction. The sentence is aimed at a fault that reaches other people, and the damage it names is measured in the faith of those who were turned.

**THE CROOKED THAT IS SOUGHT**

The fault is then described: ***“striving to make it ˹appear˺ crooked”***. On this point, Al-Jalālayn explains the expression as a state of leaning away from the truth, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a way that is presented as bent. The expression describes the picture the obstructors wanted the believers to see rather than a change in the road itself, so the sentence places the distortion in the eye of the person being led. The order of the two questions matters, because the private denial is answered first and the public obstruction second, and the community is shown which of the two faults does it more damage.

The next clause states the knowledge of the people doing it: ***“while you are witnesses ˹to its truth˺?”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the witnesses knew the way to be in their own scriptures, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the note that the upright religion is Islam as their book states. Since the witnesses are named in the same sentence as the obstruction, the reader is shown a fault done in full knowledge, and the third element of the question is the one that removes any claim of mistake.

**THE CLOSING WARNING**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah is never unaware of what you do.””***. Here, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the sentence as the statement that no act of the obstructors is hidden, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading of a knowledge that leaves nothing outside it. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sūrah's sequence out here, since the two questions are followed at once by an address to the believers themselves, and the warning serves both audiences at once.

Think of a ferryman who is paid to keep travelers away from a bridge and who tells each one that the bridge is broken. The travelers lose the crossing and the ferryman keeps the fare, and the verse reports the same trade in the coin of the hereafter. What follows is a warning addressed to the believers themselves, because the community being led astray is warned about the hand that leads.


---


## Verse 3:100

> O believers! If you were to yield to a group of those who were given the Scripture, they would turn you back from belief to disbelief

**THE WARNING GIVEN TO THE BELIEVERS**

The address changes hands: ***“O believers! If you were to yield to a group of those”*** and ***“who were given the Scripture, they would turn you back from belief to disbelief”***. On the same passage, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the occasion, which was a dispute between the Aws and the Khazraj that one of the People of the Book fanned back into flame, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account with the man named among the reports. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report in full and adds that the two tribes had come to the edge of fighting one another.

With this in view, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the warning is a caution delivered to the believers, and al-Saʿdī draws the sūrah's turn out, since the argument with the People of the Book is followed by a warning to the community about the effect of that argument on its own unity. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense that obedience to such a group returns a person to disbelief after belief. The sentence is therefore addressed to the people who could yet be turned, and the outcome is named before the step is taken.

**WHY OBEDIENCE IS THE DANGER**

The fault named in the sentence is obedience rather than debate, and the choice of the expression is deliberate, since an argument can be answered while a following cannot. In the account given there, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the obedience is what leads the believers back, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading in his treatment of the passage. The verse therefore places the danger in a habit of deference rather than in the strength of the other party's case.

The report of the two tribes gives the sentence a concrete setting, because the quarrel it describes began with words and ended at the point of weapons. Reading the sentence this way, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sūrah's method out and notes that the warnings given to the community are frequently built on its own history, so that the lesson arrives with faces attached. Since the community had been rescued from that quarrel once, the verse tells it not to walk back into it, and the warning is stated with the outcome attached.

**THE LOSS NAMED AT THE END**

The sentence ends with the return itself: belief is left, and disbelief follows. On the reading followed here, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as one of the warnings that show the believers the traps laid for them, and the closing words name the whole distance in three words. For his part, Al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of a return that undoes what a person had received, and the sentence does not need a threat after it because the loss is its own weight.

Think of a caravan that is warned not to walk behind a guide who knows only the old road, since the road has moved and the sand covers the wells. The warning is not about the guide's honesty but about the outcome of following him, and the verse warns in the same terms. What follows is the question of how a community in that position could disbelieve at all, with the messenger standing among it.


---


## Verse 3:101

> How can you disbelieve when Allah’s revelations are recited to you and His Messenger is in your midst? Whoever holds firmly to Allah is surely guided to the Straight Path

**THE QUESTION OF AMAZEMENT**

The warning is followed by a question: ***“How can you disbelieve”***. On this point, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading that the question is put in the form of wonder and reproach, and al-Baghawī keeps the same sense of a question that asks how such a thing could happen at all. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the question concerns disbelief in God and is asked by way of astonishment. The sentence therefore treats the turning as something that does not fit its own surroundings.

The surroundings are then named: ***“when Allah’s revelations are recited to you”*** and ***“and His Messenger is in your midst?”***. Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the recitation and the presence are the two supports that a community could ask for, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same two elements. Since the Book is being read aloud and the messenger is standing among them, the community is placed in the strongest position a community can occupy, and the question measures the danger of leaving it.

**THE FIRMNESS THAT IS THE ANSWER**

The sentence then supplies the answer in the form of a promise: ***“Whoever holds firmly to Allah is surely guided to the Straight Path”***. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the holding fast is adherence to the religion of God and to His Book, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the instruction given to the believers after the warning. On the same passage, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of a person who clings to God and is guided to a straight path, and the path is described as firm and clear.

The two halves of the verse belong together, since the question names the danger of being turned and the promise names the way to hold a position. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out of the pair and notes that the community is given a practice rather than a warning alone. With this in view, Ibn Kathīr keeps the same sense of a person who relies on God and is guided, and the reader is shown a promise attached to an act that can be done at any hour.

**WHY THE QUESTION IS STILL ASKED**

The question is addressed to a community that had the Book and the messenger, and it is repeated in the sūrah of every generation by the reading. Following the same line, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sentence speaks to the believers of the first community, and the same sentence meets a reader who has the text in his hands now. Since the recitation continues after the messenger's lifetime, the support named in this verse has not been withdrawn, and the promise attached to the same support has not been withdrawn either.

Think of a student in a classroom where the teacher is explaining the lesson at the front of the room and a classmate begins to sell notes that disagree with the board. The question of how the student could follow the notes writes itself, and the verse asks the same question of a community. What follows is an address that names the duty owed to God and the state in which a person should meet his end.


---


## Verse 3:102

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves, and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺

**THE DUTY AND ITS MEASURE**

The address opens with a command: ***“O believers! Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves”***. In the account given there, Al-Jalālayn keeps the explanation of the duty from the early scholars, which is that God be obeyed and not disobeyed, thanked and not met with ingratitude, remembered and not forgotten. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ālūsī keeps the repetition of the address as an honour given to the believers, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the report of the dispute between the Aws and the Khazraj that stood behind the passage, since the two tribes had begun to boast of the killings of the days before Islam.

The measure named in the sentence is the part that a reader must weigh, since the duty answered here is the duty as it is owed rather than the duty as it is convenient. On the reading followed here, Al-Baghawī keeps the account of the companions who asked who could carry such a duty, and al-Jalālayn records that the command was followed by the verse of the second chapter which asks for the duty within a person's capacity. So the sentences are read together, and the first sets the standard while the second measures the burden.

**THE END THAT IS ASKED FOR**

The command is completed with ***“and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of a death that finds a person a believer, and al-Saʿdī keeps the two commands together as the substance of what the community owes its Lord. Since death arrives at a time that no one chooses, the instruction is really about the state a person keeps until then, and the sentence asks the reader to arrange his life around his last hour.

On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets the two principles of this passage out for the community, the duty to God and the unity of the believers, and notes that the second follows from the first. In the same reading, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the plain sense of the two commands, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the readings attached to them. The verse therefore gives the community a rule that reaches every hour of a life without naming any hour in particular.

**WHY THE ORDER MATTERS**

The sentence puts the mindfulness first and the manner of death second, and the order is the instruction itself, because a person's end is shaped by what he does with his days. Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a state of surrender as the description of a Muslim, and the expression used here carries the same sense that the sūrah has been giving it since the passage about Abraham. The reader is shown that the community's name and the community's practice are the same thing.

Think of a mason who is told to keep his tools in order every evening and to be found with them in his hands whenever the inspector comes, though no one knows the day. The order of the two instructions is the whole lesson, and the verse arranges its two commands in the same way. What follows is the second principle of the passage, which joins the community to its Lord by a rope and forbids it to divide.


---


## Verse 3:103

> And hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not be divided. Remember Allah’s favour upon you when you were enemies, then He united your hearts, so you—by His grace—became brothers. And you were at the brink of a fiery pit and He saved you from it. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so that you may be ˹rightly˺ guided

**THE ROPE AND THE PROHIBITION OF DIVISION**

The second principle is stated as a command with an image: ***“And hold firmly to the rope of Allah”*** and ***“and do not be divided. Remember Allah’s favour upon you”***. Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading, carried on a sound chain from Ibn Masʿūd, that the rope is the Book of God, and al-Baghawī keeps the sense of a rope as the way by which a person reaches what he seeks. On the same passage, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of the religion itself as the rope, with the command to be together and not to scatter after submission.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of holding fast to the religion and the Book of God, and of a division in religion that the sentence forbids. The image gives the community a picture of what unity is made of, since a rope is held by many hands at once and each hand is fastened to the same line. With this in view, Al-Saʿdī draws the whole passage out as the thanks owed for the great favours that are then named, and the first favour is the command itself.

**THE ENMITY AND THE BROTHERHOOD**

The favours are then recalled in order: ***“when you were enemies”*** and ***“then He united your hearts”*** and ***“so you—by His grace—became brothers.”***. Following the same line, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the two tribes of the Anṣār, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same account of enmity before Islam and friendship afterwards. In the account given there, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the hearts were joined by the religion, and the joining is named as a work of God rather than a work of the community.

The passage then adds a second figure: ***“And you were at the brink of a fiery pit”*** and ***“and He saved you from it. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you”***. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Saʿdī keeps the reading of a people who stood at the edge of the fire and were drawn back, and the two favours together describe a rescue in two parts, one from a quarrel and one from a destination. Since the community is asked to remember rather than to argue, the sentence gives it a history with faces in it.

**THE CLEARING AND THE GUIDANCE**

The sentence closes with the purpose of the explanation: ***“so that you may be ˹rightly˺ guided”***. On the reading followed here, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps this verse as the second of the two principles given to the community, and the guidance named at the end is the fruit of the two commands. For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the reading that unity follows the rope of God, and what division cost the earlier communities has been shown in the passages before this one. On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the rope and the division, and the whole passage is read as an account of what the community owes for what it was given.

Think of two families that quarreled for a generation over a boundary stone, until a fire swept the valley and both were carried out of it by the same neighbor. The stone is not worth a second quarrel, and the verse reminds the community of the fire before it mentions the stone. What follows is an instruction to keep a group among them calling to good and forbidding evil, so that the gratitude takes a shape in the community.


---


## Verse 3:104

> Let there be a group among you who call ˹others˺ to goodness, encourage what is good, and forbid what is evil—it is they who will be successful

**THE GROUP AND ITS WORK**

The gratitude asked for in the previous verse is given a form: ***“Let there be a group among you”*** and ***“who call ˹others˺ to goodness, encourage what is good”***. In the same reading, Al-Baghawī reads the clause about the group as a command to be such a community rather than as a part taken from it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the command is a collective duty, since not every person in a community is fitted for the work. Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense, and the work is named in three parts.

Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the call is to reconciliation and to what is right, and that the enjoining concerns the oneness of God and the following of the messenger. The three parts are arranged from the general to the particular, since the call is to goodness itself, and the two commands that follow name the good and the wrong in their places. On the same passage, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage among the instructions given to the community for its own well-being.

**THE COMMAND THAT KEEPS THE COMMUNITY**

The instruction continues with ***“and forbid what is evil”*** and closes with ***“it is they who will be successful”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the passage on inviting to good and forbidding wrong, and al-Jalālayn notes that the successful ones are those who do this work. The promise is attached to the group rather than to the community as a whole, and the sentence therefore gives the work a place in the community and a reward of its own.

With this in view, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the two principles of the previous verses to their practical conclusion and notes that the community which has been warned about division is the community that must first keep its own house in order. Following the same line, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of a duty laid on those who are able for it, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the readings of the sentence. Since the work named here is a public one, the verse does not leave the good to private opinion.

**WHY THE WORK IS A DUTY OF SOME**

The phrase about the group has been read in two ways, and both readings serve the sentence, since a community is asked to contain the work and the work is asked of those who can do it. In the account given there, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the favour shown to the community, because a body that corrects itself does not wait for an outside hand. The verse therefore places a duty and a mercy in the same instruction, and the community that takes up the duty is the one that keeps its own house standing.

Think of a caravan that appoints two riders to watch the loads and call out when the ropes loosen, while the whole caravan benefits from the watch. The task belongs to the watchers and the safety belongs to all, and the verse arranges the community's work in the same way. What follows is a warning against the very thing the work prevents, which is a community that broke apart after it had been given clear proofs. The warning is placed after the instruction to call to good, and the placement is deliberate, since a community busy with one work has no attention left for the quarrels that broke the earlier ones.


---


## Verse 3:105

> And do not be like those who split ˹into sects˺ and differed after clear proofs had come to them. It is they who will suffer a tremendous punishment

**THE EXAMPLE THAT IS FORBIDDEN**

The instruction turns to a warning with a comparison: ***“And do not be like those”*** and ***“who split ˹into sects˺”***. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading of the great majority of the commentators that the people named are the Jews and the Christians, and he records the further reading that the verse speaks of those who introduce new doctrines in this community. On the reading followed here, Al-Ālūsī keeps both readings and notes the report of al-Ḥasan and al-Rabīʿ for the first.

For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the separation was a division in religion, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a scattering. The sentence therefore sets a boundary for the community by naming a fault it must not copy, and the fault is described in the past tense because the example had already happened. Since the previous verse had given the community a work to do, this verse gives it a road not to take.

**THE PROOFS THAT WERE ALREADY GIVEN**

The comparison continues with ***“and differed after clear proofs had come to them. It is they”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the proofs came from their own scriptures, and the difference named here is therefore not the disagreement of people who lacked information. In the same reading, Al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the two communities that separated after the proofs had arrived, and the sentence places their division at a date that makes it a refusal rather than an honest mistake.

Here, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as a warning given to the believers on the heels of the warning against following others, and the order matters, because the community is told what it must not become before it is told what its end would be. Taken in the same sense, Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading of the section, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out for a community that would receive the same proofs after them. Since the proofs were the same kind, the fault would be the same fault.

**THE PUNISHMENT NAMED AT THE CLOSE**

The sentence ends with ***“who will suffer a tremendous punishment”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the punishment is greater than anything a person could imagine, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a mighty chastisement. The words are placed after the comparison, and the placement gives the warning its weight without naming a single penalty or a single date.

Think of a village that is shown the report of the last village on the same road, which fell into quarrels after the flood and lost its bridge before the second flood arrived. The report is kept as a warning rather than as a story, and the verse keeps the example of the two communities in the same way. What follows is a passage that sets out the two ends on the Day of Judgment, with faces bright and faces gloomy, and the division of the community is answered there.


---


## Verse 3:106

> On that Day some faces will be bright while others gloomy. To the gloomy-faced it will be said, “Did you disbelieve after having believed? So taste the punishment for your disbelief.”

**THE DAY AND THE TWO FACES**

The warning against division is followed by the day on which its account is settled: ***“On that Day some faces will be bright”*** and ***“while others gloomy. To the gloomy-faced it will be said, “Did you disbelieve after having believed?”***. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the description of the Day of Resurrection, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same naming of the day, with the bright faces belonging to the believers and the dark ones to the people of the Fire. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the words are spoken to those faces by the angels in charge of the Fire.

The two colours stand for two states rather than for two complexions, and the sentence places them side by side so that the reader sees the division before he hears the charge. In the same reading, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the breadth of the image out and notes that the expression appears in several places in the Book, where the brightening is a sign of joy and the darkening a sign of terror. Since the sūrah had warned the community against splitting, the day named here is the day when the split is answered.

**THE QUESTION PUT TO THE DARK FACES**

The sentence then reports the question: ***“Did you disbelieve after having believed?”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the question is asked in rebuke, and al-Jalālayn records the report that the disbelief is set against the day on which the covenant was taken. The question therefore reaches back over a whole life, and the gap is named between what had been acknowledged and what was afterwards denied.

The report behind the passage is kept in al-Qurṭubī and al-Baghawī, where the day is described and the two companies are placed on either side. Taken in the same sense, Al-Saʿdī draws the difference between the two companies out and notes that the happiness and the misery named here are the two ends that the sūrah has been describing all along. What follows for the reader is a picture that makes the warning of the previous verse visible, because a divided community has a face on that day.

**THE TASTE OF THE PUNISHMENT**

The sentence closes with the two commands of the account: ***“So taste the punishment”*** and ***“for your disbelief.””***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the punishment follows what the person denied, and al-Ālūsī keeps the plain sense of a penalty tasted rather than merely announced. The word for taste is used because a penalty that is tasted reaches the person himself, and the sentence leaves him with the thing he spent his life refusing.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as part of the passage on the two destinies, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the rebuke delivered on that day. Think of a man who is shown a portrait of himself taken on the day of his own choice, and who is then asked to name the person in it. The verse does something of the same kind with the two faces, and it lets the reader see which company he is standing in. What follows is the other half of the account, where the bright faces are given their place.


---


## Verse 3:107

> As for the bright-faced, they will be in Allah’s mercy, where they will remain forever

**THE OTHER HALF OF THE ACCOUNT**

The sentence turns to the second company: ***“As for the bright-faced, they will be in Allah’s mercy”***. With this in view, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the bright faces belong to the believers and that the mercy named here is Paradise itself, and al-Ālūsī explains that the mercy is named where the place would be expected, so that the gift and the giver are stated in one word. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading of a place within the mercy of God.

The order of the two halves matters, because the sūrah described the dark faces first and the bright ones second. In the account given there, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading that the bright-faced are the people of obedience, and the two sentences together answer the question of the previous verse, where the charge was put to the people who had believed and then denied. Since the mercy is named before the duration, the reader is given the state and then its length.

**THE STATE THAT LASTS**

The verse closes with ***“where they will remain forever”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the people of the mercy never die and never leave it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of an abiding that has no end. The closing expression takes the sentence past every comparison, because a gift that lasts forever cannot be measured in the currency of this life.

On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws out the construction of the sentence and notes that the two halves of the passage are set against each other, one company under a charge and the other within a mercy. For his part, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the abiding is added at once to the mention of the mercy, so the reader is not left with a promise that could be read as temporary. What follows for the reader is a settled picture of the two ends, and the sūrah then returns to the text itself.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SHORT**

The verse is one of the shortest in the passage, and its brevity makes it the counterpart of a long warning. On this point, Al-Saʿdī keeps the two verses together as the description of the two companies, and the shortness of the second is a mercy in itself, since a reader who has heard the description of the dark faces is given the other side in a single line. In the same reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the same reading of the two destinies.

Think of two doors at the end of a corridor, where one is described at length by those who have passed through it and the other is simply named as home. The second door is named here, and the sentence adds that it does not close. What follows is a sentence about the revelations themselves, which states that the account just given is recited in truth.


---


## Verse 3:108

> These are Allah’s revelations We recite to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth. And Allah desires no injustice to ˹His˺ creation

**THE REVELATIONS AND THE RECITING**

The sūrah identifies what has just been read: ***“These are Allah’s revelations We recite to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth.”***. Here, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the sentence points to the verses that have come before it, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same sense of a text that is recited rather than composed. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the revelations are sent down with the angel and that the truth named here separates what is true from what is false.

On the same passage, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sentence names what has passed and what has been established, and al-Jalālayn keeps the plain sense of verses recited in truth. The sentence therefore puts a seal on the passage about the two destinies, because the account of the day is stated to be part of what is recited rather than a picture drawn by the community. Since the reciting is the act of God, the reader is told where the account comes from.

**THE JUSTICE THAT IS DENIED**

The verse then adds a statement about the judgment: ***“And Allah desires no injustice to ˹His˺ creation”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no injustice is intended toward the jinn or toward human beings, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a punishment that is never given for a crime that was not committed. With this in view, Al-Saʿdī draws the purpose of the sentence out and notes that the verses set out the distinction between the two companies and the reward prepared for each.

The sentence is placed after the account of the two faces, and the placement answers a question that the account would otherwise raise. Since the punishment was described in the previous verses, the reader is told that the sentence falls only where it is owed, and the two clauses belong to one argument. What follows for the reader is a view of the day in which nothing is arbitrary and nothing is hidden.

**WHY THE VERSE STANDS HERE**

The verse is a hinge between the passage that described the two ends and the passage that follows about the community. Following the same line, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the sentence is part of the same recitation, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that confirm the message and its justice. In the account given there, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sūrah's practice out at this point, since the great warnings in the Book are regularly followed by a statement about the justice of the One who gives them.

Think of a judge who publishes his finding and then states the law under which the finding was made, so that no one can say the court acted on a whim. The verse states the law in the same way, and it does so with the two words truth and justice. What follows is a sentence about the ownership of all that is in the heavens and the earth, and the sūrah returns from the day to the present.


---


## Verse 3:109

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺

**THE OWNERSHIP STATED PLAINLY**

The sentence states a fact that the reader has met before: ***“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens”*** and ***“and whatever is on the earth.”***. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the whole creation belongs to God in ownership and in making, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a dominion that no partner shares. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that what is in the heavens and the earth includes the created beings and the marvels within them.

The two clauses cover the two directions, above and below, so that no place is left outside the sentence. For his part, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a possession that is complete, and al-Saʿdī notes that the verse belongs to the passage that sets out the distinction between the two companies and the justice of the reckoning. Since the ownership is stated before the return, the reader is prepared for the next clause.

**THE RETURN OF THE MATTERS**

The verse then adds: ***“And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the return happens in the Hereafter, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of all matters going back to God. The two sentences form a single argument, because the One who owns everything is the One to whom everything returns, and the return is not a change of owner but a return to the owner.

In the same reading, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that confirm the message and its justice, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sūrah's sequence out here, since the statement about the justice of God is followed by a statement about His ownership. The two together close the door on the idea that any matter escapes the account, and the reader is left with a fact rather than a warning. What follows is the passage about the best community, which turns from God's ownership to the community's duty.

**WHY THE VERSE IS PLACED HERE**

The sentence is placed between the account of the two destinies and the address to the believers, and the placement serves both. Here, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the sentence stands on its own as a statement about God, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading in the commentary on this passage. Taken in the same sense, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse with the passage on the two companies, and the reader is shown that the account of the day rests on an ownership that no one disputes.

Think of a household where every room, every tool and every debt belongs to one owner, and where the accounts are read aloud each year in the same hall. The verse states the ownership before it states the reckoning, and the two statements hold each other up. What follows is the verse that names the community as the best raised for humanity, and the naming comes with the work that earned it.


---


## Verse 3:110

> You are the best community ever raised for humanity—you encourage good, forbid evil, and believe in Allah. Had the People of the Book believed, it would have been better for them. Some of them are faithful, but most are rebellious

**THE COMMUNITY AND ITS NAME**

The address returns to the believers with a verdict: ***“You are the best community ever raised”*** and ***“for humanity—you encourage good, forbid evil”***. On the same passage, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the sentence names the community as the best of peoples brought out for the benefit of humanity, and al-Saʿdī draws the reasons out, which are the three qualities that follow in the sentence. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the saying of the prophet's companion that the community is the last of the peoples and the most honourable before God.

The qualities are then named in order: the calling to good, the forbidding of evil and the belief in God. With this in view, Al-Baghawī keeps the report that the verse came down concerning named companions of the Anṣār, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the work described is the same work the previous passage had commanded. Since the community is named as the best, the sentence explains the title by its work rather than by its descent.

**THE THIRD QUALITY AND THE ORDER**

The three are closed with the belief in God, and the placement matters, because the work of the community is grounded in the faith that comes after it in the sentence. Following the same line, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sentence was revealed to establish the believers on the agreement they had reached, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the qualities named are the divine oneness and the following of the messenger. So the sentence holds a title and the work that keeps the title true.

**THE HOPE LEFT OPEN FOR THE OTHERS**

The passage then turns to the other community: ***“and believe in Allah. Had the People of the Book believed, it would have been better”*** and ***“for them. Some of them are faithful”*** and ***“but most are rebellious”***. In the account given there, Ibn Kathīr keeps the reading that the belief of the People of the Book would have been better for them, and the sentence adds the exception that keeps the verdict from covering a whole people. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Saʿdī draws the exception out and notes that the praise given to the community is not a licence against others.

On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the passage as a favour shown to the community through the qualities it was given, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the exception named in the sentence is taken up again in the verses that follow. Think of a guild that is told it keeps the finest hall in the city, and then hears that two of the neighbouring workshops also keep good work. The verse praises the community and leaves the door open for the others at the same time, and the reason is that the praise rests on the work rather than on the name. What follows is a passage about the harm the community should expect from those who do not believe.


---


## Verse 3:111

> They can never inflict harm on you, except a little annoyance. But if they meet you in battle, they will flee and they will have no helpers

**THE HARM AND ITS LIMIT**

The passage continues with an assurance: ***“They can never inflict harm on you, except a little annoyance.”***. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the harm named here is a slight injury and not a defeat, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading that the annoyance is the lying and the distortion and the slander that the community would hear. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the harm comes by the tongue, through vilifying and defaming, and that no weapon is included in the sentence.

The limit is the point of the clause, since the sentence grants the annoyance and denies the harm. In the same reading, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the exception is connected to the same kind of thing, because the annoyance is a lesser injury rather than a different category. Since the community had been told in the previous passage what its work was, this sentence tells it what to expect from those who refuse the work.

**THE OUTCOME WHEN THEY MEET THE COMMUNITY**

The sentence then states the outcome of a meeting: ***“But if they meet you in battle, they will flee”*** and ***“and they will have no helpers”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that they turn their backs in defeat and are not saved from the sword or from being taken, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a retreat with no help from any quarter. The clause about helpers is placed at the end, so that the sentence closes the last door on the other side.

Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the passage deals with the plans of the People of the Book to harm the community in its property and its persons, and that the last sentence carries the prophecy that they would not succeed. On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the address goes to the messenger and to those who hold to the same way, and the sentence reaches the reader as a promise made to a community that keeps its work. What follows is a passage that describes the state of those who do not keep it.

**WHY THE PROMISE IS STATED**

The promise is stated in the middle of a passage of warnings, and the placement gives the community a floor under its feet. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Saʿdī draws the sequence out of the passage, since the community is praised for its work and then told what its opponents can and cannot do. The sentence therefore answers the fear that a warning would otherwise produce, and it does so with two limits, one on the harm and one on the helpers.

Think of a village that is told the raiders can shout at the gate but cannot break it, and that if they come in force they will scatter before the courtyard is reached. The promise is specific about what is possible, and the verse is specific in the same way. What follows is a passage about the disgrace that covers the other side wherever they go, and about the reasons for it.


---


## Verse 3:112

> They will be stricken with disgrace wherever they go, unless they are protected by a covenant with Allah or a treaty with the people. They have invited the displeasure of Allah and have been branded with misery for rejecting Allah’s revelations and murdering ˹His˺ prophets unjustly. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**THE DISGRACE AND THE TWO ROPES**

The passage describes the state of the other side: ***“They will be stricken with disgrace wherever they go”*** and ***“unless they are protected by a covenant with Allah”*** and ***“or a treaty with the people.”***. With this in view, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading that the disgrace falls on the Jews, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a humiliation that reaches life, property and family. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the humiliation is the tax laid upon them and that they cannot stand against the believers.

The exception is then stated with the image of two ropes, one from God and one from the people. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the first is the faith and the second is a pledge taken with the rulers, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of a covenant of security granted with the payment that goes with it. So the sentence gives the only two ways in which the disgrace is lifted, and neither of them is a strength of their own.

**THE ANGER AND THE MISERY**

The sentence then names the causes: ***“They have invited the displeasure of Allah”*** and ***“and have been branded with misery”*** and ***“for rejecting Allah’s revelations”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the anger is deserved and that the misery is a garment laid upon them, and al-Saʿdī keeps the reading that the disgrace described here is a state that follows them wherever they are found.

The causes continue: ***“and murdering ˹His˺ prophets unjustly. This is ˹a fair reward˺”*** and ***“for their disobedience and violations”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the passage on the abasement cast upon them, and the two causes named at the close explain the state described at the beginning. Since the murder of the prophets is named beside the rejection of the revelations, the sentence shows a refusal that went from a word to a hand.

**WHY THE PASSAGE EXPLAINS ITSELF**

The passage is unusual in that it supplies its own reason at the end, and the reason comes after the description so that the reader weighs the state before he reads the cause. For his part, Al-Ālūsī keeps the report of the earlier discussion of the same subject in the second chapter, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the meaning of the disgrace and the anger has been treated there at length. The sentence therefore closes the description of the other side with a statement about justice rather than about ancestry.

Think of a family that lost its market stalls after the father of the house broke a treaty, and the town's chronicle that names the treaty rather than the family. The deeds are named here and the descent is left out, and the reader is left with the deeds. What follows is the exception that keeps the whole passage from covering a people, since the next verse states that they are not all alike.


---


## Verse 3:113

> Yet they are not all alike: there are some among the People of the Book who are upright, who recite Allah’s revelations throughout the night, prostrating ˹in prayer˺

**THE EXCEPTION THAT OPENS THE PASSAGE**

The sūrah corrects the sweep of the previous description: ***“Yet they are not all alike”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that those among the People of the Book who believed are not like those who did not, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of an equality denied. In the same reading, Al-Ālūsī keeps the report of Ibn Isḥāq and the early collectors that the verse came down when the companions of ʿAbdallāh b. Salām embraced the faith, and the exception is stated before the group is named.

The group is then named: ***“there are some among the People of the Book”*** and ***“who are upright, who recite Allah’s revelations throughout the night, prostrating ˹in prayer˺”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the upright community holds to the oneness of God and recites the revelations in the hours of the night, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a group that keeps the truth and stands in prayer.

**THE QUALITIES THAT MARK THEM**

The sentence names three qualities in one breath, a standing on the truth, a reciting of the revelations, and a prostration in prayer. Taken in the same sense, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading that the group described is composed of the people of obedience, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the exception refers to the People of the Book as a body, so that the passage does not condemn a people for its majority. On the same passage, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the purpose out and notes that the verse gives a complementary profile of those among them who believed.

Since the night is named as the time of their reciting, the sentence describes a devotion that is not performed for an audience. The qualities are therefore evidence of the faith rather than its substitute, and the reader is shown what the exception looks like in practice. What follows for the reader is a sentence that adds the belief and the work of the same group.

**WHY THE CORRECTION IS NEEDED**

The correction is needed because the passage before it had described a community under disgrace, and a reader could carry that description too far. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Saʿdī keeps the two passages together, with the deviators named first and the upright named after them, and the order teaches the reader to judge by the deed. The verse therefore protects the honest from the verdict passed on the dishonest, and it does so in one sentence.

Think of a market in which two stalls are shut for cheating, and a notice that states that the other forty stalls are not to be judged by those two. The notice corrects the impression without defending the offence, and the verse corrects the impression in the same way. What follows is a verse that names the belief, the work and the reward of the group described here.


---


## Verse 3:114

> They believe in Allah and the Last Day, encourage good and forbid evil, and race with one another in doing good. They are ˹truly˺ among the righteous

**THE BELIEF AND THE WORK**

The sentence describes the group further: ***“They believe in Allah”*** and ***“and the Last Day, encourage good”*** and ***“and forbid evil, and race with one another in doing good. They are ˹truly˺ among the righteous”***. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the belief covers God, the scriptures of the messengers and the resurrection, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a belief in God and in the Last Day joined to the work of ordering and forbidding.

The qualities repeat the pattern of the description given to the believers a few verses earlier, and the repetition is the point, since the same work is recognised wherever it appears. Following the same line, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sentence is a further description of the upright community, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the account of the upright among the People of the Book. The verse therefore measures the group by the work the community itself was given.

**THE RACING AND THE REWARD**

The third work is stated with the image of a race, and the image carries the sense of speed rather than of mere performance. In the account given there, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the description of the group that believes and does good, and al-Jalālayn keeps the sense of rivals in good works. The sentence then closes with a word of assurance: ***“They are ˹truly˺ among the righteous”***.

Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the exception and its description belong to the same passage on the People of the Book, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the profile of this group alongside the profile of the community that was named as the best. Since the sentence ends with a rank rather than a promise, the reader is shown where such people stand rather than what they receive. What follows is the sentence that names the reward and the knowledge that keeps it.

**WHY THE RACE IS NAMED**

The race is named last among the works, and the placement is deliberate, because the sentence has moved from belief to conduct and from conduct to zeal. On the reading followed here, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading that the good works are the ones commanded by the messenger, and the image of the race gives the reader a picture of a group that does not need to be pushed. The verse therefore answers the question of what the exception looks like when it is active.

Think of two men who hear the same call to repair the town wall and who both reach for the same stone, one to carry it and one to lay it. The work is the same and the speed is the measure, and the sentence uses the same measure for the group it describes. What follows is a short verse about the reward of any good they do and about the knowledge of the One who keeps it.


---


## Verse 3:115

> They will never be denied the reward for any good they have done. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of those mindful ˹of Him˺

**THE REWARD THAT IS NOT WITHHELD**

The passage closes with a promise: ***“They will never be denied the reward”*** and ***“for any good they have done.”***. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that no good deed of theirs is refused, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a reward that is never denied. The sentence is stated in the negative so that no amount of good is left outside it, and the smallest work of the group described in the previous verses is included.

On this point, Ibn Kathīr keeps the sentence as the closing of the passage on the People of the Book who believed, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse with the account of the upright group. Since the previous verse had named the work and the rank, this sentence answers the question of what happens to the work, and the answer is given without a condition. What follows for the reader is a rule that applies to the group described and to every person who does the same work.

**THE KNOWLEDGE THAT KEEPS THE ACCOUNT**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of those mindful ˹of Him˺”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God is aware of the people who guard themselves against wrong, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of a knowledge that reaches the inward as well as the outward. The sentence is placed after the promise so that the reader knows why no good is lost, and the reason is the knowledge of the One who keeps the record.

Here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws the passage's close out and notes that the promise and the knowledge stand together as one statement, since a promise without knowledge could be lost in the crowd. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the verse as the end of the account of the upright group, and the reader is left with a sentence that covers the smallest deed. Since the mindfulness named at the close is the quality that goes with the work, the sentence names what the record is kept for.

**WHY THE PASSAGE ENDS HERE**

The passage began with the description of a people under disgrace and ends with a promise to those among them who believed, and the two ends hold the whole account. On the same passage, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence as a general assurance, and al-Baghawī keeps the same sense for the group described. The sūrah then turns to a new address, and the reader is left with the knowledge that no deed of the upright is wasted.

Think of a treasurer who is told that every coin given to the fund has been entered in the ledger by hand, and that no entry is ever erased. The promise and the record are named in one sentence, and the same pairing is made here. What follows is a passage that turns to the community with a warning against the company of those who disbelieve, and the sūrah keeps moving between the two addresses.


---


## Verse 3:116

> Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**THE WEALTH AND THE CHILDREN**

The sūrah returns to the people who refused the message and states what their possessions can do: ***“Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah. It is they”***. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the sentence denies any benefit from riches or offspring where the judgment of God is concerned, and al-Jalālayn notes why these two are named, since a person in danger ordinarily ransoms himself with his wealth or looks for help among his children.

On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the sentence concerns those who disbelieved in the message and in the Book, and he names the leaders of the Jewish community among those addressed. The sentence is placed after the passage about the upright among the People of the Book, and its function is to separate the two cases, since a text that praises one group must state what becomes of the others. What follows for the reader is a rule that applies to every owner of property and every parent.

**THE RESIDENTS OF THE FIRE**

The sentence then names the state of the people described: ***“who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that they are the dwellers of the Fire and that their stay has no end, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a permanent residence. Here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws the sentence out and notes that the benefit denied at the beginning and the residence named at the end belong to a single account of loss.

Taken in the same sense, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the grammatical structure of the sentence and the readings attached to it, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage beside the next verse as a statement about what the disbelievers' spending can and cannot do. Since the sūrah had warned the community against trusting its opponents, the sentence shows what the opponents themselves can rely on, and the answer is nothing. The reader is therefore shown the end of a whole class of hopes.

**WHY THE VERSE IS HERE**

The sentence stands between a passage about the account of the two companies and a passage about the community's own conduct, and the position gives it both functions. On the same passage, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse in its place in the sūrah, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out, since the profile of the believers among the People of the Book is followed by the account of those who did not believe. The placement keeps the two groups distinct for the reader.

Think of a merchant who has filled his warehouse against a coming flood, and who is told that the water will not be stopped by the size of the warehouse. The verse makes the same point about wealth and children, and it does so without saying that either of them is worthless in itself. What follows is a likeness that shows what becomes of the good such a person does in this world.


---


## Verse 3:117

> The good they do in this worldly life is like the harvest of an evil people struck by a bitter wind, destroying it ˹completely˺. Allah never wronged them, but they wronged themselves

**THE LIKENESS OF THE FIELD**

The sentence states a comparison: ***“The good they do in this worldly life is like the harvest of an evil people struck by a bitter wind, destroying it ˹completely˺.”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Jalālayn explains the elements of the picture, with the wind carrying an intense heat or cold and the tillage belonging to a people who wronged themselves, so that the crop is destroyed and nothing of it is left. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the picture concerns what the wrongdoers spend in their own way of life.

Following the same line, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the likeness stands as an argument against the benefit denied in the previous verse, since a field is worth the harvest and not the labour. The image is drawn from the countryside the first hearers knew, and the sentence therefore measures the labour of the disbelievers by the crop and not by the sowing. What follows for the reader is a picture of work that is finished and lost at once.

**THE WIND AND THE FIELD**

In the account given there, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the wind named in the picture is a destructive one, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the same image. The sentence then states the verdict that the image was carrying: ***“Allah never wronged them”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no injustice was done to them in the loss, and the clause removes the complaint before it is made, since a field destroyed by weather still belongs to its owner.
