The verse closes with ***“but they wronged themselves”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the wrong was done by the people to themselves, through the denial and the disobedience that emptied their work of value, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a ruin they brought on their own field. Since the two clauses stand side by side, the sentence shows a rule of justice and an act of choice in the same line.

**WHY THE IMAGE WORKS**

The image works because it separates the act from the outcome, since the sowing was real and the crop was real, and the destruction came between them. For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the passage as the account of what such spending is worth, and al-Saʿdī draws the lesson out, since the sūrah is teaching the community where to place its hope. The verse therefore answers the question of what happens to good work done without faith, and the answer is given as a field and a wind.

Think of two farmers who sow the same seed in the same month, one on ground he owns and one on ground he has taken by force, and a storm that takes both crops in one night. The storm is not the injustice, and the verse says as much about the loss of the disbelievers' work. What follows is a warning to the community about the company it keeps, and the warning names the kind of friendship it must refuse.


---


## Verse 3:118

> O believers! Do not associate closely with others who would not miss a chance to harm you. Their only desire is to see you suffer. Their prejudice has become evident from what they say—and what their hearts hide is far worse. We have made Our revelations clear to you, if only you understood

**THE WARNING AGAINST INTIMACY**

The address turns to the community with a prohibition: ***“O believers! Do not associate closely with others”***. In the commentary on the passage, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading that the prohibition concerns a trust placed in those who are not of the community, and al-Baghawī keeps the report that the verse came down about the confidences that some of the helpers placed in the Jewish settlements around Madīnah. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the believers were forbidden to reveal their secrets to such people.

The word used for the close companion is the word for the inner lining of a garment, and the image carries the meaning of the sentence, since a lining touches the body and a betrayal from that position reaches furthest. In the same vein, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the intimates in view are those outside the faith, whether Jews, Christians or hypocrites. What follows for the reader is a rule about the person a community lets closest, rather than a rule about the people it may greet.

**THE DESIRE AND THE HIDDEN HATRED**

The sentence then describes the danger: ***“who would not miss a chance to harm you. Their only desire is to see you suffer. Their prejudice has become evident from what they say”***. For the same reason, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that such people spare no effort to corrupt the community, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense of a wish to see the believers stumble. The sentence states that the harm is intended rather than accidental, and the reader is given the intention before the act.

The description continues: ***“and what their hearts hide is far worse. We have made Our revelations clear to you”***. On that reading, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the hatred which has appeared in speech is only the smaller part of what the hearts hold, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the warning that stands at the head of the whole passage about the community's dealings. Since the hidden part is named as worse, the sentence measures the danger by what cannot be seen.

**WHY THE REVELATIONS ARE NAMED**

The verse closes with the statement about the clarity of the revelations and the condition attached to understanding: ***“if only you understood”***. For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the warning against taking such people as advisors, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the setting of the passage, with the settlements around Madīnah and the old ties of neighbourhood that drew individuals together. The sentence therefore supplies the knowledge that makes the warning possible, and the understanding of the reader is what the sentence asks for.

Think of a household that hires a steward from a family that has claimed the estate for years, and a list of incidents that is handed to the owner. The list is the revelation named here, and the understanding is the owner's part of the work. What follows is a passage that describes the community's own feelings toward these people, and the description shows how unequal the affection was.


---


## Verse 3:119

> Here you are! You love them but they do not love you, and you believe in all Scriptures. When they meet you they say, “We believe.” But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “˹May you˺ die of your rage!” Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**THE UNEQUAL AFFECTION**

The sentence turns the warning around and points at the community: ***“Here you are!”*** and ***“You love them but they do not love you”***. On this point, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the address calls attention to a plain fact, and that the community loved such people for the ties of family and for a pretended friendship while the affection was not returned. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the love came from ties of marriage and of fostering.

The reason for the difference is then named: ***“and you believe in all Scriptures.”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community acknowledges every revealed book and every messenger, while the others do not acknowledge the Book of the community. Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of a faith that includes the earlier books, and the sentence therefore explains why the affection ran in one direction only. Since the community accepted what the others had, the refusal came from the other side entire.

**THE TWO FACES OF THE SAME PEOPLE**

The sentence then shows the behaviour that accompanied the hidden feeling: ***“When they meet you they say, “We believe.””*** and ***“But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “˹May you˺ die of your rage!””***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the declaration was made in public and that the rage showed once the company parted, and the biting of the fingertips is kept by al-Jalālayn as the picture of a fury that has nowhere to go.

The command then turns the rage back on the person who holds it, and the sentence closes the scene with a verdict delivered in the imperative. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the words spoken in rebuke, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reports attached to the passage. Since the sentence has shown the public declaration and the private fury in one breath, the reader is shown a company whose speech and whose heart have no relation.

**THE KNOWLEDGE THAT CLOSES THE VERSE**

The verse ends with ***“Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart”***. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God knows what the hearts conceal, and the sentence answers the picture of a secret that had been discovered, since the secret was never secret to the One who made the heart. Following the same line, Ibn Kathīr keeps the passage as part of the warning against trusting such company, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the strange position of a community that loves where it is not loved.

Think of a man who lends his tools to a neighbour and later hears him complaining in the next room. The verse reports the same situation, and it does so before the neighbour is named, because the reader is meant to recognise the pattern. What follows is a passage about the reactions of such people to the fortunes of the community, and the passage answers the question of how much harm they can do.


---


## Verse 3:120

> When you ˹believers˺ are touched with good, they grieve; but when you are afflicted with evil, they rejoice. ˹Yet,˺ if you are patient and mindful ˹of Allah˺, their schemes will not harm you in the least. Surely Allah is Fully Aware of what they do

**THE TWO REACTIONS**

The passage describes the reaction of the people just mentioned to the news of the community: ***“When you ˹believers˺ are touched with good, they grieve”*** and ***“but when you are afflicted with evil, they rejoice. ˹Yet,˺”***. In the account given there, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the touch of good is a blessing and the evil is a misfortune, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense with the two reactions attached to each. The sentence therefore measures the other party by its feelings rather than by its words.

The two reactions are placed side by side so that the reader sees a single disposition in two shapes, since grief at another's good and joy at another's harm are one wish. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the warning against taking such people into confidence, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a company whose feelings run against the community. Since the sūrah has already shown what these people say in public, this sentence shows what they feel in private.

**THE CONDITION THAT MAKES THE HARM HARMLESS**

The sentence then states the condition under which the schemes come to nothing: ***“if you are patient”*** and ***“and mindful ˹of Allah˺, their schemes will not harm you in the least. Surely Allah is Fully Aware of what they do”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the two qualities named here are the same two that the sūrah has been asking of the community, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The promise is therefore attached to conduct rather than to strength.

For his part, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the warning that goes before the account of the battle of Uḥud, and the reader is shown what the community was asked to bring to that day. In the commentary on the passage, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the setback came from the failure to keep these two rules fully, and the verse states the rule before the account of the failure. The sentence therefore gives the community both its safety and its test in one line.

**WHY THE VERSE IS PLACED HERE**

The verse closes the warning with a promise, and the promise is placed before the narrative of the battle that follows. Along the same lines, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as part of the passage on the schemes of the opponents, and the closing words name the knowledge that keeps the account of every plan. The reader is therefore prepared to read the narrative that follows, since he has been told what the outcome depends on.

Think of a household that is warned about a neighbour who rejoices at every loss and is told that the door will hold if the shutters are closed at night. The promise is specific and the condition is stated, and the verse makes the same promise in the same way. What follows is a passage that takes the reader to the morning of the battle, when the messenger positioned the believers on the field.


---


## Verse 3:121

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you left your home in the early morning to position the believers in the battlefield. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**THE MORNING THE LINE WAS DRAWN**

The warning is followed by a scene: ***“˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you left your home in the early morning to position the believers in the battlefield.”***. In the same vein, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the day, which is the day of Uḥud, and notes that the messenger set out with about a thousand men while the opposing force numbered three thousand. For the same reason, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the departure was from Madīnah and that the positioning was the assignment of the believers to their places.

On that reading, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the opening word is joined to an implied command to remember, and the scene is therefore laid before the reader as a matter to be recalled rather than as a report to be filed. For his part, Al-Saʿdī keeps the account of the departure and of the enemy force that had come near the mountain. Since the placing of the troops is named rather than the fighting, the reader is shown the messenger at work before the battle begins.

**THE TWO NAMES AT THE CLOSE**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the hearing covers what was said on the field and the knowledge covers what was hidden in the hearts of the men who were placed, and the two names are placed after the scene for that reason. In the same reading, Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a God who hears the speech and knows the circumstances of the people involved.

Here, Ibn Kathīr keeps the verse at the head of the passage on Uḥud, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the day and its setting. The naming of the two attributes after a scene of preparation tells the reader that the account which follows is not a report of chance, since the One who hears and knows was present at the placing of every man. What follows for the reader is a promise of victory that is tied to the conduct of the community.

**WHY THE SCENE IS RECALLED**

The scene is recalled at this point because the passage that follows deals with a setback, and the reader must be taken back to the moment before it happened. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out, since the verses about patience and mindfulness are followed by the account of the day on which both were tested. On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the departure as a scene that the messenger himself is asked to remember.

Think of a general who walks the line at dawn and stops at each post, and a chronicler who begins the story of the battle with that walk rather than with the first arrow. The verse begins in the same place, and it adds the two names of the One who watched the walk. What follows is a verse about the two groups that nearly gave way and about the trust that steadied them.


---


## Verse 3:122

> ˹Remember˺ when two groups among you ˹believers˺ were about to cower, then Allah reassured them. So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**THE TWO GROUPS THAT NEARLY GAVE WAY**

The scene continues with a detail from the line: ***“˹Remember˺ when two groups among you ˹believers˺ were about to cower”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Jalālayn names the two groups as the clans of Salimah and Ḥārithah, the two flanks of the army, and records that they were close to withdrawing after the hypocrite leader and his companions had turned back. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading and notes that the two tribes almost shrank from the fighting on the day of Uḥud.

The verse then states what held them: ***“then Allah reassured them.”***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God preserved them and prevented them from leaving, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the two clauses of the verse are joined, so that the intention and the protection are stated in one breath. The sentence therefore records a decision that was never taken, and the record is kept so that the reader knows how near the line came to breaking.

**THE TRUST THAT IS ASKED FOR**

The verse closes with an instruction: ***“So in Allah let the believers put their trust”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the believers ought to place their reliance on God, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the same sense of a trust that is owed by the community. The sentence turns the account into a lesson, since the two groups that nearly left were the people who had not yet placed their weight anywhere else.

Reading the sentence this way, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as part of the account of Uḥud and of the state of the army on that day, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the passage moves from the placing of the troops to the state of the hearts behind them. The verse therefore shows two things at once, a danger that was real and a protection that was decided before it arrived. What follows for the reader is a promise of a help that no one should need to be ashamed of.

**WHY THE FAILING IS RECORDED**

The failing is recorded although it did not happen, and the record is a mercy to the community, because it shows that the fear itself was not a sin. On the reading followed here, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the two groups that nearly lost heart, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two clans. The reader is therefore taught that the danger lies in the act and not in the feeling.

Think of two soldiers who are told that the two men beside them hesitated at the same moment they did, and who then hear that the line held and the commander knew. The report steadies the whole company, and the verse steadies its readers with the same news. What follows is a reminder of the earlier victory, which is placed beside the hardness of the present day.


---


## Verse 3:123

> Indeed, Allah made you victorious at Badr when you were ˹vastly˺ outnumbered. So be mindful of Allah, perhaps you will be grateful

**THE VICTORY THAT WAS GIVEN**

The passage turns from the near-failing to an earlier deliverance: ***“Indeed, Allah made you victorious at Badr”*** and ***“when you were ˹vastly˺ outnumbered.”***. For his part, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the day and notes the place between Mecca and Madīnah, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the location. In the commentary on the passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the small number of the community on that day, with the three hundred and thirteen men named in the reports.

The contrast between the two clauses is the substance of the sentence, since the victory is named first and the small number second, so that the reader sees the gift before he sees the odds. Along the same lines, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the verse is a demonstration of what follows from patience and mindfulness, since the previous passage had named those two qualities. The sentence therefore functions as evidence rather than as history.

**THE REMINDER AND ITS PURPOSE**

The verse closes with an instruction and a hope: ***“So be mindful of Allah, perhaps you will be grateful”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the mindfulness asked for is the same duty that the community had been given throughout the sūrah, and he notes the context of warfare and obedience to the leader placed over them. For the same reason, Al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the duty as the means by which gratitude is reached.

On that reading, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the reminder of the favour given at Badr, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out, since the present verses set the two battles beside each other so that the community can compare them. The sentence therefore gives the reader a lesson in both directions at once, since the earlier day shows what the duty produces and the later day shows what happens when it is left. What follows for the reader is a passage about the help that was promised in the middle of the present danger.

**WHY THE TWO DAYS ARE JOINED**

The two days are joined because the community needed to remember one in order to face the other, and the sūrah places them together for that purpose. For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the reminder of Badr, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the day and its numbers. On this point, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as a reminder addressed to the community that had seen both.

Think of a household that has once been saved from a flood by closing a single gate, and that is shown the gate again when the water rises a second time. The reminder is practical rather than sentimental, and the verse reminds the community in the same way. What follows is the promise of angels sent down, which was given to the same community on the same day.


---


## Verse 3:124

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you said to the believers, “Is it not enough that your Lord will send down a reinforcement of three thousand angels for your aid?”

**THE WORDS SPOKEN TO THE BELIEVERS**

The passage recalls what the messenger said on the day: ***“˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you said to the believers, “Is it not enough”***. In the same reading, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the promise given as reassurance, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the question was addressed to the community at a moment of need. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the words were spoken on the day of Uḥud and that the question concerned the help promised against the enemy.

The promise is then stated: ***“that your Lord will send down a reinforcement of three thousand angels”*** and ***“for your aid?”***. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the angels were sent down from heaven to help the community, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the number. On the same passage, Al-Jalālayn adds the note that the reinforcement grew by stages, with the thousand of the earlier passage and the three thousand named here and the five thousand named in the next verse.

**THE QUESTION THAT CARRIES A PROMISE**

The shape of the sentence is a question whose answer is not in doubt, and the shape is the mercy of the verse, because a promise given as a question leaves the hearer to supply the answer. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the pledge given to the believers, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the favours that were granted on the day. The community is therefore addressed with its own confidence rather than with a command.

With this in view, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the wisdom of the passage out and notes that the help was sent for the sake of the hearts of the believers, so that the promise is as much about steadiness as about the outcome of a battle. Since the next verse states the conditions attached to the help, the two sentences are read as one promise with two sides. What follows for the reader is the condition under which the promise was to be fulfilled.

**WHY THE NUMBER IS NAMED**

The number is named so that the community could weigh the help against the enemy force it had counted, and the naming is therefore part of the reassurance. Following the same line, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse in the passage on Uḥud, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the help as an act of God rather than as a force the community could command. The reader is shown a host that is not counted in the community's own ledger.

Think of a village told at night that three thousand riders are on the road to help before morning, and a watchman who counts them as they arrive. The promise is specific enough to be trusted and large enough to be remembered, and the verse gives a promise of the same kind. What follows is the verse that names firmness and mindfulness as the conditions of the help.


---


## Verse 3:125

> Most certainly, if you ˹believers˺ are firm and mindful ˹of Allah˺ and the enemy launches a sudden attack on you, Allah will reinforce you with five thousand angels designated ˹for battle˺

**THE CONDITIONS OF THE HELP**

The promise is completed with its conditions: ***“Most certainly, if you ˹believers˺ are firm”*** and ***“and mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the firmness named here is patience in the fighting and that the mindfulness is the keeping of the command and the avoidance of disobedience, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the two qualities. The sentence therefore makes the help conditional on a state of the community rather than on the size of the enemy.

The answer to the earlier question is then given with the affirmation that opens the verse, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the word is an affirmation of what the previous question proposed. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Baghawī keeps the report of the promise in the passage on Uḥud, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the enemy in view was the force coming from the direction of Mecca. Since the promise is stated with the conditions attached, the reader is shown what the community held in its own hands.

**THE ATTACK AND THE REINFORCEMENT**

The verse then names the danger and the help in a single sentence: ***“and the enemy launches a sudden attack on you, Allah will reinforce you with five thousand angels designated ˹for battle˺”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the angels were sent with marks by which they were known, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of a host sent for the purpose. The number has risen from the earlier pledge, and the increase is kept by the commentators as one of the signs of a promise made larger as the danger grew.

For his part, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the account of the help given to the community in its battles, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the purpose out, since the help was sent so that the believers would know that their strength was not in their own numbers. The sentence therefore closes the promise with a picture that no community could arrange for itself. What follows for the reader is a verse that states the purpose of the help that was sent.

**WHY THE HELP IS NAMED IN FULL**

The help is named in full so that the account of Uḥud carries both sides, the failure of the community and the support it was offered, and the two together make the lesson complete. In the commentary on the passage, Ibn Kathīr keeps the passage as the promise of the angels, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the numbers. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of a help that would come only as God willed it.

Think of a guard post that is promised a relief column on the condition that the watch is kept; the column is real and the condition is real, and neither cancels the other. The verse states both in one sentence, and the reader is left with the work he can do. What follows is a verse that explains why the help was given in the form of a promise rather than in the form of a visible army.


---


## Verse 3:126

> Allah ordained this ˹reinforcement˺ only as good news for you and reassurance for your hearts. And victory comes only from Allah—the Almighty, All-Wise—

**THE PURPOSE OF THE HELP**

The promise of the angels is followed by its reason: ***“Allah ordained this ˹reinforcement˺ only as good news”*** and ***“for you and reassurance”*** and ***“for your hearts.”***. For his part, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the help was made a message of good news and a means of steadying the hearts, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the pronoun and what it refers to, which is the help that had been promised. The sentence therefore names the work the promise was meant to do, and the work was done in the hearts before it was done on the field.

On this point, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sending of the angels was not a display of numbers, and the clause about the hearts shows where the weakness had been. The community had been shaken by the size of the opposing force, and the promise was sent to answer that fear, so the sentence moves the reader from the count of the enemy to the state of the heart. What follows for the reader is the statement that settles where the victory actually came from.

**THE VICTORY AND ITS SOURCE**

The sentence then states the source of the outcome: ***“And victory comes only from Allah”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the help and the victory belong to God, and al-Baghawī keeps the same sense in the passage on the battle. The clause is placed after the promise of the angels, so that the reader does not rest on the help that was named and look past the One who sent it.

The verse closes with the two names: ***“the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the first as the power that is not resisted and the second as the wisdom that arranges the outcome of a battle, and the pair is kept by al-Ṭabarī in the same reading. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the purpose of the passage out and notes that the wisdom stands behind the timing of the help as much as behind the victory itself. Since the two names come after the promise, the reader is shown where both the strength and the arrangement belong.

**WHY THE LESSON IS STATED**

The lesson is stated because the community would soon need it on a day that ended differently, and the sūrah prepares the reader for both outcomes. On the same passage, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the help sent to the believers, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the favours granted to the community in its battles. The sentence therefore teaches the reader to read success and setback in the same frame.

Think of a village that is told a relief column is coming, and then told that the arrival of the column is not what saves the village, since the road it takes is chosen by the one who sends it. The verse makes the same distinction, and it makes it before the community lost a day. What follows is a verse that states one purpose the help served, which was the scattering of a hostile force.


---


## Verse 3:127

> to destroy a group of the disbelievers and humble the rest, causing them to withdraw in disappointment

**THE PURPOSE BEHIND THE HELP**

The sentence continues the account of the help with its effect on the other side: ***“to destroy a group of the disbelievers”*** and ***“and humble the rest, causing them to withdraw in disappointment”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the help was sent against the enemy force that had attacked, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the passage as part of the account of the day. The sentence therefore completes the purpose of the help named in the verse before it.

The verse belongs to the previous sentence grammatically and the two are read together, so that the help, its purpose and its outcome form one statement. With this in view, Al-Baghawī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and the cutting of a group and the humbling of the rest describe a force that was broken in two ways. Since the community had been told that the victory came from God, this sentence shows what the victory did to the army that came against it.

**THE WITHDRAWAL IN DISAPPOINTMENT**

The verse closes with the state of the survivors: they turn back with their aim unfulfilled. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the humbling left them without the victory they had come for, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a return that carries nothing home. The word used at the close describes a person who does not reach what he set out for, and the army's march home is therefore written as a loss rather than a retreat.

In the account given there, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as part of the account of the help that was promised, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the passage as the account of the aid sent down to the community. Reading the sentence this way, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the help had a purpose that could be seen in the state of the enemy, which is the mark of help that is measured by its effect and not by its number. The sentence therefore gives the community a way of reading a battle by its outcome.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SHORT**

The verse is short because it carries an effect rather than an argument, and the shortness keeps the account moving toward the instruction that follows. On the reading followed here, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence in its place, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the help that was sent. The reader is shown the purpose of the help in one line and is then taken to the address that follows.

Think of a garrison that is told the relieving force is on the road and that its task is to break one wing of the besiegers and send the rest home. The purpose is stated so that the defenders know what to expect, and the verse states the purpose in the same way. What follows is an address to the messenger himself about a matter that was not his to decide.


---


## Verse 3:128

> You ˹O Prophet˺ have no say in the matter. It is up to Allah to turn to them in mercy or punish them, for indeed they are wrongdoers

**THE LIMIT SET FOR THE MESSENGER**

The passage turns to the messenger with a statement about his authority: ***“You ˹O Prophet˺ have no say in the matter. It is up to Allah to turn to them in mercy”***. For his part, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the matter named here is the outcome for the opposing people, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a decision that belongs to God alone. In the commentary on the passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the sentence concerns the people who had fought the community, and the report behind the passage is kept in the collections with the messenger's own concern for their guidance.

The sentence sets a boundary around the messenger's office, and the boundary is placed in a passage about a battle. Along the same lines, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse as the statement that the affair belongs to God, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reports attached to it. Since the community had just been given a promise of help, the sentence teaches it where the outcome of the other side's case rests, and the answer is not in the community's hands either.

**THE MERCY AND THE PUNISHMENT**

The sentence then states the two possibilities: ***“or punish them, for”*** and ***“indeed they are wrongdoers”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God may forgive whom He wills and punish whom He wills, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a decision with two outcomes. The sentence does not name which of the two will happen, and the omission is the substance of the verse, since the reader is left with the two possibilities and the One who decides.

For the same reason, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as part of the account of the day, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out for the community, since the caller to the faith is not the one who judges the response to it. On that reading, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the passage on the decision that belongs to God, and the verse therefore closes the narrative of the help with a reminder about who owns the outcome. What follows for the reader is a statement about the ownership of the heavens and the earth and the forgiveness that follows from it.

**WHY THE BOUNDARY IS DRAWN**

The boundary is drawn because the messenger had been concerned for a people who had refused him, and the verse answers that concern with the truth of the matter. For his part, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse in its place in the sūrah, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the sentence is addressed to the messenger and through him to everyone who carries a message. The reader is therefore taught where zeal ends and where trust begins.

Think of a bridge builder who has finished the span and who spends the evening worrying about the travellers who will not cross it. The road is his work and the crossing is another's choice, and the verse draws the same line. What follows is a sentence about the ownership of everything in the heavens and the earth, together with the two names of forgiveness and mercy.


---


## Verse 3:129

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. He forgives whoever He wills, and punishes whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**THE OWNERSHIP THAT SETTLES THE MATTER**

The sentence answers the limit set in the previous verse with a statement of ownership: ***“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens”*** and ***“and whatever is on the earth. He forgives whoever He wills”***. On this point, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sentence stands on its own as a statement of the rule that belongs to God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a dominion that covers all created beings. The two clauses together explain why the decision named in the previous verse is not divided.

The next clause states the two acts: ***“and punishes whoever He wills.”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that forgiveness is given to those who deserve it and punishment to those who deserve it, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the same sense of a will that is never arbitrary. The pairing of the two clauses is the answer to the question of the previous verse, since the One who owns the creation is also the One who decides the case of every person within it.

**THE TWO NAMES THAT CLOSE THE PASSAGE**

The verse closes with ***“And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of forgiveness for the one who repents and mercy for the one who dies in that state, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading with mercy toward those who obey. Taken in the same sense, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as the statement of the disposal of affairs in the heavens and the earth, and the two names at the close keep the reader from reading the punishment as the last word.

On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws the sentence out and notes that the mention of the ownership comes after the mention of the messenger's lack of say in the matter, so that the reader is taken from a limit to a rule. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the passage as part of the account of the help and the battle, and the verse therefore closes that account with the two names that belong to the One who decides. What follows for the reader is an address to the believers about a practice that was destroying their community from within.

**WHY THE VERSE IS PLACED HERE**

The verse is placed between a passage about a battle and an address about money, and the placement joins the two, since the same authority that decides the outcome of a war decides the case of a person's wealth. With this in view, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses on the ownership of God, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is therefore prepared for a command about dealings by a sentence about the One who sees them.

Think of a household where the estate belongs to one owner, who forgives the debts he chooses and collects the ones he chooses, and whose ledger is not open to the tenants. The verse states the ownership and the two acts in one line, and the tenants are then given an instruction. What follows is the prohibition of a practice that had been multiplying debts against the poor, given with the promise of success attached to the keeping of the command.


---


## Verse 3:130

> O believers! Do not consume interest, multiplying it many times over. And be mindful of Allah, so you may prosper

**THE PRACTICE THAT IS FORBIDDEN**

The address turns to dealings: ***“O believers! Do not consume interest, multiplying it many times over.”***. Following the same line, Al-Jalālayn keeps the explanation of the practice, which was the increase of the amount owed when a loan fell due and the borrower asked for more time, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the practice in the days before Islam. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of a debt that doubled in amount as the term was extended.

The word used in the sentence is the word for eating, and the image carries the sense of a gain that enters a person, since the increase was consumed rather than merely held. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the practice as it was known to the first hearers, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars together with the detailed rules that were drawn from the passage. The prohibition is therefore levelled at a specific practice, and the description of it in the verse is what makes the prohibition plain.

**THE DUTY AND THE PROMISE**

The sentence then gives the remedy and the reward: ***“And be mindful of Allah”*** and ***“so you may prosper”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the mindfulness here is the keeping of the command about the practice, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a mindfulness that consists in leaving the practice behind. The promise attached at the close is the word for success, and the sentence therefore joins the two things a trader cares about, the command and the outcome.

For his part, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the address as the opening of a passage of commands, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the instruction given to the community about its dealings and its own people. In the commentary on the passage, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the rules of the practice out at length and notes the difference between the increase that was prohibited and the trade that was permitted. Since the community was warned about the company it kept, this passage warns it about the dealings it made, and both warnings are aimed at the same thing.

**WHY THE VERSE IS ADDRESSED TO THE BELIEVERS**

The prohibition is addressed to the believers, and the address places the practice inside the community rather than outside it, since the people who lent money in this way were among the first hearers. Along the same lines, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the prohibition of the practice with its increase, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of a command that the community was to carry out in its own market. The reader is therefore given a rule about his own ledger rather than about another community's.

Think of a town that forbids a miller to raise the price of flour each time a farmer needs another week to pay, and a notice that is nailed to the miller's own door. The prohibition is addressed to the person who had been collecting, and the verse addresses the same person. What follows is a verse that names the fire and then the duty, so that the warning and the command stand together.


---


## Verse 3:131

> Guard yourselves against the Fire prepared for the disbelievers

**THE FIRE AND THE GUARDING**

The sentence follows the prohibition with a warning: ***“Guard yourselves against the Fire prepared”*** and ***“for the disbelievers”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the guarding is the avoidance of the practice that had been forbidden and of the fate that belonged to those who refused the command, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a fire that was prepared and a command to be spared from it.

The fire is described as prepared, and the expression places the warning outside the present hour, since what is prepared is waiting rather than falling. The sentence therefore gives the reader a destination to avoid rather than a scene to imagine, and the restraint is part of the warning. A reader who has been told what the fire was made for is left with the plain question of where he is standing. For the same reason, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the warning tells the community to separate itself from the conduct of the people the fire was prepared for, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The verse therefore turns a prohibition about money into a warning about the destination of the person who keeps the practice.

**WHY THE VERSE STANDS ALONE**

The verse is short enough to be carried in memory, and its position between a command and a command gives the passage its shape. On that reading, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the sentence in its place, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the warning is addressed to the believers although the fire is named with the disbelievers. The reader is therefore told that the door he is being pushed away from is not reserved for the people outside his community.

For his part, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the instruction on dealings, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the connection out and notes that the command to keep from interest is followed by the warning and then by the command of obedience. The sequence teaches the reader that the practice belongs to the same scale as the refusal of a messenger, and the sentence states that scale in one line. What follows for the reader is the command of obedience with the promise of mercy.

**THE SHAPE OF THE WARNING**

The warning is given as a command to guard rather than as a threat, and the shape leaves the reader with something to do. On this point, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the warning attached to the prohibition, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The sentence therefore does the work of a signpost, and it tells the reader which way the road runs without describing the fire itself.

Think of a path along a cliff where a rail is set a few steps before the edge and a sign names the drop rather than the traveler. The sign is meant for whoever is walking, and the verse is meant for the community that was walking with the practice in its market. What follows is the command that gathers the passage together, which is the obedience of God and the messenger.


---


## Verse 3:132

> Obey Allah and the Messenger, so you may be shown mercy

**THE COMMAND AND ITS PROMISE**

The sentence gives the community the command that gathers the passage: ***“Obey Allah and the Messenger”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the obedience concerns the prohibition of the practice that had been named and the avoidance of it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the plain sense of a command to obey God and the messenger. The sentence places the two obediences in one command, and the messenger is named beside God because the instruction about interest had come through him.

The promise is then attached: ***“so you may be shown mercy”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that mercy here is the covering that saves a person from punishment, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a mercy that is looked for rather than earned. The sentence therefore ends the passage of warning on a note that draws the reader forward, since the obedience and the mercy stand in one line.

**WHY THE VERSE REPEATS THE COMMAND**

The command of obedience appears in the sūrah more than once, and its repetition here marks the end of a passage about money and its beginning in the community's life. Taken in the same sense, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the sentence in its place, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the sentence is joined to the commands that precede it, so that the passage reads as one instruction with several parts. The reader is therefore given a closing command rather than a new subject.

On the same passage, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the account of what the community was told about its dealings and its own conduct, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the commands of the passage are built on the two qualities the sūrah had asked for. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses of instruction, and the sentence therefore joins the warning about the fire to the promise of mercy. What follows for the reader is an invitation to move quickly toward what is offered.

**THE MERCY THAT IS ASKED FOR**

The mercy is named at the close rather than at the beginning, and the placement gives the command its direction. A community that hears the command first and the promise second has been told both what is expected of it and what is held out to it, and the order keeps the two in their places. With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence, and the reader is shown that the obedience is not a toll but a road. Think of a household that is told the door is unlocked for whoever comes in and shuts the window against the storm, and the two instructions belong to the same evening. The verse joins the two in the same way, and what follows is an invitation to hasten toward forgiveness and toward a garden.


---


## Verse 3:133

> And hasten towards forgiveness from your Lord and a Paradise as vast as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**THE HASTENING AND WHAT IT SEEKS**

The passage turns from warning to invitation: ***“And hasten towards forgiveness from your Lord”***. Following the same line, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the command as an invitation to move toward what is offered, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the readings of the sentence and the words of the early scholars on it. The word used here is the word for racing, and the sentence therefore asks for speed rather than for a settled intention.

What is sought is then named in two parts: ***“and a Paradise as vast”*** and ***“as the heavens and the earth, prepared”***. In the account given there, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the garden with its breadth described, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a place whose width is measured by the two creations named in other passages of the Book. The forgiveness and the garden are joined in one command, and the reader is shown that the destination is the larger of the two things sought.

**THE WIDTH AND THE PREPARATION**

The verse describes the garden by its width rather than by its length, and the description is kept by the commentators as the measure of a place that no traveler could cross. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the sentence with the words of the early scholars, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the garden as the reward prepared for the people who guard themselves. The word prepared returns at the close, and it stood in the previous passage with the fire, so that the two destinations are stated in the same terms.

The verse ends with ***“for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the garden is prepared for the people who fear God and keep His commands, and the same quality has been named in the commands of the passage. For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the invitation to seek forgiveness and the garden, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as the opening of the passage that describes the people who are mindful.

**WHY THE VERSE IS PLACED HERE**

The verse is placed after the prohibition and the warning, and the order gives the reader a door after a wall. In the commentary on the passage, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the sentence in its place in the sūrah, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the two commands of the passage as a pair that moves the reader from refraining to seeking. The reader is therefore invited to leave the practice by walking toward something rather than by standing still.

Think of a market where a lane is closed because the floor has given way, and a second lane is opened at the same hour with a light at the end of it. The closure and the opening belong to one announcement, and the verse announces the two in the same order. What follows is a passage that describes the people for whom the garden was prepared, beginning with what they do with their money.

**THE TWO GIFTS IN ONE COMMAND**

The forgiveness and the garden are held in one command, and the pairing teaches the reader that the two are sought together. Along the same lines, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the qualities of the people named at the close out at length, and the verse that follows begins the description. The sentence therefore serves as a door into the passage on the character of the mindful, and the reward is named before the conduct is described.


---


## Verse 3:134

> ˹They are˺ those who donate in prosperity and adversity, control their anger, and pardon others. And Allah loves the good-doers

**THE GIVING IN TWO CONDITIONS**

The description opens with the first quality: ***“˹They are˺ those who donate in prosperity”*** and ***“and adversity, control their anger”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the giving named here is done in ease and in hardship alike, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a hand that is open in both conditions. The pairing is the point of the two clauses, since a person who gives when he has plenty has not yet been tested.

The second quality follows in the same sentence: ***“and pardon others.”***. For the same reason, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a person who holds his temper and forgives the one who wronged him, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of the two qualities placed together. The two belong to one disposition, since a person who cannot hold his anger cannot pardon, and the sentence arranges them so that the reader sees the root and the fruit.

**THE ENDING OF THE SENTENCE**

The verse closes with a statement of love: ***“And Allah loves the good-doers”***. On that reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the good-doers are the people described in the sentence itself, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a love given to those who do good in their dealings with God and with people. The word for the good-doer is broad enough to cover the qualities named above, and the sentence therefore leaves the reader with a title rather than a list.

For his part, Ibn Kathīr keeps the passage as the description of the people for whom the garden was prepared, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as the first of the qualities of the mindful. On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the whole profile out and notes that the qualities are arranged in a rising order, from the giving of money to the control of the self and then to the pardon of another person. Since the sūrah has been speaking about money, the first quality named here returns to the same ground.

**WHY THE GIVING IS NAMED FIRST**

The giving is named first because the passage has just forbidden a way of taking, and the remedy is placed beside the disease. In the same reading, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the description of the mindful, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The reader is therefore shown the opposite of the practice that had been prohibited, and the opposite is a hand that opens in need as well as in plenty.

Think of a well that is kept open in the dry season as well as the wet, and a village that has never had to dig a second one. The sentence describes the same kind of resource, and the two conditions in which the well is tested are named in it. What follows is the second quality of the same people, which concerns what they do after they have done wrong.


---


## Verse 3:135

> ˹They are˺ those who, upon committing an evil deed or wronging themselves, remember Allah and seek forgiveness and do not knowingly persist in sin—and who forgives sins except Allah

**THE WRONG AND THE REMEMBRANCE**

The description continues with what the same people do after a fault: ***“˹They are˺ those who, upon committing an evil deed”*** and ***“or wronging themselves, remember Allah”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the two phrases cover the fault done against others and the fault done against oneself, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a person who returns to God after either. The sentence therefore describes a people who are not free of faults and who are known by what they do with them.

The next clause names the act: ***“and seek forgiveness and do not knowingly persist in sin”***. Taken in the same sense, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of a person who asks forgiveness and does not settle in the fault once he knows it, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same sense of a return that is repeated rather than postponed. The word used at the close describes a deliberate staying in the wrong, and the sentence therefore leaves room for human weakness while closing the door on a settled choice.

**THE QUESTION AT THE END**

The verse finishes with the question that the whole description was leading toward: ***“and who forgives sins except Allah”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the question as a statement that no one else can forgive, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a door that is open to the one who asks. The question is put at the end of the sentence so that the reader supplies the answer, and the answer is what sends him back to the first clause.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the account of the qualities of the mindful, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the profile to its close and notes that the description ends with the remedy for failure. With this in view, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the description of the people of the garden, and the reader is shown a community whose members are not expected to be sinless. What follows for the reader is a passage about the fate of those who refused the truth after it had been shown to them.

**WHY THE DESCRIPTION ENDS HERE**

The description ends with the return after a fault, and the ending answers a fear that the earlier qualities would otherwise produce. Following the same line, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the passage as the portrait of the people who are mindful. A reader who has been shown the standard of giving and of pardoning is then shown what to do when he has fallen short of it.

Think of a household that keeps a basin by the door for washing the feet, placed there because the road is dusty rather than because no one ever stumbles. The basin is used often, and the verse describes a people who use one. What follows is a passage that names the punishment of the communities that refused the proof, and the contrast between the two passages carries the sūrah's lesson about what is offered and what is lost.


---


## Verse 3:136

> Their reward is forgiveness from their Lord and Gardens under which rivers flow, staying there forever. How excellent is the reward for those who work ˹righteousness˺

**THE REWARD OF THE PEOPLE DESCRIBED**

The description of the mindful closes with what they are given: ***“Their reward is forgiveness from their Lord”***. For his part, Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the account of what those people receive, and al-Qurṭubī notes the order in which the gift is arranged, with the covering of sins placed before the gardens. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the forgiveness is the wiping away of their faults, and the phrase from their Lord marks the gift as one no work could purchase.

The second part of the reward follows: ***“and Gardens under which rivers flow, staying there forever. How excellent is the reward”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of gardens beneath whose trees and dwellings the rivers run, with the four rivers of the reports named in the same passage, and al-Jalālayn keeps the sense of an abiding that the people are told will not end. The two halves of the sentence move from the removal of a debt to the granting of a home.

**THE PRAISE OF THE WORKERS**

The sentence ends with ***“for those who work ˹righteousness˺”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the workers are those whose deeds are good, and the praise is attached to the work rather than to the people as a class. Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the pronoun points to the people described in the verses just before, so the reward is answered to the qualities that were named, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same connection between the description and its end.

On the same passage, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the order of the passage out and notes that the qualities of the earlier verses concern dealings with people while the verses that follow concern the rights owed to God, so that the reader is taken from one side of a life to the other. The sentence therefore closes a portrait and opens a new subject at the same time, and the reader is left with the word for work in his ear.

**WHY THE SENTENCE COMES FIRST**

The reward is stated before the account of the days of battle, and the placement tells the reader how the story that follows is meant to be read. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the close of the passage on spending and forgiveness, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the verse is a promise given to the people who were described. The sentence is therefore a light held up before a hard passage, and the reader is prepared for a setback by a promise.

Think of a household that is told the terms of an inheritance on the morning of a difficult journey, so that the road is walked with the destination in view. The verse gives the terms in the same position, and the road that follows is the account of Uḥud. What comes next is an instruction to look at the ruins of earlier communities, since the lesson of that day had been learned before by other peoples.


---


## Verse 3:137

> Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers

**THE PATTERNS THAT PRECEDED THEM**

The address turns from the promise to history: ***“Similar situations came to pass before you”***. With this in view, Al-Baghawī keeps the readings of the early scholars on the word used here, with one taking it as the ways of God and another as His ordinances, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same range with the note that the passage was given to console the believers after Uḥud. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the passage concerns the pattern of reward for the repentant and punishment for the unrepentant.

In the account given there, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the ways of God had passed before the community, with people given time and then seized, and the sentence is therefore a reminder that the community's day was not a new event in the world. Since the previous verse had named the reward of the mindful, this verse names the fate of those who denied, and the two sentences place the two ends of the same pattern before the reader.

**THE JOURNEY AND WHAT IT SHOWS**

The sentence then gives a command: ***“so travel throughout the land”*** and ***“and see the fate of the deniers”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the command asks the community to travel and to reflect on the outcome of the earlier nations, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of an invitation to look rather than to argue. The command is attached to a reason that is visible on the ground, since ruins do not require a proof.

On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the passage on Uḥud, and al-Saʿdī notes that the patterns themselves were consolation, since the community had been told that its hardship was one of the ways of God rather than a sign of abandonment. Along the same lines, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the same consolation out, and the sentence therefore answers the grief of a defeated people with the history of a hundred defeated peoples. The reader is shown that the pattern is older than his own wound.

**WHY THE COMMAND IS TRAVEL**

The command is to travel rather than to read, and the choice suits the audience, since the first hearers were a trading people who knew the roads. In the same vein, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence as an invitation to consider the ends of the deniers, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The verse therefore teaches a method, since a community that looks at ruins with understanding has learned something that no argument could give it.

Think of a merchant who is warned about a particular kind of partnership and who then walks through the quarter where three such ventures ended in a boarded shop. The warning becomes knowledge at the third door, and the verse sends its reader on the same walk. What follows is a sentence that names the whole Book as the explanation of what the walk shows.


---


## Verse 3:138

> This is an insight to humanity—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**THE BOOK AND ITS READERS**

The sentence identifies what has been given: ***“This is an insight to humanity”*** and ***“a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing”***. On that reading, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading of al-Ḥasan and others that the reference is to the Book itself, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading with the note that the sentence may also point to the account just given. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the Book shows people what is lawful and what is not.

On this point, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of an explanation for all people, a guidance away from error and an admonition for those who guard themselves, and the three words are arranged in a rising order. In the same reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the same three elements and the same addressees, and the sentence therefore distinguishes between the offer and the taking, since the explanation is for everyone while the guidance is named with the people who fear God.

**THE ORDER OF THE THREE WORDS**

The three words carry three different works, and the order is worth reading: an explanation addresses the mind, a guidance addresses the will, and a lesson addresses the memory. Here, Al-Baghawī keeps the words of the early scholars on each of the three, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading that the book works in all three ways at once. The sentence therefore describes a text that does not leave the reader where it found him.

Taken in the same sense, Ibn Kathīr keeps the verse with the two around it as part of the instruction given to the community after the battle, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the comfort given to the believers. On the same passage, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the reminder of the past and the naming of the Book stand together, so that the reader sees where the knowledge of the past comes from. The sentence therefore answers the command to travel and look with the statement that the reading itself is the explanation.

**WHY THE GOD-FEARING ARE NAMED SECOND**

The second clause names the people who guard themselves, and the naming is not a restriction on the offer but a description of the response. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two addresses, and the reader is shown that the same book is an explanation for all and a lesson for those who take it as a lesson. The sentence therefore leaves the reader with a choice about how he is to be counted.

Think of a public notice that explains the new law to the whole street and that changes the conduct of the households that read it as a warning. The notice is the same for all, and the verse describes a book in the same way. What follows is an address that lifts the community off the ground of its defeat.


---


## Verse 3:139

> Do not falter or grieve, for you will have the upper hand, if you are ˹true˺ believers

**THE TWO THINGS FORBIDDEN**

The address turns to the community after the battle: ***“Do not falter or grieve”***. With this in view, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading that the two prohibitions were given as comfort after the killing and the wounding of the day, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of an address that steadies the companions. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the faltering is the weakening before the enemy and the grief is over what was missed.

The two words name two states rather than two acts, and the order runs from the outer to the inner, since a people who weaken in front of an enemy then grieve over what they lost. In the account given there, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of a prohibition against shrinking from the fight and against sorrow for what happened at Uḥud, and the sentence therefore treats the discouragement itself as the danger. What follows for the reader is the reason given for the command.

**THE REASON AND THE CONDITION**

The sentence then gives the ground for the command: ***“for you will have the upper hand”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community will be given victory over its enemies, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of a promise that reaches beyond the day of the battle. The sentence does not promise that no loss will come, and it promises that the community will not be left beneath the other side.

The condition follows: ***“if you are ˹true˺ believers”***. On the reading followed here, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the promise is tied to the faith of the community, and the sentence therefore makes the outcome depend on the quality that had been tested on the field. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the consolation given after Uḥud, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the strengthening of the community's resolve. The reader is shown a promise with a condition that can be kept.

**WHY THE VERSE ADDRESSES THE LOSING SIDE**

The verse addresses the side that had lost the day, and that is the point of the sentence. In the same vein, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consolation out and notes that the community is told of its final position rather than of its present one, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The voice is therefore the voice of a commander who speaks to his men after a hard hour and names the end of the campaign.

Think of a crew that has lost its mast in a storm and is told that the hull is sound and the harbour is ahead, and a captain who adds that the report holds for as long as the crew keeps its work. The verse speaks in the same way to the same kind of people, and the next sentence explains why the loss of a day does not decide the war.


---


## Verse 3:140

> If you have suffered injuries ˹at Uḥud˺, they suffered similarly ˹at Badr˺. We alternate these days ˹of victory and defeat˺ among people so that Allah may reveal the ˹true˺ believers, choose martyrs from among you—and Allah does not like the wrongdoers—

**THE WOUNDS ON BOTH SIDES**

The sentence sets the day in proportion: ***“If you have suffered injuries ˹at Uḥud˺, they suffered similarly ˹at Badr˺. We alternate these days ˹of victory and defeat˺ among people”***. On that reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading that the wounding named here is the harm done to the community on its own day, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading with the two days named. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community had been hurt and the other side had been hurt before it.

On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the account of the two battles out and notes that seventy of the community were killed on the day of Uḥud while the other side had lost its leading men at Badr, so the comparison is exact. The sentence therefore answers the grief of the community with an observation about the ordinary course of war, and the reader is shown that no side owns the days.

**THE TURNING OF THE DAYS AND ITS PURPOSE**

The sentence then states the purpose: ***“so that Allah may reveal the ˹true˺ believers, choose martyrs from among you”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the revealing is the bringing out of those who hold firm, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a distinction that the day makes visible. The choosing of the martyrs is kept by al-Ṭabarī as one of the ends that the days of trial serve, and the community is told that its losses were not wasted.

The sentence closes with ***“and Allah does not like the wrongdoers”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the wrongdoers here are those who broke the command on the field, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the closing clause. The reader is therefore given three purposes in one sentence, a testing, a ranking and a warning, and the sentence holds them together without ranking one above the others.

**WHY THE DAYS ARE NAMED IN THE PLURAL**

The days are named more than once because the lesson concerns a pattern rather than a single afternoon, and the wider bearing belongs to the whole sentence. Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of a turning that belongs to the ordinary course of events, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the consolation given to the community after its loss. On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same reading of the verse as part of the account of the battle.

Think of a market where one merchant is up this week and another next, and an old trader who watches both without concluding that the world has changed hands. The verse states the same rule about the days of a community, and it states it before the reader can draw a larger conclusion from one bad week. What follows is a verse that completes the purpose named here with two more of its ends.


---


## Verse 3:141

> and distinguish the ˹true˺ believers and destroy the disbelievers

**THE PURPOSES COMPLETED**

The sentence continues the purpose of the previous verse: ***“and distinguish the ˹true˺ believers”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the distinguishing is the purifying of the believers from their faults through what befell them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a proving that leaves the community cleaner than it was. The expression suggests a separating, since a hard day separates those who hold from those who do not. The two clauses of the sentence are therefore one act seen from two sides, and the community is shown what its own day was doing while it was happening.

The second clause follows: ***“and destroy the disbelievers”***. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the destruction named here is the defeat of the other side in the fighting, and the sentence is joined to the previous verse so that the two are read as one list of ends. Following the same line, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as a statement of what the days of trial accomplish, and the reader is shown that both the victory and the defeat serve the same purpose.

**WHY THE TWO CLAUSES STAND TOGETHER**

The two clauses stand together because a battle is a sieve on both sides, and the sentence describes the same event from the side of the community and from the side of its enemy. In the account given there, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consolation out and notes that the passage was given to the community after its loss, so that the loss itself is shown to have a work to do. Reading the sentence this way, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the comfort of the believers, and the sentence therefore moves the reader's eyes from the field to the purpose.

On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the passage as part of the account of Uḥud and its lessons, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on both clauses. The verse is short because it carries a conclusion rather than a story, and the shortness lets the reader hold the two ends in one hand. What follows for the reader is a question put directly to the community about what it had expected.

**THE WORDS THAT DO THE WORK**

The two verbs are the substance of the verse, since one prunes and the other cuts down, and the pairing describes a single day's work. Along the same lines, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the clause as the purification of the believers, and the reader is shown a community that came out of its hard day smaller and sounder. In the same vein, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the purposes that the battle served, and the sentence therefore closes the argument about the meaning of the loss.

Think of a farmer who thins a crop so that the remaining plants stand, and who says that the thinning and the harvest are both work of the same field. The verse speaks of two results in the same way, and it does so without apology for either. What follows is a question about the garden the community expected to enter without such a day.


---


## Verse 3:142

> Do you think you will enter Paradise without Allah proving which of you ˹truly˺ struggled ˹for His cause˺ and patiently endured

**THE EXPECTATION PUT IN QUESTION**

The passage turns to the community with a question: ***“Do you think you will enter Paradise without Allah proving”***. On that reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the question as a rebuke addressed to people who had expected the reward without the work, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a supposition that the sentence refuses. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the question concerns a community that wanted the garden without the fighting.

The word for the proving is then carried into two clauses: ***“which of you ˹truly˺ struggled ˹for His cause˺”*** and ***“and patiently endured”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the struggle named here is the fighting on the day of Uḥud and that the patience is the endurance of hardship, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same pairing of the two qualities. The sentence therefore names the two things the day had tested, and the community had been tested in both.

**WHY THE TEST IS NAMED AS A PROVING**

The test is named as a proving rather than as a punishment, and the difference is the mercy of the sentence. In the same reading, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the knowledge of God is made manifest in the world through the deeds of people, and al-Baghawī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is therefore shown a day that produced a fact rather than a verdict, since what is proven can be seen by the people who lived it.

Here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the address given to the community after Uḥud, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as the strengthening of the believers. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the question follows the two commands about courage and grief, so that the reader is first told what to feel and then asked what he expected. The sentence therefore closes the passage on consolation with a question of its own.

**WHY THE QUESTION IS STILL ASKED**

The question is asked in the sūrah of every community that reads it, and it is not aimed at the people of one battle alone. On the same passage, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the trial that the community underwent, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The reader who expects the garden is met by the same question, and the question names the struggle and the patience as the two things that stand in the way of an easy answer.

Think of a guild whose members are told that the rank of master is given without a test, and an old craftsman who asks whether they have ever seen a hall of masters where nobody had served an apprenticeship. The question is meant for the people in the room, and the verse asks its question of the community that had just come off the field. What follows is a sentence about the wish such people had expressed before the day arrived.


---


## Verse 3:143

> You certainly wished ˹for the opportunity˺ for martyrdom before encountering it, now you have seen it with your own eyes

**THE WISH THAT WAS MADE**

The sentence reminds the community of what it had asked for: ***“You certainly wished ˹for the opportunity˺”*** and ***“for martyrdom before encountering it, now you have seen it with your own eyes”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the wish, which was made in the words that the community might have had a day like the day of Badr and stood in it as its martyrs had stood.
