With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the wish was expressed before the community met the fighting on the day of Uḥud.

The sentence is therefore a mirror held up to a people who had spoken about a hard day while it was still a distance away. Following the same line, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the address as part of the consolation given after the battle. The reader is shown a wish that was sincere and untested, and the two qualities are not the same thing.

**THE SIGHT THAT WAS GIVEN**

The second clause reports what happened: the community saw the thing it had wished for. In the account given there, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading that the sight named here is the fighting and its weapons, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading with the swords of the opposing side named among what was seen. The sentence therefore grants the wish and reports its cost in one line, and the reader is left to weigh the two halves of it.

Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the account of the day, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the address that steadied the community afterwards. On the reading followed here, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the turning point out and notes that the wish was not condemned in the sentence, and the fault lay in what the day then produced in the hearts of the people who had wished. The sentence therefore corrects the reader without mocking the desire.

**WHY THE WISH IS RECALLED**

The wish is recalled because a community that knows its own words can measure its own conduct, and the sentence does the measuring without a further comment. Along the same lines, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as part of the account of Uḥud and its lessons. The reader is therefore shown that the desire and the endurance are two different gifts, and a person may hold the first while failing in the second.

Think of a young man who tells his family that he will stand at the front of the queue on the day the river must be crossed, and who then reaches the bank and finds the water higher than he had pictured. The wish is not a fault and the crossing is not a fair test of it, and the verse holds the same two things before its readers. What follows is a sentence about the messenger himself, which was given when a rumour of his death shook the field.


---


## Verse 3:144

> Muḥammad is no more than a messenger; other messengers have gone before him. If he were to die or to be killed, would you regress into disbelief? Those who do so will not harm Allah whatsoever. And Allah will reward those who are grateful

**THE RUMOUR THAT SHOOK THE FIELD**

The sentence answers a rumour: ***“Muḥammad is no more than a messenger”*** and ***“other messengers have gone before him.”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the report that the companions came to the messenger and told him they had heard that he had been killed and that this was why they had left the field, and al-Jalālayn keeps the report of the rout that followed the rumour together with the words of the hypocrites who told the believers to return to what they had left.

The sentence places the messenger within the line of the messengers, and the placement is the answer, since a messenger who has predecessors is a man who will also have an end. On that reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse as the statement that the message does not depend on the length of the messenger's life, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reports attached to the passage. The reader is shown the one fact that the field had forgotten in an afternoon.

**THE QUESTION AND THE ANSWER**

The sentence then puts the matter as a question: ***“If he were to die”*** and ***“or to be killed, would you regress into disbelief?”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the words are addressed to the people who had been shaken, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of a question that asks about the direction of a person's steps after a death. The two ways of ending are named together, so that no one could build a case on the manner of a death.

The sentence then states the consequence for the person who turns: ***“Those who do so will not harm Allah whatsoever.”***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the harm falls on the one who turns and not on God, and the clause is placed before the promise so that the two results stand side by side. The sentence therefore leaves the reader with two possibilities and no third, and the choice is stated as a matter of walking rather than of feeling.

**THE REWARD AND THE GRATEFUL**

The verse closes with ***“And Allah will reward those”*** and ***“who are grateful”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the reward belongs to the people who remain steadfast and thankful, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a reward given to the grateful. The sentence ends the account of the rumour with a promise, and the promise is attached to gratitude rather than to grief.

Here, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as part of the consolation given to the community after the day, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequel of the battle out and notes that the field was steadied by a single sentence about the nature of a messenger. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as the answer to the rumour, and the reader is shown a community whose whole position depended on one distinction. What follows for the reader is a sentence about the term of every soul, which takes the same lesson to its root.


---


## Verse 3:145

> No soul can ever die without Allah’s Will at the destined time. Those who desire worldly gain, We will let them have it, and those who desire heavenly reward, We will grant it to them. And We will reward those who are grateful

**THE TERM THAT NO ONE CUTS SHORT**

The sentence states the rule that the previous verse was building toward: ***“No soul can ever die without Allah’s Will at the destined time. Those”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no soul leaves this life except by the permission of God and at a term that is already written, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a fixed time that is neither brought forward nor put back. The sentence therefore answers the fear that had emptied the field, since a person who cannot die early cannot die late either.

The word used for the term carries the sense of a writing, and the commentators keep the same reading of a decree that is set down before it is lived out. On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of a term that belongs to the knowledge of God, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The community is therefore told that the day of Uḥud did not shorten a single life, and the sentence is placed directly after the rumour about the messenger.

**THE TWO DESIRES AND THE TWO ANSWERS**

The sentence then divides people by what they want: ***“who desire worldly gain, We will let them have it”*** and ***“and those who desire heavenly reward, We will grant it to them.”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the two groups are separated by their intention and that each is given according to what he sought, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a reward measured by the aim rather than by the deed alone.

Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the two clauses as a statement about the way the gifts are distributed, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the address given to the community after the battle, where the two aims had appeared in the same line of men. The sentence therefore explains the disorder of the day from within, since a hand that fights for a share of the spoils and a hand that fights for what is with God are two different instruments. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson of steadfastness out at this point, and the two desires are the ground on which it stands.

**THE REWARD AND THE GRATEFUL**

The verse closes with ***“And We will reward those”*** and ***“who are grateful”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the reward is given to the people who remain thankful in hardship, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a promise attached to gratitude. The clause closes the sentence with an act rather than a rank, and the reader is shown that the quality named here can be practised on any day of his life.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lesson of Uḥud, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the term of every soul. Think of a caravan that is told the day of its arrival was fixed before it set out, and a traveler who then stops counting the miles as if they changed the destination. The verse makes the same point about the term of a life, and it makes it in the middle of a battle narrative. What follows is a sentence about the people of earlier prophets, who stood under the same threat and did not shift.


---


## Verse 3:146

> ˹Imagine˺ how many devotees fought along with their prophets and never faltered despite whatever ˹losses˺ they suffered in the cause of Allah, nor did they weaken or give in! Allah loves those who persevere

**THE COMPANIES OF THE EARLIER PROPHETS**

The sentence turns the community's eyes to the past: ***“˹Imagine˺ how many devotees fought along with their prophets”***. With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence as a statement of number, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the devotees are the people who stood with their prophets against the enemy. Following the same line, Al-Jalālayn keeps the readings of the sentence and notes the sense of a great many, so that the community is shown that its situation had been faced by crowds before it.

The next clause says what those companies did not do: ***“and never faltered despite whatever ˹losses˺ they suffered in the cause of Allah”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that they did not weaken for anything that happened to them in the path of God, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of a firmness that held through losses. The sentence therefore gives the community a standard that was set by people who are not named, and the anonymity is part of its force.

**THE TWO THINGS THEY DID NOT DO**

The sentence then adds two denials: ***“nor did they weaken”*** and ***“or give in!”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the first as a failure of resolve and the second as a submission to the enemy, and al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the two clauses as a pair, one describing what happens inside a person and one describing what follows from it. The order is the same as in the earlier address to the community, since the inner state comes before the outer act.

On the reading followed here, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the address that steadied the believers after the battle, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the account of the earlier companies out and notes that the community is being shown its own conduct in a mirror held up by history. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lesson of Uḥud. The sentence therefore gives the reader a picture of a company that held, and the picture is offered without a single name in it.

**THE LOVE THAT CLOSES THE SENTENCE**

The verse closes with ***“Allah loves those who persevere”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the love is given to the people who endure, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a title that belongs to the steadfast. The sentence ends a description of conduct with a statement about love, and the reader is shown what the community had in its hands on the day of the battle.

Think of a town that is told of a company of masons who rebuilt a wall three times after three floods, and a sentence at the end of the report naming the regard in which such people are held. The record is meant to move the reader to the same conduct, and the verse reports the earlier companies for the same purpose. What follows is the prayer that those companies prayed, which is given here as a model of what to say when the line is under pressure.


---


## Verse 3:147

> And all they said was, “Our Lord! Forgive our sins and excesses, make our steps firm, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”

**THE PRAYER THAT WAS THE WHOLE OF IT**

The sentence records what the earlier companies said: ***“And all they said was, “Our Lord!”***. On that reading, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the sentence reports the whole of their speech after their prophet had been killed while they stood their ground, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same account of a company that remained firm at its post. The construction of the sentence narrows what they said to a single thing, so that the prayer is presented as the whole of their response.

The first petition follows: ***“Forgive our sins and excesses, make our steps firm”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the sin named here is the lesser fault and the excess is the serious one, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same distinction between the two words. The pairing is honest about the people who spoke, since they asked pardon before they asked for firm ground, and the order of the two petitions is the substance of the prayer.

**THE PETITION THAT ASKS FOR VICTORY**

The prayer closes with ***“and grant us victory over the disbelieving people.””***. On this point, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the request is for help against the people who had refused the message, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of a prayer for the outcome of the field. The sentence therefore ends where the company's circumstance was, since a people who have asked for pardon and for footing may then ask for the day.

In the same reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the prayer as one sentence spoken by a company under pressure, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same account with the words of the early scholars on its parts. Here, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the lesson drawn from the earlier peoples, and the reader is given a model of speech rather than a report of an event. What follows for the reader is the answer that the prayer received.

**WHY THE PRAYER IS QUOTED IN FULL**

The prayer is quoted in full because the community needed words for the hour it was in, and a model is more useful than a description. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the account of the earlier companies, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson of steadfastness out at this point. The three petitions cover the past, the present and the future of the person who speaks them, and the sentence therefore gives the reader a form of words that can be said on any hard day.

Think of a boatman's family that has kept one short prayer for storms for three generations and that says it in the same order every time, first for what has already gone wrong, then for the oars, then for the far shore. The verse preserves a prayer of the same shape, and it preserves it for the people who were still on the water. What follows is the account of the answer the earlier companies received.


---


## Verse 3:148

> So Allah gave them the reward of this world and the excellent reward of the Hereafter. For Allah loves the good-doers

**THE ANSWER IN TWO PARTS**

The sentence reports the answer to the prayer: ***“So Allah gave them the reward of this world”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the reward named here is the victory and the spoils that came to them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a gift given in the present life. The sentence therefore places a tangible result beside the petition for help, and the reader is shown that the earlier companies did not wait for everything.

The second part follows: ***“and the excellent reward of the Hereafter.”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the second reward is the garden, and al-Jalālayn keeps the note that the word excellent marks an addition beyond what was owed. The two clauses are therefore arranged in a rising order, with the lesser gift first, and the sentence teaches the reader how the two rewards stand in relation to each other.

**WHY THE TWO REWARDS ARE JOINED**

The two rewards are joined because the community that had just lost a day needed to see both, and the pairing is the consolation of the sentence. With this in view, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lesson of the earlier peoples, and al-Baghawī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two gifts. Following the same line, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the passage as the account of what the steadfast were given, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of the address to the community after the battle.

In the account given there, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson of the earlier companies out and notes that the record is given so that the community would know the pattern, and the sentence therefore states the outcome before the narrative returns to Uḥud. The reader is shown a company that prayed, held and received, and the three parts stand in one short verse. What follows for the reader is a warning about the company the community was keeping on its own day.

**THE LOVE THAT REPEATS**

The verse closes with ***“For Allah loves the good-doers”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the people answered in this verse are the same people described in the verse before, and the repetition of the title joins the two sentences into one lesson. On the reading followed here, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as a statement about the steadfast, and the reader is left with a word that has appeared earlier in the same sūrah in a description of the generous.

Think of a workshop where the same stamp is put on a beam that has passed three inspections, and the stamp appears again on the finished frame. The title is given twice because the work was seen twice, and the verse gives the title to the same people it described at the start. What follows is an address to the community that pulls it in the opposite direction.


---


## Verse 3:149

> O believers! If you yield to the disbelievers, they will drag you back to disbelief—and you will become losers

**THE WARNING ABOUT YIELDING**

The sūrah returns to the community with a warning: ***“O believers! If you yield to the disbelievers, they will drag you back to disbelief”***. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the address names two companions and the group that had invited them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of a warning against obedience to people who refused the message. In the same vein, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the setting, with the rumour of the messenger's death and the mischief that grew in its shadow.

The sentence names the act and the result in one line, and the action is a dragging rather than a fall. On that reading, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of a promise of return to the earlier state, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The community is therefore told that obedience to the other side does not lead to a compromise but to a return, and the sentence leaves no middle position inside it.

**THE LOSS THAT IS NAMED AT THE END**

The verse closes with ***“and you will become losers”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the loss is the loss of the reward and the standing that the community had been given, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of an outcome that is complete. The word is placed after the dragging, so the reader sees the loss as the end of a movement rather than as a penalty added from outside.

On this point, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the warnings given to the believers after the battle, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse in the same passage on the conduct of the community. The sentence therefore answers a moment when some in the community were inclined to listen to the other side, and it answers with the plainest possible account of where that listening ends. What follows for the reader is the sentence that states where the community's protection actually lies.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SHORT**

The verse is short because the danger it names is near, and a warning about a step does not need a long argument. In the same reading, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the address to the community, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the warnings that follow the account of Uḥud. The reader is shown a single step and a single end, and the sentence does not leave room for a third possibility.

Think of a mountain path where a single rail stands at the bend and a voice behind the travelers says only two words, the name of the drop and the name of the walker. The warning is short because the bend is close, and the verse is short for the same reason. What follows is a sentence that answers the warning with a statement about who holds the community's affairs.


---


## Verse 3:150

> But no! Allah is your Guardian, and He is the best Helper

**THE ANSWER TO THE WARNING**

The warning is answered at once: ***“But no! Allah is your Guardian”***. Here, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading of the sentence as a refusal of the path just named, and the word at the opening turns the reader away from the other side before the alternative is stated. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the guardianship named here is the preservation of the community and its victory over its enemies, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the same reading of a protection that belongs to God.

The sentence places a guardian before the community, and the word used for it carries the sense of a patron who takes charge of a person's affairs. On the same passage, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as the answer to the warning in the verse before it. The community is therefore shown that the loss of a day does not remove the One who holds its affairs.

**THE SECOND NAME AND ITS WORK**

The verse closes with ***“and He is the best Helper”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God is the strongest of helpers, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a helper whose help is not exceeded. The pair of names answers the pair of dangers in the previous verse, since a guardian answers the dragging and a helper answers the loss, and the two sentences are therefore read as one exchange.

With this in view, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse as the statement of who holds the community's affairs, and al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading with the words of the early scholars on the passage. Following the same line, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage's turn out and notes that the answer to a warning is a statement about God rather than a promise about the field. In the account given there, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the address to the community after the battle. What follows for the reader is an account of the terror that was cast into the other side.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SO SHORT**

The verse is one of the shortest in the passage, and its brevity matches the answer it gives, since a statement about who holds an affair does not need elaboration. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that steadied the community, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence as the close of the warning. The reader is left with two names and two clauses, and the whole weight of the passage rests on them.

Think of a child in a crowded market who is told not to follow a stranger and who is then told that her father is standing at the end of the lane. The second sentence answers the first without argument, and the verse answers the warning in the same way. What follows is a passage about the fear that was thrown into the hearts of the other side on the day of the battle.


---


## Verse 3:151

> We will cast horror into the hearts of the disbelievers for associating ˹false gods˺ with Allah—a practice He has never authorized. The Fire will be their home—what an evil place for the wrongdoers to stay

**THE HORROR IN THE HEARTS**

The passage opens with a promise about the other side: ***“We will cast horror into the hearts of the disbelievers”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the horror named here is the fear of the community, so that the opposing force runs from it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the report that the enemy resolved to return and finish the community but were struck with terror and did not come back.

On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets the verse in the account of Uḥud and reads it as part of the sequel to the battle, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The reader is therefore shown the other side of a day that had gone against the community, since the force that left the field in possession of it did not return for the second march it had planned. The promise is stated in the future tense, and the reports show it fulfilled.

**THE REASON AND THE PRACTICE DENIED**

The sentence then names the reason: ***“for associating ˹false gods˺ with Allah”*** and ***“a practice He has never authorized.”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the association named here is the partner they set up beside God, and the clause about authorization closes the door on any claim that the practice was permitted. Here, Al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a worship that no command of God ever established.

The sentence therefore links the defeat of the community's enemies to their own doctrine rather than to the community's weakness, and the link is the substance of the passage. Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the two clauses together, and the reader is shown a fear that entered where the case was weakest. Since the community had been taught that the outcome of the days belongs to God, this sentence shows what the same authority did with the other side's intention.

**THE HOME AND THE PLACE**

The verse closes with ***“The Fire will be their home”*** and ***“what an evil place”*** and ***“for the wrongdoers to stay”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the fire is the dwelling of the people described, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a resting place that is named with revulsion. The word for the dwelling carries the sense of a place where a person settles, and the sentence therefore moves from a passing fear to a permanent end.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the three clauses, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the account of what came to the community's enemies from their own conduct. Think of a raiding party that turns back in the dark and then finds that the road it took home leads to a worse place than the field it left. The verse reports the same two steps, and it reports them as the end of the party's plan. What follows is an account of the day itself, with its promise, its shaking and its pardon.

**WHY THE VERSE COMES FIRST**

The verse is placed before the account of the community's own conduct, and the order keeps the reader from reading the battle as a failure. With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the promise as part of the account of Uḥud, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the passage among the verses that describe what happened to the two sides. The sentence therefore draws the outer edge of the story before the inner one, and the reader sees the same day from the far side.


---


## Verse 3:152

> Indeed, Allah fulfilled His promise to you when you ˹initially˺ swept them away by His Will, then your courage weakened and you disputed about the command and disobeyed, after Allah had brought victory within your reach. Some of you were after worldly gain while others desired a heavenly reward. He denied you victory over them as a test, yet He has pardoned you. And Allah is Gracious to the believers

**THE PROMISE THAT WAS KEPT**

The sentence begins with what was given: ***“Indeed, Allah fulfilled His promise to you”*** and ***“when you ˹initially˺ swept them away by His Will”***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community routed the enemy at the beginning of the fight by the command and help of God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same stage of the battle with the promise of victory that had been made to them. The first part of the day is therefore stated plainly, and the reader is shown that the beginning had gone as promised.

The expression suggests a force that moves over an enemy without resistance, and the sentence uses it before the turn is named. In the account given there, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse as the account of the day with its stages, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars together with the reports that name the men who held the pass. The community is therefore shown the day in the order in which it happened, and the first stage is credited to the will of God.

**THE TURN AND ITS FOUR STEPS**

The sentence then names what changed: ***“then your courage weakened”*** and ***“and you disputed about the command”*** and ***“and disobeyed, after Allah had brought victory within your reach. Some of you were after worldly gain”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the weakening came before the quarrel and the quarrel before the disobedience, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sequence with the disagreement over the order the messenger had given.

The four steps are arranged in the order in which a line breaks, from a state of heart to a dispute and then to an act, and the sentence does not leave the community a place to hide behind the last of them. The next clause divides the people: ***“while others desired a heavenly reward. He denied you victory over them”***. On the reading followed here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the two aims inside the same army, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the account of the day and its lessons.

**THE TEST AND THE PARDON**

The sentence then names the purpose and the mercy: ***“as a test, yet He has pardoned you.”***. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the denial of the day was a trial rather than a verdict, and the pardon is stated in the same sentence, so that the community is not left with its failure alone. In the same vein, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the high station of the companions out and notes that the passage records their fault and their pardon in the same place, which is the way the Book speaks of its own people.

The verse closes with ***“And Allah is Gracious to the believers”***. On that reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the grace named here is the kindness that covers a fault once the fault has been named, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a favour given where it was not owed. For his part, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage in the account of Uḥud, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same passage as the lesson drawn from the day. The reader is therefore shown a community that was disciplined and pardoned in one account, and the two parts of the sentence are read together.

**WHY THE WHOLE STORY IS TOLD**

The whole story is told because a community learns from its own record, and the sūrah tells it without hiding either the failure or the pardon. On this point, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the promise and the day, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the several clauses. Think of a crew whose log records the hour the wind shifted, the argument on deck and the fact that the ship came home, and a captain who reads all three to the next crew. The log of the day is read in the same way. What follows is the scene of the field itself, with the messenger calling behind the running men.


---


## Verse 3:153

> ˹Remember˺ when you were running far away ˹in panic˺—not looking at anyone—while the Messenger was calling to you from behind! So Allah rewarded your disobedience with distress upon distress. Now, do not grieve over the victory you were denied or the injury you suffered. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**THE RUNNING AND THE CALL**

The scene is set with the community in flight: ***“˹Remember˺ when you were running far away ˹in panic˺”*** and ***“not looking at anyone”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community climbed the hill after the defeat and paid no heed to anyone, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account with the messenger's call named in the words of the field.

The sentence then sets the voice against the running: ***“while the Messenger was calling to you from behind!”***. Here, Al-Jalālayn records the call itself, which was an appeal to the men to come back to him, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the messenger's position on the field. The two halves of the sentence form one picture, since the running is measured by the voice that was calling after it. Taken in the same sense, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the account of the day, and the reader is shown a scene that the community itself is asked to remember.

**THE DISTRESS UPON DISTRESS**

The sentence then names what followed: ***“So Allah rewarded your disobedience with distress upon distress.”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community was repaid with one grief after another, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account with the layers of loss named in the reports, the loss of the day, the loss of the news that ran through the ranks and the grief that came after both. The sentence is the answer to the flight rather than a punishment added to it, and the two words for distress carry the sense of one wave following another.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as the account of the day and its states, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out and notes that the setbacks of the day were a mercy meant to keep the community away from the causes of the defeat. The reader is therefore shown that the distress was a form of teaching rather than a verdict, and the sentence states it in the language of a recompense.

**THE COMMAND AND THE KNOWLEDGE**

The verse closes with an instruction and a statement: ***“Now, do not grieve over the victory you were denied”*** and ***“or the injury you suffered.”*** and ***“And Allah is All-Aware of what you do”***. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the grief forbidden here concerns what was missed of the spoils and what was suffered of wounds, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a prohibition that turns the community from what is past toward what is next.

Think of a crew that rows back to a harbour it failed to reach and is told not to sit counting the miles it lost but to look at the wind that is coming. The verse turns its readers in the same direction, and the last clause names the knowledge that attends what they do next. The sentence therefore closes the account of the flight with an instruction and a reminder, and the reader is left with the day's work rather than its losses. What follows is a passage about the two parties on the field after the fighting stopped.


---


## Verse 3:154

> Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you, while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, “Do we have a say in the matter?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “All matters are destined by Allah.” They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you. They say ˹to themselves˺, “If we had any say in the matter, none of us would have come to die here.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Even if you were to remain in your homes, those among you who were destined to be killed would have met the same fate.” Through this, Allah tests what is within you and purifies what is in your hearts. And Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**THE SERENITY AND ITS FORM**

The sentence opens with a gift given after the worst hour: ***“Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you”***. Following the same line, Al-Jalālayn keeps the reading that the security came down as a slumber that covered one party of the community, with the men swaying under their shields and their weapons slipping from their hands, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same account of the men of sincerity who slept on the field.

The form of the gift is the part of the sentence that carries the mercy, since sleep in the presence of an enemy is the sign of a settled heart. In the account given there, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the slumber that came down upon a party of them, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the day. The reader is shown a rest that arrived while the fighting was still near, and the sentence places it after the distress so that the two states can be measured against each other.

**THE OTHER PARTY AND ITS THOUGHTS**

The sentence then turns to the second party: ***“while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah”*** and ***“the thoughts of ˹Pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, “Do we have a say in the matter?””***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the other party was troubled in its own soul and thought about God in a way that belonged to the days before the message, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of people who were not sure of the promise they had been given.

The question they asked is reported as part of their thought rather than as a public speech, and the sentence therefore shows what was being said inside a man while he stood in a line. On the reading followed here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as the account of the two parties on the field, and the reader is shown that the day separated the community into two companies by what each of them thought of God. Along the same lines, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the meaning of the passage out and notes that the two parties stood in the same danger and left it with different hearts.

**THE ANSWER ABOUT THE MATTER**

The sentence then gives the answer: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “All matters are destined by Allah.””***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the whole affair belongs to God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a decree that leaves no matter outside it. The answer is given to a question about a say in the matter, and the sentence answers it without denying the question's pain, since a person may ask why while the answer stands above him.

The next clause describes the party's conduct after the answer: ***“They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you. They say ˹to themselves˺, “If we had any say in the matter, none of us would have come to die here.””***. On that reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the thought was kept hidden and then spoken among themselves, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the account of the two parties on the day. The reader is shown a sentence that a person says to himself when he believes that a step could have saved him, and the sentence is reported so that the answer to it can be given.

**THE ANSWER THAT FOLLOWS THE HIDDEN THOUGHT**

The answer then follows: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Even if you were to remain in your homes, those among you who were destined to be killed would have met the same fate.””***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the term of every person is written and that no house gives shelter from it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a death that finds its own place. The sentence therefore answers the hidden thought on its own ground, since the men who said that staying home would have saved them are told that the house would not have saved them either.

On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse with its several clauses, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. In the same reading, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the two parties and their thoughts out at length and notes that the answer given here is the same as the answer given earlier in the sūrah about the term of a soul. The reader is therefore shown a lesson repeated, and the repetition is placed at the point where the community could have argued with it. A person who says that a different road would have saved him is answered by the term that was fixed before the road was chosen, and the answer is given twice in the sūrah so that no one misses it.

**THE PURPOSE AND THE LAST WORD**

The sentence then names the purpose of the whole day: ***“Through this, Allah tests what is within you and purifies what is in your hearts.”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the day was a trial of the inward and a cleaning of it, and the two clauses name a revealing and a refining in one sentence. Taken in the same sense, Al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of the purpose of the battle, and the reader is shown that the day had a work to do inside the people who lived it.

The verse closes with ***“And Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God knows what the hearts conceal, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a knowledge that reaches what no report can reach. The sentence therefore ends a long verse about hidden thoughts with a statement about the One who hears them, and the two clauses are read together, since the hiding and the knowing are set side by side in one breath. Think of a household that argues about a door left open while the father, who has been listening the whole time, finally says what the wind did and what the watch was for. The verse lets the reader hear the last word of a day of argument, and the answer is about the hearts rather than about the door. What follows is a sentence about the men who fled and the pardon that met them.


---


## Verse 3:155

> Indeed, those ˹believers˺ who fled on the day when the two armies met were made to slip by Satan because of their misdeeds. But Allah has pardoned them. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**THE MEN WHO FLED**

The passage names the people involved: ***“Indeed, those ˹believers˺ who fled on the day”*** and ***“when the two armies met were made to slip by Satan”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the sentence concerns those who turned back on the day the two hosts met, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account with the report that twelve men held their places. The sentence therefore names the men who left their posts, and the naming is followed by the cause of the leaving.

The cause is stated as the slipping worked by the enemy who is never seen, and the expression portrays a foot that loses its place rather than a choice made in cold blood. With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the sentence as the account of the men who turned away, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is shown that the fault is named and traced to its source, and the sentence does neither more nor less than that.

**THE CAUSE AND THE PARDON**

The sentence then names the reason for the slip: ***“because of their misdeeds.”***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that some of what they had earned beforehand opened the door to the slip, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of a fault that had a history behind it. The sentence therefore refuses both simplifications, since the men are not excused as if they had done nothing and not condemned as if the enemy's whisper were the whole story.

The pardon follows at once: ***“But Allah has pardoned them. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the pardon is a covering of the fault, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a mercy that reaches the men who ran. The two clauses of the mercy are placed after the account of the fault, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as the close of the account of the day. The reader is therefore shown a record that ends with a pardon rather than with a verdict.

**WHY THE VERSE ENDS AS IT DOES**

The verse ends with the two names rather than with a warning, and the ending tells the reader what the passage was for. Reading the sentence this way, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson of the day out and notes that the community was corrected and forgiven in the same account, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lessons of Uḥud. The sentence therefore closes the battle narrative in a way that leaves the reader hope rather than shame, since the men named here are the same men who carried the message afterward.

Think of a foreman who writes both the mistake and the repair into the same page of the log, so that the next crew knows the danger and the men named in it are not left without a trade. The verse writes the page in the same way, and the pardon is entered by the One who has the right to enter it. What follows is an address to the community about the words that people were saying over their dead.


---


## Verse 3:156

> O believers! Do not be like the unfaithful who say about their brothers who travel throughout the land or engage in battle, “If they had stayed with us, they would not have died or been killed.” Allah makes such thinking a cause of agony in their hearts. It is Allah who gives life and causes death. And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**THE WORDS ABOUT THE DEAD**

The address turns to a habit of speech: ***“O believers! Do not be like the unfaithful”*** and ***“who say about their brothers”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the passage concerns the people who spoke over those who had died on their travels or in battle, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of a saying that was common among them. The sentence therefore forbids the community to copy a way of talking, and the way of talking is then quoted.

The quotation follows: ***“who travel throughout the land”*** and ***“or engage in battle, “If they had stayed with us, they would not have died”*** and ***“or been killed.””***. On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the two journeys named here, one for trade and one for fighting, and al-Baghawī keeps the same account of the two kinds of departure. The saying is built on a single assumption, that a place decides a death, and the sentence puts the assumption in the mouth of the people who made it.

**THE AGONY THAT FOLLOWS THE SAYING**

The sentence then names what the saying produces: ***“Allah makes such thinking a cause of agony in their hearts.”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the grief named here is the regret that settles in the speaker, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a punishment built into the saying itself. The sentence therefore does not need a further penalty, since the words have already produced their own result in the person who repeats them.

Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the address given to the community after the battle, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as the warning against the reasoning of the other side. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out and notes that the community is being taught how to speak about its dead, and the sentence therefore corrects a habit of the tongue rather than a doctrine. The reader is shown that the fault of the saying lies in what it takes away from God.

**THE LIFE AND THE DEATH**

The sentence then states the fact the saying had ignored: ***“It is Allah”*** and ***“who gives life and causes death.”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no one else gives life or brings death, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a disposal that belongs to God alone. The clause is placed directly after the reported saying, so that the two are read as a question and an answer, and the answer leaves no room for the house, the journey or the battlefield.

The verse closes with ***“And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God sees the deeds of people and the words they speak, and the clause is the last of the sentence for that reason. With this in view, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse as part of the address given to the community in the account of Uḥud, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the warnings of the passage. The reader is therefore left with the two names, the one who holds the term and the one who watches the conduct.

**WHY THE VERSE IS ADDRESSED TO THE BELIEVERS**

The prohibition is addressed to the believers, and the address is the point of the sentence, since the saying it forbids was one the community might have picked up from its neighbours. Following the same line, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the sentence in the account of the day, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The verse therefore treats a way of speaking as something to be refused, and it attaches the refusal to the two facts about life and death.

Think of a village that begins to say that the men who went to the mill were foolish, and an elder who answers that the mill did not grind them and the road did not bury them. The verse answers the same kind of talk with the same kind of fact, and it answers it before the habit hardens. What follows is a sentence that weighs the two ends of a life against each other.


---


## Verse 3:157

> Should you be martyred or die in the cause of Allah, then His forgiveness and mercy are far better than whatever ˹wealth˺ those ˹who stay behind˺ accumulate

**THE TWO WAYS OF ENDING**

The sentence states a condition with two branches: ***“Should you be martyred”*** and ***“or die in the cause of Allah”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the two branches name the death that comes in the path of God, whether at the hand of an enemy or in an ordinary way, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of two ways that lead to the same reward. The sentence therefore takes the fear out of one kind of death in particular, since the second branch is available to every person.

Reading the sentence this way, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse as the statement of what awaits the people who end their lives in the path of God, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of the two clauses. The sentence is placed after the words of the people who counted the houses of the dead, and the placement is the answer, since the two branches are opened where the saying had closed a door. What follows for the reader is the comparison that the sentence draws.

**THE COMPARISON WITH WEALTH**

The comparison follows: ***“then His forgiveness and mercy are far better than whatever ˹wealth˺ those ˹who stay behind˺ accumulate”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the forgiveness and the mercy of God outweigh everything the people who remain at home gather up, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a preference stated plainly. The sentence does not deny the value of what is gathered, and it measures it against something else and finds the scale tipped.

Along the same lines, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the address given to the community after Uḥud, and al-Saʿdī keeps the verse in the same address as the two around it. In the same vein, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consolation out and notes that the passage gives the community a way of weighing what it lost, and the sentence does that weighing in its last clause. The reader is therefore shown the terms of the comparison and left to draw the conclusion.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SO SHORT**

The verse is short because it carries a comparison rather than an argument, and a comparison is finished once the two sides are named. On that reading, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lesson of the day, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that steadied the community after the battle. The reader is given the two sides and nothing else, and the brevity lets him hold both in one hand.

Think of a man who is offered a purse for a field he has planted and who is asked only to compare the purse with the harvest standing in front of him. The comparison is the argument, and the verse states it in the same way. What follows is a sentence that takes the same comparison further, since the assembly that no one escapes is named in it.


---


## Verse 3:158

> Whether you die or are martyred—all of you will be gathered before Allah

**THE TWO OUTCOMES AND THE ONE GATHERING**

The sentence names the two ways of ending again, and adds what follows both: ***“Whether you die or are martyred”*** and ***“all of you will be gathered before Allah”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the gathering is the assembly before God for the account, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a return that includes everyone named in the two branches.

The word for the gathering carries the sense of a people brought together in one place, and the sentence therefore answers the saying of the previous passage with the fact that no one is left outside the assembly. On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the verse as the statement of the general return, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is shown that the difference between the two kinds of death ends at the same door.

**WHY THE TWO BRANCHES RETURN**

The two branches return in this sentence because the sūrah is answering the same fear twice, and the repetition is deliberate. In the same reading, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse in the address to the community, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the consolidation of the believers after the day. The sentence therefore removes the distinction between a death at home and a death in the field at the point where the distinction matters most, which is the gathering.

Here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage with the words of the early scholars, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the two verses form a single lesson about what a person keeps of his own life. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the sentence as part of the address to the community. The reader is left with a fact that applies to every person he knows, and the fact is stated without a threat attached to it.

**WHY THE VERSE STANDS ALONE**

The verse is short and complete, and it stands alone in the passage as a single statement about everyone. On the same passage, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that speak about the end of a life, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on it. The sentence therefore gives the reader a floor under the whole passage, since the ending named here is the one thing that no one in the community could avoid.

Think of a river that has many fords and one mouth, and a ferryman who says that every road in the valley arrives at the same water. The fords differ in comfort and in length, and none of them changes the river, and the verse states the same fact about the ends of human lives. The verse states the same fact about the ends of human lives, and it states it in eleven words. What follows is an instruction to the messenger himself about how to treat the people he leads.


---


## Verse 3:159

> It is out of Allah’s mercy that you ˹O Prophet˺ have been lenient with them. Had you been cruel or hard-hearted, they would have certainly abandoned you. So pardon them, ask Allah’s forgiveness for them, and consult with them in ˹conducting˺ matters. Once you make a decision, put your trust in Allah. Surely Allah loves those who trust in Him

**THE LENIENCY AND ITS SOURCE**

The sentence names the quality that held the community together: ***“It is out of Allah’s mercy”*** and ***“that you ˹O Prophet˺ have been lenient with them.”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the leniency shown to the men who had failed on the field came from the mercy of God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a gentleness that was granted rather than produced. The sentence therefore lifts the quality out of the messenger's own character and names its source, which is the mercy that stands over both him and the people he led.

With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the account of the day, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence. The reader is shown a leader who had every right to be angry and who was gentle instead, and the sentence says where the gentleness came from. What follows is the reason the gentleness mattered, stated as a condition that was not met.

**THE CONDITION THAT WAS NOT MET**

The sentence then states what would have happened otherwise: ***“Had you been cruel”*** and ***“or hard-hearted, they would have certainly abandoned you.”***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that a harsh manner would have scattered the people around him, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a company that would have broken away. The sentence therefore describes the danger of a quality rather than the fault of a person, and it does so for the benefit of whoever leads after him.

The two words name an outward harshness and an inward hardness, and the pair is arranged in the same order the sūrah has used before, from what shows to what lies under it. In the account given there, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage as the instruction given after the day, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as the consolidation of the community. The reader is shown a leader whose strength was in his gentleness, and the sentence states the alternative that the community escaped.

**THE FOUR COMMANDS AND THE TRUST**

The sentence then gives four instructions: ***“So pardon them, ask Allah’s forgiveness”*** and ***“for them, and consult with them in ˹conducting˺ matters. Once you make a decision, put your trust in Allah.”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the pardon covers what the men had done, the prayer asks God to cover it, the consultation binds the community to the decision, and the trust hands the outcome over. The four steps are arranged in an order that begins with a person's own hand and ends with God.

On the reading followed here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the reading of the passage as the instruction to the messenger after the battle, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the four commands out and notes that the consultation named here was practised by the messenger himself. Along the same lines, the reading kept by Ibn ʿUthaymīn places the verse in the lesson of Uḥud. The reader is therefore given a way of handling a community that has failed him, and the way ends with trust rather than with resentment.

**THE LOVE THAT CLOSES THE VERSE**

The verse closes with ***“Surely Allah loves those”*** and ***“who trust in Him”***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the love is given to the people who hand their affairs to God, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a title that belongs to the trusting. The sentence therefore ends the instruction with a statement about love, and the reader is shown what the four commands were moving toward.

Think of a captain who sets the course after listening to the crew, and who then goes to the wheel himself rather than standing in the middle of the deck arguing about it. That order of work is the one portrayed in the verse, and the quality of the man at the wheel is what the sentence closes on. What follows is a sentence that states the same trust in a shorter form.


---


## Verse 3:160

> If Allah helps you, none can defeat you. But if He denies you help, then who else can help you? So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**THE HELP THAT SETTLES EVERYTHING**

The sentence states a condition with its consequence: ***“If Allah helps you, none can defeat you.”***. On that reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no force can overcome a people whom God has helped, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a victory that cannot be taken away. The sentence is placed after the account of a day that had gone badly, and the placement makes it an answer to what the community had seen.

The second branch follows: ***“But if He denies you help”*** and ***“then who else can help you?”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no helper remains once the help of God is withheld, and the question is left for the reader to answer. On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as the statement of where help comes from, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two branches. The reader is shown that the two clauses exhaust the possibilities, and neither of them leaves room for a party of allies.

**THE TRUST THAT CLOSES THE PASSAGE**

The verse closes with ***“So in Allah let the believers put their trust”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the believers ought to place their reliance on God, and the sentence returns to the command that the sūrah had given on the way to the battle. Here, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse as the close of the passage, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as the address given to the community after the day.

Taken in the same sense, Al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the sentence, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson of the passage out and notes that the community was taught to weigh its own strength and its own losses in the same scale. On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as part of the lesson of Uḥud. The reader is therefore left with the same instruction he received a few verses before, and the repetition marks the end of the account of the battle.

**WHY THE PASSAGE ENDS HERE**

The passage ends with trust because the whole account concerns the limits of a community's own arrangements, and the last sentence names the thing that outlasts both victory and defeat. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the close of the account of the day, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse. The reader is shown a community that was corrected, pardoned and instructed, and the last word is a command to lean on the One who did all three. A body that has been taught the term of every soul and the worth of its own plans has been left with one thing to hold, and the sentence names it.

Think of a market town that has built its wall, drilled its watch and buried its dead, and a mayor who closes the year by saying that the wall and the watch were never the thing that kept the town. The wall and the watch were real, and the verse does not deny them either; the point is what a community leans on when both of them fail. The verse closes the account of the battle in the same way, and the sentence is short enough for a child to keep. What follows for the reader is a passage about the property a person leaves behind when he is called away from it.


---


## Verse 3:161

> It is not appropriate for a prophet to illegally withhold spoils of war. And whoever does so, it will be held against them on the Day of Judgment. Then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**THE SPOILS AND THE OFFICE**

The sentence opens with a denial: ***“It is not appropriate”*** and ***“for a prophet to illegally withhold spoils of war.”***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the report that a piece of red cloth went missing on the day of Badr and that some people began to wonder whether the messenger had taken it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of the suspicion that was answered here. The sentence therefore clears the office before it rules on the conduct of anyone else, and the two parts of the denial belong together, since what is refused is not merely the act but the possibility of the act in that station.

The office of a prophet is the reason named in the sentence, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage together with the reports that came down about the day. The reader is shown a community that had begun to suspect its own leader over a missing item, and the answer given is about the office rather than about the cloth. On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the subject out and notes that the verse came down when the question of the spoils was at the front of everyone's mind.

**THE MISAPPROPRIATION AND THE DAY IT IS HELD**

The sentence then turns from the office to the act: ***“And whoever does so, it will be held against them on the Day of Judgment.”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that whoever withholds any part of what was taken in the field will be brought with it on the day the accounts are made, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the same clause. The expression for the withholding is the one used for something taken out of the common store by stealth, and the sentence therefore names a small act with a large appointment attached to it.

Here, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage as part of the address to the community after the day of Uḥud, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as the account of what the community owed its own members. Think of a quartermaster who removes a sack from the store and finds the sack written into the ledger of a day that has not yet come. The sentence reports the same arrangement, and it reports it without naming a place of punishment or a length of time, which leaves the reader with the weight of the clause itself. What follows in the same verse is the principle on which the ledger is kept.

**THE FULL PAYMENT AND THE JUSTICE OF IT**

The sentence closes with the general rule: ***“Then every soul will be paid in full”***, ***“for what it has done,”*** and ***“and none will be wronged”***. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that every soul is given what its own acts have earned, whether that is a full reward or a full accounting, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a payment that leaves no remainder. The two last clauses are read as one, since the promise of full payment and the denial of any wrong are the two halves of a single statement about the day.

On the same passage, Al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that speak about the reckoning. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the passage as part of the lessons drawn from the day of Uḥud. The reader is therefore shown a rule that applies to the small theft and to the great deed alike, and the rule is stated without a comparison, which is why it can be carried away by anyone who hears it.


---


## Verse 3:162

> Are those who seek Allah’s pleasure like those who deserve Allah’s wrath? Hell is their home. What an evil destination

**THE TWO MEN AND THE QUESTION**

The sentence puts two people side by side: ***“Are those who seek Allah’s pleasure like those who”*** and ***“deserve Allah’s wrath?”***. With this in view, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the first is the one who took his share honestly and kept away from deceit, while the second is the one whose deceit brought the wrath upon him, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same two figures with the one following the pleasure of God and the other laden with His anger. The question is asked in the shape of a comparison, and a comparison of this kind carries its own answer, since no one hearing it would put the two on the same footing.

The sentence therefore does not have to say that the two are different, and the form of the question states it. Following the same line, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of the two figures. The reader is left to supply the answer, and the answer is expected to be immediate.

**THE HOME AND THE END**

The sentence then names what waits for the second of the two: ***“Hell is their home. What an evil destination”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the fire is the dwelling and the final place of the man who earned the wrath, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a journey whose end is named with revulsion. The two words are placed next to each other, the home and the destination, so that the reader sees both the stay and the road that led to it.

Reading the sentence this way, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage out and notes that the comparison answers the men who had argued about the spoils, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of the address about the two ways. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the reading of the verse as one of the lessons of the day. Think of two men walking away from the same market at night, one with a lantern and one with a debt, and the difference between their two roads is the whole of the sentence.

Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the comparison and notes that the two figures are drawn from the two companies that the day of Uḥud separated, since a reward and a wrath do not arrive at the same man. The question is therefore put instead of answered because every hearer already carries the answer, and the sentence lets him state it for himself. Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse among the lessons of the day, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of the address that followed the fighting. The reader is therefore given two figures rather than a rule, and the comparison of the two carries the ruling without a formal statement of it.


---


## Verse 3:163

> They ˹each˺ have varying degrees in the sight of Allah. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do

**THE DEGREES**

The sentence states what the comparison had implied: ***“They ˹each˺ have varying degrees in the sight of Allah.”***. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the people who avoided deceit have ranks in the garden while the people who practised it have levels in the fire, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of stations that differ with what each person did. The expression for the degrees is the one used for steps on a stair, and the sentence therefore presents the difference between people as a matter of height rather than of kind.

In the same vein, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading of the two companies and their two sets of stations. The reader is shown that the two paths do not merely end in different places but run at different levels all the way, and the sentence names the sight of God as the measure of both.

**THE SIGHT OF WHAT IS DONE**

The sentence closes with ***“And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do”***. On that reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that God sees the deeds of both companies and that nothing of what a person does is hidden from the accounting, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a knowledge that takes in the act and the actor. The clause is placed after the mention of the degrees, and the placement gives the reader the ground of the difference, which is not the opinion of people but what was actually done.

For his part, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage in the address about the spoils, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on it. On this point, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the verse stands between the comparison and the address that follows it, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the passage as part of the same lesson. The reader is left with a short sentence about rank and a short sentence about sight, and the two together say that the ranks are assigned by the One who saw everything.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SHORT**

The verse is short because the two clauses are the summary of the comparison above it, and a summary does not need to be argued out. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the ranks, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the address about the two companies. The reader is therefore given the two facts that settle the question, which are the difference in station and the sight that assigned it.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the ranks named here are not the ranks people give one another in the world, and the difference is the measure used to place each person. The point of the verse is that no one is lost in a crowd, since the One who sees what each person does has already fixed what each person receives.


---


## Verse 3:164

> Indeed, Allah has done the believers a ˹great˺ favour by raising a messenger from among them—reciting to them His revelations, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom. For indeed they had previously been clearly astray

**THE FAVOUR AND THE MESSENGER**

The sentence gives the gift before it gives the parts: ***“Indeed, Allah has done the believers a ˹great˺ favour by raising a messenger from among them”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the messenger was sent from among the people themselves, with a lineage they knew, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of a man of their own tongue rather than an angel or a stranger, so that they could understand what he said and be honoured by it. The choice of the messenger is therefore part of the favour, and the sentence begins with the word that marks a gift given without being owed.

Here, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the greatness of this favour, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as the statement of what the community was given. The reader is shown a people who had received a teacher from their own houses, and the sentence measures the gift by the standing of the one sent rather than by the number of his hearers.

**THE FOUR WORKS**

The sentence then lists what the messenger was sent to do: ***“reciting to them His revelations,”***, ***“purifying them,”***, ***“and teaching them the Book”*** and ***“and wisdom.”***. Taken in the same sense, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the four parts are a recitation that carries the words, a purification that clears the heart, a teaching of the Book that gives the text, and a wisdom that gives the understanding, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sequence of the two pairs. The order moves from hearing to cleaning to knowing, and the sentence therefore describes a work that goes all the way down.

On the same passage, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the four clauses, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on them. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage out and notes that the teaching of the Book and the wisdom are named as two separate gifts, since a text without understanding does not settle a person. With this in view, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same passage as the account of what a messenger brings. The reader is therefore shown a favour that is not a single gift but a set of works done in a fixed order.

**THE FORMER CONDITION**

The sentence closes with a description of what the people had been before all of this: ***“For indeed they had previously been clearly astray”***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community had been in a plain error before the messenger was raised among them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a straying that was visible to anyone who looked. The clause is placed last so that the reader can measure the gift by the distance it covered, and the measurement is the point of the sentence.

In the account given there, Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the address about the blessings given to the community, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that recount the favour of the messenger. Think of a caravan that lost its road and wandered until a guide came out of the same camp and led it back, and the members of the caravan never stop speaking of the guide. The sentence records the same kind of debt, and it records it as a favour rather than as a correction people had asked for.


---


## Verse 3:165

> Why is it when you suffered casualties ˹at Uḥud˺—although you had made your enemy suffer twice as much ˹at Badr˺—you protested, “How could this be?”? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “It is because of your disobedience.” Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**THE QUESTION ABOUT THE TWO DAYS**

The sentence begins with the question the community was asking: ***“Why is it when you suffered casualties ˹at Uḥud˺”*** and ***“although you had made your enemy suffer twice”*** and ***“as much ˹at Badr˺”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the disaster of the day of Uḥud is set beside the double disaster the community had inflicted on the enemy at Badr, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same comparison with the numbers of the fallen on both days. The question is therefore built out of the community's own memory, since the earlier day had gone entirely its way.

The sentence then reports the words they spoke: ***“you protested, “How could this be?”?”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the community asked how such a thing could happen when they were the people of the truth, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is shown the shape of the question, which is not a request for instruction but an expression of surprise, and the surprise is what the next clause answers.

**THE ANSWER AND ITS REASON**

The sentence then gives the reply: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “It is”*** and ***“because of your disobedience.” Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything”***. Along the same lines, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the cause named here is the disobedience that had been committed on the field, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of an answer that sends the community back to its own conduct rather than to the strength of the enemy. The question had asked about the outcome, and the answer speaks about the cause.

In the same vein, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse in the address given after the day, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two parts of the sentence. On that reading, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out and notes that the closing name of power is placed here so that the community does not read the defeat as a weakness in the promise it had been given. For his part, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the passage as part of the lessons of Uḥud. The reader is therefore shown a defeat explained without the enemy being named, and the explanation is the more useful for that.

Al-Saʿdī keeps the passage among the lessons of the day and notes that the question and the answer together teach the community how to read its own losses, since a defeat that is traced to a cause can be repaired while a defeat that is blamed on fortune cannot. Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the accounts of the two battles, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two days. The reader is therefore left with a comparison that runs from Badr to Uḥud, and the point of the comparison is that the same community stood on both days.


---


## Verse 3:166

> So what you suffered on the day the two armies met was by Allah’s Will, so that He might distinguish the ˹true˺ believers

**THE HARM AND THE WILL BEHIND IT**

The sentence takes up what had happened on the field: ***“So what you suffered on the day the two armies met was by Allah’s Will,”***.
