For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the harm named here is the killing and the wounding of the day when the two hosts met, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the day at Uḥud with the clause that places the whole event under the will of God. The sentence therefore does not put the day outside the rule by which everything happens, and it names no other cause for it.

On this point, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading of the day and its harm. The reader is shown an account in which the loss of the community and the decree of God are stated in one breath, and the statement answers the surprise that the previous verse had reported. What follows in the same sentence is the purpose the day was serving.

**THE PURPOSE OF THE DAY**

The sentence then names what the day was for: ***“so that He might distinguish the ˹true˺ believers”***. In the same reading, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the trial was set up so that the true believers would be known, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a knowledge that appears outwardly in what people do. The sentence therefore does not carry the idea that God learned something He had not known; the difference between people was brought into the open where the community could see it.

Here, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage in the account of the day, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of the lesson drawn from Uḥud. Taken in the same sense, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the clearest proof of a person's faith often arrives in the hour when his plan has collapsed. On the same passage, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse among the lessons of the day. Think of a house that is tested by a storm and shows at last which of its beams were sound. The sentence reports the same revealing, and it reports it about people rather than about houses.

**THE TWO COMPANIES THE DAY PRODUCED**

The purpose named in the sentence was worked out in the people who lived the day, and the two companies it produced are described in the verses that follow. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the day with the two groups, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is therefore shown that a trial of this kind does not create the difference between people but makes it visible, since the hidden state of a heart is not known until a day presses on it.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the accounts of Uḥud, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out and notes that the loss of the day and the profit of the day were given to the same community. The point of the sentence is that the harm came by the will of God and the separation came by the same will, so that neither of them can be read as an accident.


---


## Verse 3:167

> and expose the hypocrites. When it was said to them, “Come fight in the cause of Allah or ˹at least˺ defend yourselves,” they replied, “If we had known there was fighting, we would have definitely gone with you.” They were closer to disbelief than to belief on that day—for saying with their mouths what was not in their hearts. Allah is All-Knowing of what they hide

**THE SECOND COMPANY EXPOSED**

The sentence continues the purpose named before it: ***“and expose the hypocrites.”***. In the reports kept from the early scholars, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the group in question was the company of ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy, who turned back toward the city and were seen for what they were, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of the men whose conduct on the road to Uḥud uncovered them. The sentence therefore stands beside the previous one, and the two together name the two kinds of people the day separated.

With this in view, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reading of the passage as the account of the day, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the words of the early scholars on the company named here. The reader is shown that the same event which strengthened one group exposed another, and the sentence says it in three words. What follows is the exchange of words that made the exposure plain.

**THE INVITATION AND THE REPLY**

The sentence then reports what was said to them: ***“When it was said to them, “Come fight in the cause of Allah”***, ***“or ˹at least˺ defend yourselves,””*** and ***“they replied, “If we had known there was fighting, we would have definitely gone with you.””***. Following the same line, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the call was to join the fight or at least to stand with the community, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account with the men turning back while the fighting was still ahead of them. The reply they gave is a sentence that sounds like an excuse and reads like a confession, since a man who says that he would have come had he known there was fighting has said that he only follows a certain outcome.

The two halves of the answer are therefore set against each other by the sentence itself, and al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the exchange in the account of the day. In the account given there, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage out and notes that the men were not accused of spoken denial but of a heart that did not match the tongue. The reader is shown a group whose words were correct in form and empty in substance, and the sentence reports the words without comment.

**THE NEARNESS TO DISBELIEF**

The sentence then gives its verdict on them: ***“They were closer to disbelief than to belief on”***, ***“that day—for saying with their mouths what was not in their hearts.”*** and ***“Allah is All-Knowing of what they hide”***. Reading the sentence this way, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the men of the day were nearer to the company of disbelief than to the company of belief, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading with the reason attached, which is the saying of one thing while the heart held another. The sentence therefore does not call them disbelievers and does not leave them among the believers, and the middle place it gives them is the meaning of the word used for them.

On the reading followed here, Al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse among the lessons of Uḥud. Along the same lines, Ibn Kathīr keeps the section as the account of the group and its conduct. Think of a man on a ship who speaks of the harbour and keeps his own bag packed by the rail; the sentence describes the same arrangement between a mouth and a heart, and it closes with the statement that the One who sees what is hidden knows both.


---


## Verse 3:168

> Those who sat at home, saying about their brothers, “Had they listened to us, they would not have been killed.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Try not to die if what you say is true!”

**THE MEN WHO STAYED BEHIND**

The sentence names the people and their words: ***“Those who sat at home,”*** and ***“saying about their brothers, “Had they listened to us, they would not have been killed.””***. In the same vein, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the people named here are the ones who stayed in the city and spoke about their brothers who had gone out to fight, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same account of a group whose advice was never offered in time to be tested. The words they used turn the deaths of the fallen into the result of a decision about staying at home, and the sentence reports them exactly as they were spoken.

On that reading, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage out and notes that the reply given here answers the same kind of talk that an earlier verse had forbidden, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the two clauses. The reader is shown a group making a claim about a road they never walked, and the sentence leaves the claim standing for one line before the answer comes.

**THE ANSWER IN ONE LINE**

The answer follows at once: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Try not to die”*** and ***“if what you say is true!””***. For his part, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the reply turns the claim back on the speaker, since a man who holds that a house preserves its owner from death has only to wait in his house and see, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a challenge that needs no further argument. The sentence therefore answers the claim with a test rather than with a proof, and the test is one that every person can run.

On this point, Al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading of the passage in the account of the day, and al-Baghawī keeps the same reading of the exchange. In the same reading, Al-Saʿdī keeps the verse as part of the address given after Uḥud. The reader is left with a short sentence that closes a long argument, and the brevity of the answer is the point of it, since the claim was as thin as the reply.

**WHY THE CHALLENGE IS PUT THIS WAY**

The challenge is put in the form of a test rather than a refutation, and the choice of form belongs to the meaning of the sentence. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the group and its claim, and al-Saʿdī keeps the passage as part of the address given after the day of Uḥud. The reader is therefore not asked to weigh an argument but to consider a plain fact, since a man who expects to live because he stays at home has only to stay at home and wait.

Al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the passage among the lessons of the day. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the same reply answers the talk of every generation that measures a life by the safety of its own room. The point of the sentence is that the term of a person is not kept in a house and not lost on a road.


---


## Verse 3:169

> Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead. In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for—

**THE PROHIBITION OF THE THOUGHT**

The sentence forbids a way of thinking rather than a way of speaking: ***“Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead.”***. Here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that the people in view are those killed on the days of Badr and Uḥud, and that they are not to be counted as other dead people are counted, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the passage with the persons named by the sake of the One for whom they died. The sentence therefore addresses the survivor's thought before it addresses his tongue, and the two are close together in a person's mind.

Taken in the same sense, Al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reading of the passage with the questions that people raised about the state of the fallen. The reader is shown a grief that pressed into the community, and the answer to it begins by refusing the word that had settled on those deaths.

**THE LIFE AND THE PROVISION**

The sentence then states what is true of them: ***“In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for”***. On the same passage, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that they are living and provided with gifts, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of spirits that move freely in a wide place near their Lord. The two clauses name a life and a provision, and the order in which they come means that the provision follows the presence, which is the greater of the two.

In the reports kept from the early scholars, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the passage as the consolation given to the community after the fighting, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of that consolation. With this in view, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the verse out and notes that the statement is given in the present tense, so that the reader hears the condition of the fallen as a fact of the moment rather than as a promise for later. Following the same line, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse among the lessons of Uḥud. The reader is left with a sentence that does not soften the loss and changes the state of the one who suffered it.

**WHY THE PROHIBITION COMES FIRST**

The prohibition comes before the statement because a grief that has settled into a way of thinking is harder to move than a grief that has only been felt. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of the fallen and their state, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the verse. The reader is therefore given the instruction first and the reason after it, since the sentence works on the thought before it works on the sadness.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses that speak about the people killed in the path of God, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same passage as part of the consolation given to the community. The point of the verse is that the change described is in the condition of the fallen and not in the feelings of those who remain, so that a family mourning at home hears both the loss and the news at once.


---


## Verse 3:170

> rejoicing in Allah’s bounties and being delighted for those yet to join them. There will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**THE JOY OF THE PROVIDED**

The sentence describes the state of the people just named: ***“rejoicing in Allah’s bounties”*** and ***“and being delighted for those yet to join them.”***. In the account given there, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that they are glad for what God has given them of His honouring, and that their gladness reaches out to their brothers who are still behind them in the world and who will join them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the two clauses with the second attached to the first. The sentence therefore gives the reader a company that is not closed in on its own reward.

Reading the sentence this way, Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the passage out and notes that the second joy is a joy about other people rather than about oneself, which is the higher of the two, and al-Ṭabarī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is shown a company whose pleasure is not diminished by leaving others behind, and the sentence says what the objects of that pleasure are without describing the place in which it is felt.

**THE FEAR AND THE GRIEF REMOVED**

The sentence then removes the two things that trouble a person: ***“There will be no fear for them,”*** and ***“nor will they grieve”***. On the reading followed here, Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading that no fear waits for them over what is coming and no grief lies over what has passed, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a state that is finished with both directions of care. The two clauses are placed at the end of the passage, and the placement makes them the summary of the whole condition.

Along the same lines, Al-Baghawī keeps the reading of the verse with the passage of consolation, and al-Saʿdī keeps the same passage as part of what the community was told about its dead. In the same vein, Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the verse as one of the lessons of the day. Think of a family that has sent its young men on a voyage and hears at last that they are settled in a good country and are asking after the ones still at home; the two sentences report the same news, and they end the account of the day on the note the community needed most.

**WHY THE TWO CLAUSES CLOSE THE PASSAGE**

The two clauses close the passage because they remove the two cares that outlast an event, the fear of what is coming and the grief over what has gone. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the reading of both clauses, and al-Ālūsī keeps the words of the early scholars on the passage. The reader is therefore left with a condition that has nothing left to lose, since a person who has neither a fear nor a grief has been given the whole of his peace.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the section among the verses about the people killed in the path of God, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out and notes that the joy of the company is given before the relief that has come to it. The point of the sentence is that the reward and the relief belong to the same people, and the reader is shown both in the space of a few words.


---

> They are joyful for receiving Allah’s grace and bounty, and that Allah does not deny the reward of the believers

## Verse 3:172

> ˹As for˺ those who responded to the call of Allah and His Messenger after their injury, those of them who did good and were mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have a great reward
